Q: Dan has $ 3. For a total of $ 4 he bought 2 candy bar each one costing the same amount of money. How much did each candy bar cost?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Dan initially has $3.
- He spends a total of $4 to buy 2 candy bars.
- Each candy bar costs the same amount.
- We assume Dan borrowed $1 to make up the difference between his initial $3 and the $4 he spent.
- We are tasked with finding the cost of one candy bar.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Divide the total amount spent ($4) by the number of candy bars (2). This method directly calculates the cost of one candy bar.

**Method 2:**  
Set up an equation to represent the situation. Let the cost of one candy bar be \( x \). Since Dan bought 2 candy bars, the total cost is \( 2x = 4 \). Solve for \( x \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Division**
1. Total amount spent = $4.
2. Number of candy bars = 2.
3. Cost of one candy bar = Total amount spent ÷ Number of candy bars:
   \[
   \text{Cost of one candy bar} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
   \]
4. Double-check: If each candy bar costs $2, then \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \), which matches the total amount spent. This confirms the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method assumes the total amount spent is evenly divisible by the number of candy bars, which is true in this case.

---

#### **Method 2: Algebraic Equation**
1. Let the cost of one candy bar be \( x \).
2. Since Dan bought 2 candy bars, the total cost is \( 2x \). We know the total cost is $4, so:
   \[
   2x = 4
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \) by dividing both sides of the equation by 2:
   \[
   x = \frac{4}{2} = 2
   \]
4. Double-check: If \( x = 2 \), then \( 2x = 2 \times 2 = 4 \), which matches the total amount spent. This confirms the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method requires setting up the equation correctly, but it is straightforward in this case.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity:** Method 1 is simpler and more intuitive because it directly divides the total cost by the number of candy bars.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, but Method 2 introduces an extra step (setting up and solving the equation), which could lead to errors in more complex problems.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is faster because it skips the need for algebraic setup.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  \text{Cost of one candy bar} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If each candy bar costs $2, then the total cost for 2 candy bars is \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \), which matches the given total amount spent.
- The solution is reasonable and consistent with the problem's conditions.

---

The answer is **2**.

  \]

Q: Baker made 170 cakes. He sold 78 of them and bought 31 new cakes. How many more cakes did baker sell than those he bought?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Baker made 170 cakes initially.  
- He sold 78 cakes.  
- He bought 31 new cakes.  
- The question asks how many more cakes Baker sold than those he bought.  
- We assume no cakes were lost, discarded, or otherwise unaccounted for.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Direct subtraction of the number of cakes sold and the number of cakes bought: \( 78 - 31 \).  
**Method 2:** Compare the two quantities by calculating the difference step-by-step: Start with the number sold (78), subtract the number bought (31), and verify the difference.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the number of cakes sold: \( 78 \).  
2. Subtract the number of cakes bought: \( 78 - 31 \).  
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   - \( 78 - 31 = 47 \).  
4. Double-check the subtraction:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 78 - 30 = 48 \), then subtract 1 more: \( 48 - 1 = 47 \).  

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step comparison**
1. Start with the number of cakes sold: \( 78 \).  
2. Subtract the number of cakes bought: \( 31 \).  
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   - \( 78 - 31 = 47 \).  
4. Verify the result by reasoning:  
   - Baker sold significantly more cakes than he bought, and the difference is \( 47 \).  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single subtraction operation.  
- **Method 2** is slightly redundant, as it essentially repeats the same subtraction process but adds extra reasoning steps.  
- Both methods are reliable, but **Method 1** is clearer and faster for this problem.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution: \( 78 - 31 = 47 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the subtraction: \( 78 - 31 = 47 \).  
- Reasonableness: Baker sold significantly more cakes than he bought, and the difference of \( 47 \) makes sense given the numbers.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **47**.

Q: There are many different books in the ' crazy silly school ' series. If you have read 13 of the books and are yet to read 8 books How many books are there in the ' crazy silly school ' series?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- You have read 13 books in the "Crazy Silly School" series.
- You have 8 books left to read.
- We assume there are no additional books in the series beyond the ones you have read and the ones you are yet to read.
- The total number of books in the series is the sum of the books you have read and the books you are yet to read.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct addition. Add the number of books you have read (13) to the number of books you are yet to read (8) to find the total number of books in the series.

**Method 2:** Use subtraction as a verification step. Assume the total number of books is \( x \). Subtract the number of books you have read (13) from \( x \), and check if the remainder equals the number of books you are yet to read (8). Solve for \( x \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Addition**
1. Start with the number of books you have read: 13.
2. Add the number of books you are yet to read: \( 13 + 8 \).
3. Perform the calculation: \( 13 + 8 = 21 \).
4. Double-check the calculation: Adding 13 and 8 step by step:
   - Start with 13.
   - Add 8: \( 13 + 8 = 21 \).
5. Result: The total number of books in the series is 21.

#### **Method 2: Subtraction Verification**
1. Assume the total number of books in the series is \( x \).
2. Subtract the number of books you have read (13) from \( x \): \( x - 13 \).
3. Set the remainder equal to the number of books you are yet to read (8): \( x - 13 = 8 \).
4. Solve for \( x \):
   - Add 13 to both sides: \( x = 8 + 13 \).
   - Perform the calculation: \( x = 21 \).
5. Double-check the calculation:
   - Subtract 13 from 21: \( 21 - 13 = 8 \), which matches the number of books you are yet to read.
6. Result: The total number of books in the series is 21.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient because it directly adds the two known quantities (books read and books yet to read) to find the total. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is useful as a verification method but is less efficient because it introduces an extra variable (\( x \)) and requires solving an equation. While it is reliable, it is more complex than necessary for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution:
  - Add the number of books you have read (13) to the number of books you are yet to read (8): \( 13 + 8 = 21 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by breaking down the addition: \( 13 + 8 = 21 \).
- Verify using subtraction: If the total is 21 and you have read 13, then \( 21 - 13 = 8 \), which matches the number of books you are yet to read.
- The result is reasonable and consistent with the problem's information.

---

The answer is **21**.

Q: Matthew had 23 crackers. He has 11 crackers left after he gave equal numbers of crackers to his 2 friends. How many crackers did each friend eat?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Matthew started with **23 crackers**.
- He has **11 crackers left** after giving some away.
- He gave **equal numbers of crackers to 2 friends**.
- We assume no crackers were lost, broken, or eaten by Matthew himself.
- The goal is to determine how many crackers **each friend received**.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction and division approach**  
1. Subtract the number of crackers Matthew has left (11) from the total he started with (23) to find the total number of crackers he gave away.  
2. Divide the total number of crackers given away by 2 (since he gave equal amounts to 2 friends) to find how many crackers each friend received.

**Method 2: Trial-and-error approach**  
1. Assume a number of crackers each friend received, multiply it by 2, and subtract the result from the total crackers Matthew started with (23).  
2. Check if the remainder matches the number of crackers Matthew has left (11).  
3. Adjust the guess until the condition is satisfied.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction and division approach**
1. Subtract the number of crackers Matthew has left from the total he started with:  
   \[
   23 - 11 = 12
   \]  
   This means Matthew gave away **12 crackers** in total.

2. Divide the total crackers given away by 2 to find how many each friend received:  
   \[
   12 \div 2 = 6
   \]  
   Each friend received **6 crackers**.

3. Double-check: If each friend received 6 crackers, the total given away is:  
   \[
   6 \times 2 = 12
   \]  
   Subtracting this from the starting total:  
   \[
   23 - 12 = 11
   \]  
   This matches the number of crackers Matthew has left, so the calculation is correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-error approach**
1. Assume each friend received **5 crackers**. Multiply by 2 to find the total given away:  
   \[
   5 \times 2 = 10
   \]  
   Subtract this from the starting total:  
   \[
   23 - 10 = 13
   \]  
   This does not match the number of crackers Matthew has left (11), so the guess is incorrect.

2. Assume each friend received **6 crackers**. Multiply by 2 to find the total given away:  
   \[
   6 \times 2 = 12
   \]  
   Subtract this from the starting total:  
   \[
   23 - 12 = 11
   \]  
   This matches the number of crackers Matthew has left, so the guess is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it uses straightforward arithmetic (subtraction and division) to arrive at the solution. It minimizes the risk of error and does not require guessing.  
- **Method 2** involves trial-and-error, which can be time-consuming and less reliable, especially if the numbers are larger or more complex. However, it can be useful for verifying results or when the problem is less structured.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is better.**  
1. Subtract the number of crackers Matthew has left from the total he started with:  
   \[
   23 - 11 = 12
   \]  
   Matthew gave away **12 crackers** in total.

2. Divide the total crackers given away by 2 to find how many each friend received:  
   \[
   12 \div 2 = 6
   \]  
   Each friend received **6 crackers**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Total crackers given away:  
  \[
  6 \times 2 = 12
  \]  
- Subtracting this from the starting total:  
  \[
  23 - 12 = 11
  \]  
  This matches the number of crackers Matthew has left, confirming the solution is correct.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **6**.

Q: Dan has $ 5. He bought a chocolate for $ 3 and a candy bar for $ 7. How much money did he spend to buy candy bar than he did to buy chocolate?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Dan has $5 initially.  
- He bought a chocolate for $3.  
- He bought a candy bar for $7.  
- The question asks how much more money Dan spent on the candy bar compared to the chocolate.  
- We assume the prices are correct, and Dan has enough money to make the purchases (even though his initial $5 is insufficient, this discrepancy is irrelevant to the comparison).  

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Subtract the cost of the chocolate from the cost of the candy bar: $7 - $3. This directly calculates the difference in spending.  
**Method 2:** Calculate the ratio of the candy bar's cost to the chocolate's cost, then convert the ratio into a difference by subtracting the chocolate's cost from the candy bar's cost.  

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Subtract the cost of the chocolate from the cost of the candy bar:  
  $7 - $3 = $4.  
- This calculation directly gives the difference in spending.  
- Pitfall: None, as subtraction is straightforward and directly answers the question.  

**Method 2:**  
- First, calculate the ratio of the candy bar's cost to the chocolate's cost:  
  $7 ÷ $3 ≈ 2.33.  
- Interpret the ratio: Dan spent approximately 2.33 times as much on the candy bar as on the chocolate.  
- To find the difference, subtract the chocolate's cost from the candy bar's cost:  
  $7 - $3 = $4.  
- Pitfall: The ratio calculation is unnecessary for this problem and does not directly answer the question. It introduces extra steps that could confuse the solution.  

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is better because it directly calculates the difference in spending without introducing unnecessary steps or complexity.  
- **Method 2** involves calculating a ratio, which is irrelevant to the question and adds unnecessary complexity.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Using **Method 1**, subtract the cost of the chocolate from the cost of the candy bar:  
  $7 - $3 = $4.  

---

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recheck the subtraction: $7 - $3 = $4.  
- The result is reasonable because the candy bar costs $4 more than the chocolate.  

---

The answer is **4**.

Q: Jerry had 9 action figures on a shelf in his room. Later he added 7 more action figures to the shelf. If he also has 10 books on the shelf How many more action figures than books were on his shelf?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jerry initially had **9 action figures** on the shelf.
- He added **7 more action figures** to the shelf.
- He also has **10 books** on the shelf.
- The goal is to determine how many **more action figures than books** are on the shelf.
- Assumptions:
  - All action figures and books are on the same shelf.
  - No action figures or books are removed or lost during the process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- First, calculate the total number of action figures by adding the initial number (9) to the additional number (7).  
- Then subtract the number of books (10) from the total number of action figures to find the difference.

**Method 2:**  
- Combine the steps into a single equation:  
  \[
  \text{Difference} = (9 + 7) - 10
  \]  
- Solve the equation directly to find the difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step calculation**
1. **Calculate the total number of action figures:**  
   \[
   9 + 7 = 16
   \]  
   So, there are **16 action figures** on the shelf.

2. **Find the difference between action figures and books:**  
   \[
   16 - 10 = 6
   \]  
   The difference is **6**.

3. **Check for potential pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure all numbers are added and subtracted correctly.
   - Verify that the problem asks for the difference between action figures and books, not the total count of items.

#### **Method 2: Single equation**
1. **Set up the equation:**  
   \[
   (9 + 7) - 10
   \]

2. **Simplify the parentheses first:**  
   \[
   9 + 7 = 16
   \]

3. **Subtract the number of books:**  
   \[
   16 - 10 = 6
   \]  
   The difference is **6**.

4. **Check for potential pitfalls:**  
   - Ensure the equation is set up correctly.
   - Confirm that the subtraction is performed in the correct order (action figures minus books).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller, manageable steps. This makes it easier to follow and reduces the chance of errors.  
- **Method 2** is more concise and efficient, but it requires careful attention to ensure the equation is set up correctly. It may be less intuitive for some people.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability, especially for beginners or when explaining the solution.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Calculate the total number of action figures:  
   \[
   9 + 7 = 16
   \]

2. Subtract the number of books:  
   \[
   16 - 10 = 6
   \]

3. Verify the result:  
   - There are 16 action figures and 10 books.  
   - The difference is indeed **6**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  \[
  (9 + 7) - 10 = 16 - 10 = 6
  \]  
  The result matches.

- Verify the logic:  
  - The problem asks for the difference between action figures and books.  
  - The calculations align with the given information.

---

The answer is **6**.

Q: Edward spent $ 16 to buy 92 books each book costing him the same amount of money. Now he has $ 6. How much did Edward have before he spent his money?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Edward spent $16 to buy 92 books, with each book costing the same amount.
- After spending $16, Edward has $6 left.
- We are tasked with finding how much money Edward had before he spent the $16.
- Assumptions:
  - The cost of each book is evenly distributed (no fractional books or discounts).
  - Edward's total money before spending is the sum of the $16 he spent and the $6 he has left.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct addition of spent and remaining money.**
- Edward's total money before spending is the sum of the $16 he spent and the $6 he has left.
- Total money = Spent money + Remaining money.

**Method 2: Reverse the spending process.**
- Start with Edward's remaining $6 and "add back" the $16 he spent to determine his original total.
- This method essentially reverses the subtraction operation.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of spent and remaining money**
1. Edward spent $16 and has $6 left.
2. Add the two amounts:  
   \[
   \text{Total money} = 16 + 6 = 22
   \]
3. Double-check: If Edward started with $22 and spent $16, he would have $6 left, which matches the problem statement.

#### **Method 2: Reverse the spending process**
1. Edward has $6 left after spending $16.
2. Reverse the spending by adding the $16 back to the $6:  
   \[
   \text{Total money} = 6 + 16 = 22
   \]
3. Double-check: If Edward started with $22 and spent $16, he would have $6 left, which matches the problem statement.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- Both methods are straightforward and yield the same result.
- **Method 1** is slightly more intuitive because it directly adds the spent and remaining money, which aligns with how we naturally think about combining amounts.
- **Method 2** is essentially the same process but framed as reversing the spending, which may be less intuitive for some.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.
- Calculation:  
  \[
  \text{Total money} = 16 + 6 = 22
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- If Edward started with $22 and spent $16, he would have $6 left. This matches the problem statement, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **22**.

Q: Haley grew 14 trees in her backyard. After a typhoon 9 died. How many more trees died in the typhoon than those that survived?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Haley initially grew **14 trees** in her backyard.
- A typhoon caused **9 trees to die**.
- We are tasked with finding how many **more trees died** in the typhoon than those that survived.
- Assumptions:
  - No trees were added or removed during the typhoon other than the 9 that died.
  - The remaining trees are the ones that survived.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction approach**
1. Calculate the number of trees that survived by subtracting the number of trees that died from the total: \( 14 - 9 \).
2. Compare the number of trees that died (9) to the number that survived (calculated in step 1) by subtracting the survivors from the dead: \( 9 - \text{(survivors)} \).

**Method 2: Ratio and difference approach**
1. Recognize that the total number of trees is split into two groups: those that died and those that survived.
2. Use the difference between the two groups directly: \( \text{(died)} - \text{(survived)} \), where the number of survivors is \( 14 - 9 \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction approach**
1. Calculate the number of trees that survived:
   \[
   \text{Survived} = 14 - 9 = 5
   \]
2. Compare the number of trees that died to the number that survived:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 9 - 5 = 4
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - Total trees = \( \text{Died} + \text{Survived} = 9 + 5 = 14 \), which matches the initial total.
   - The difference \( 9 - 5 = 4 \) is consistent.

#### **Method 2: Ratio and difference approach**
1. Calculate the number of survivors:
   \[
   \text{Survived} = 14 - 9 = 5
   \]
2. Use the difference formula directly:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = \text{(died)} - \text{(survived)} = 9 - 5 = 4
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - Survivors = \( 14 - 9 = 5 \), which is correct.
   - Difference = \( 9 - 5 = 4 \), which matches the result from Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, as it directly calculates the survivors and compares them to the number of trees that died.
- **Method 2** is slightly more abstract but equally valid, as it focuses on the difference between the two groups without explicitly emphasizing the total.
- Both methods are reliable and efficient, but **Method 1** is more intuitive for most people because it follows a natural step-by-step process.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.
1. Calculate the number of survivors:
   \[
   \text{Survived} = 14 - 9 = 5
   \]
2. Compare the number of trees that died to the number that survived:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 9 - 5 = 4
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Total trees = \( \text{Died} + \text{Survived} = 9 + 5 = 14 \), which matches the initial total.
- The difference \( 9 - 5 = 4 \) is consistent with the problem's logic.

---

The answer is **4**.

Q: Because of the decision Sofia asked the students to suggest specific types of food. If 218 students suggested adding mashed potatoes as well as bacon while 351 suggested adding only bacon to the menu How many students suggested adding bacon?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- **Given Information:**
  - 218 students suggested adding both mashed potatoes and bacon to the menu.
  - 351 students suggested adding only bacon to the menu.
- **Assumptions:**
  - "Only bacon" means these students did not suggest mashed potatoes.
  - The total number of students who suggested adding bacon includes both groups: those who suggested bacon alone and those who suggested bacon along with mashed potatoes.
  - There is no overlap or ambiguity in the categories.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
- **Method 1:** Add the two groups directly. Since the total number of students who suggested bacon includes both those who suggested bacon alone and those who suggested bacon along with mashed potatoes, the sum of these two groups gives the total.
- **Method 2:** Use a set-based approach. Treat the problem as a union of two disjoint sets: one set for students who suggested "only bacon" and another set for students who suggested "mashed potatoes and bacon." The union of these sets represents the total number of students who suggested bacon.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Addition**
1. Start with the number of students who suggested mashed potatoes and bacon: **218**.
2. Add the number of students who suggested only bacon: **351**.
3. Perform the addition:  
   \[
   218 + 351 = 569
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 218 + 300 = 518 \), then \( 518 + 51 = 569 \).
   - Verify using subtraction: \( 569 - 218 = 351 \), confirming the result.

#### **Method 2: Set-Based Approach**
1. Define two sets:
   - Set \( A \): Students who suggested mashed potatoes and bacon (\( |A| = 218 \)).
   - Set \( B \): Students who suggested only bacon (\( |B| = 351 \)).
2. The total number of students who suggested bacon is the union of these two disjoint sets:  
   \[
   |A \cup B| = |A| + |B|
   \]
3. Substitute the values:  
   \[
   |A \cup B| = 218 + 351 = 569
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation as in Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, requiring only basic addition. It is efficient and avoids unnecessary abstraction.
- **Method 2** introduces a set-based framework, which is useful for more complex problems involving overlapping groups or intersections. However, in this case, the sets are disjoint, making the set-based approach equivalent to direct addition.
- **Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**, add the two groups directly:
  \[
  218 + 351 = 569
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the addition step-by-step: \( 218 + 300 = 518 \), then \( 518 + 51 = 569 \).
- Confirm the logic: The total number of students who suggested bacon includes both groups, and the sum is reasonable given the problem context.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **569**.

Q: In Haley's class some boys love to play marbles. Haley gives 2 marbles to each boy. If she had 28 marbles How many boys did she give the marbles to?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Haley has 28 marbles in total.
- She gives 2 marbles to each boy.
- We are tasked with finding how many boys received marbles.
- Assumptions:
  - All marbles are distributed evenly, with no leftovers.
  - Each boy receives exactly 2 marbles.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- Since each boy receives 2 marbles, divide the total number of marbles (28) by the number of marbles per boy (2). This will give the total number of boys.

**Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**  
- Start with 28 marbles and repeatedly subtract 2 marbles (the amount given to each boy) until no marbles are left. Count how many subtractions are performed to determine the number of boys.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Total marbles = 28.
2. Marbles per boy = 2.
3. Divide: \( 28 \div 2 = 14 \).
4. Interpretation: 14 boys received marbles.
5. Check: Multiply the number of boys (14) by the marbles each boy received (2): \( 14 \times 2 = 28 \). This matches the total number of marbles, so the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** Ensure the division is exact (no remainder). Since 28 is divisible by 2, this method works perfectly.

---

#### **Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with 28 marbles.
2. Subtract 2 marbles for each boy and count the subtractions:
   - After 1st subtraction: \( 28 - 2 = 26 \) (1 boy).
   - After 2nd subtraction: \( 26 - 2 = 24 \) (2 boys).
   - After 3rd subtraction: \( 24 - 2 = 22 \) (3 boys).
   - After 4th subtraction: \( 22 - 2 = 20 \) (4 boys).
   - After 5th subtraction: \( 20 - 2 = 18 \) (5 boys).
   - After 6th subtraction: \( 18 - 2 = 16 \) (6 boys).
   - After 7th subtraction: \( 16 - 2 = 14 \) (7 boys).
   - After 8th subtraction: \( 14 - 2 = 12 \) (8 boys).
   - After 9th subtraction: \( 12 - 2 = 10 \) (9 boys).
   - After 10th subtraction: \( 10 - 2 = 8 \) (10 boys).
   - After 11th subtraction: \( 8 - 2 = 6 \) (11 boys).
   - After 12th subtraction: \( 6 - 2 = 4 \) (12 boys).
   - After 13th subtraction: \( 4 - 2 = 2 \) (13 boys).
   - After 14th subtraction: \( 2 - 2 = 0 \) (14 boys).
3. Count the total subtractions: 14.
4. Check: Multiply the number of boys (14) by the marbles each boy received (2): \( 14 \times 2 = 28 \). This matches the total number of marbles, so the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method is time-consuming and prone to errors if the subtraction or counting is not done carefully.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is more efficient, as it involves a single division operation and a quick verification step. It is less prone to errors and is the most straightforward way to solve the problem.
- **Method 2 (Repeated Subtraction Approach)** is less efficient, as it requires multiple steps and careful tracking of subtractions. While it works, it is more tedious and error-prone.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is the better method.
1. Total marbles = 28.
2. Marbles per boy = 2.
3. Divide: \( 28 \div 2 = 14 \).
4. Verify: \( 14 \times 2 = 28 \). The calculation is correct.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Both methods agree, and the result is reasonable given the problem's context.

Q: 6 packs of dvds can be bought with 120 dollars. How much does each pack cost?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Joan can buy **6 packs of DVDs** for **$120**.
- We are tasked with finding the cost of **1 pack of DVDs**.
- Assumptions:
  - The cost of each pack is the same.
  - There are no additional taxes, discounts, or fees.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- Divide the total cost ($120) by the number of packs (6) to find the cost of one pack. This is the most direct approach.

**Method 2: Unit Reduction Approach**  
- First, calculate the cost of 2 packs by dividing $120 by 3 (since 6 packs can be grouped into 3 sets of 2). Then divide the cost of 2 packs by 2 to find the cost of 1 pack. This method breaks the problem into smaller steps.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Start with the total cost: $120.
2. Divide by the number of packs:  
   \[
   120 \div 6 = 20
   \]
3. The cost of one pack is $20.

**Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
- Multiplying the result back: \( 20 \times 6 = 120 \), which matches the total cost.  
- This method is straightforward and avoids unnecessary steps.

---

#### **Method 2: Unit Reduction Approach**
1. Group the 6 packs into 3 sets of 2 packs each. Divide the total cost by 3 to find the cost of 2 packs:  
   \[
   120 \div 3 = 40
   \]
   So, 2 packs cost $40.
2. Divide the cost of 2 packs by 2 to find the cost of 1 pack:  
   \[
   40 \div 2 = 20
   \]
3. The cost of one pack is $20.

**Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
- Multiplying the result back: \( 20 \times 6 = 120 \), which matches the total cost.  
- This method works but introduces an extra step, which could lead to errors in more complex problems.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct, efficient, and less prone to errors because it involves a single division step.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily breaks the problem into smaller steps, which increases the chance of mistakes in more complex scenarios.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.  
- Solution:  
  \[
  120 \div 6 = 20
  \]
- The cost of one pack is $20.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the cost of one pack by the number of packs:  
  \[
  20 \times 6 = 120
  \]
- The result matches the total cost, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **20**.

Q: Mary is baking a cake. The recipe calls for 9 cups of flour and 11 cups of sugar. She already put in 4 cups of flour. How many more cups of sugar than cups of flour does she need to add now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mary is baking a cake.
- The recipe calls for **9 cups of flour** and **11 cups of sugar**.
- Mary has already added **4 cups of flour**.
- We are tasked with finding how many **more cups of sugar than cups of flour** Mary needs to add.
- Assumptions:
  - The recipe quantities are fixed and must be followed exactly.
  - No flour or sugar has been spilled or wasted.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct subtraction and comparison.**
1. Calculate how many more cups of flour Mary needs to add: \( 9 - 4 \).
2. Compare this to the total cups of sugar required: \( 11 \).
3. Subtract the remaining cups of flour from the total cups of sugar to find the difference.

**Method 2: Simultaneous tracking of flour and sugar.**
1. Track the remaining cups of flour Mary needs to add: \( 9 - 4 \).
2. Track the total cups of sugar Mary needs to add: \( 11 \).
3. Subtract the remaining cups of flour from the remaining cups of sugar to find the difference.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction and comparison.**
1. Calculate how many cups of flour Mary still needs to add:
   \[
   9 - 4 = 5 \, \text{cups of flour}.
   \]
2. Compare this to the total cups of sugar required:
   \[
   11 \, \text{cups of sugar}.
   \]
3. Subtract the remaining cups of flour from the total cups of sugar:
   \[
   11 - 5 = 6 \, \text{more cups of sugar than flour}.
   \]

#### **Method 2: Simultaneous tracking of flour and sugar.**
1. Track the remaining cups of flour Mary needs to add:
   \[
   9 - 4 = 5 \, \text{cups of flour}.
   \]
2. Track the total cups of sugar Mary needs to add:
   \[
   11 \, \text{cups of sugar}.
   \]
3. Subtract the remaining cups of flour from the remaining cups of sugar:
   \[
   11 - 5 = 6 \, \text{more cups of sugar than flour}.
   \]

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Clarity:** Both methods are clear, but Method 1 is slightly more intuitive because it directly compares the remaining flour and sugar quantities.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, as they involve straightforward arithmetic.
- **Efficiency:** Both methods involve the same number of steps, so they are equally efficient.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

- Since both methods yield the same result, either can be used. For simplicity, we will use **Method 1**.

1. Calculate how many cups of flour Mary still needs to add:
   \[
   9 - 4 = 5 \, \text{cups of flour}.
   \]
2. Compare this to the total cups of sugar required:
   \[
   11 \, \text{cups of sugar}.
   \]
3. Subtract the remaining cups of flour from the total cups of sugar:
   \[
   11 - 5 = 6 \, \text{more cups of sugar than flour}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify the calculations:
  - Remaining flour: \( 9 - 4 = 5 \).
  - Total sugar: \( 11 \).

Q: Robin cut off 13 inches of his hair. If his hair was 14 inches long initially How long is his hair now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Robin's hair was initially **14 inches long**.
- He cut off **13 inches** of his hair.
- We assume no additional hair was cut or lost, and the cutting process was precise.
- The goal is to determine how long Robin's hair is now.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtraction  
- Subtract the length of hair cut off (13 inches) from the initial length (14 inches). This directly gives the remaining length.

**Method 2:** Reverse addition  
- Start with the remaining length of hair (unknown) and add the length cut off (13 inches). The result should equal the initial length (14 inches). Solve for the unknown.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Start with the initial length of hair: **14 inches**.
2. Subtract the length cut off:  
   \[
   14 - 13 = 1
   \]
3. The remaining length of hair is **1 inch**.

**Check:** Add the remaining length (1 inch) to the length cut off (13 inches):  
\[
1 + 13 = 14
\]  
This matches the initial length, confirming the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** Subtraction errors could occur if not careful, but this is a straightforward calculation.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse Addition**
1. Let the remaining length of hair be \( x \).
2. Add the length cut off (13 inches) to the remaining length (\( x \)):  
   \[
   x + 13 = 14
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 14 - 13 = 1
   \]
4. The remaining length of hair is **1 inch**.

**Check:** Substitute \( x = 1 \) back into the equation:  
\[
1 + 13 = 14
\]  
This matches the initial length, confirming the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method introduces an extra step (solving for \( x \)), which could lead to errors if the equation is set up incorrectly.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction)** is more direct, efficient, and intuitive for this problem. It involves fewer steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2 (Reverse Addition)** is more complex because it introduces an equation and requires solving for \( x \). While it works, it is less efficient for such a simple problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice.  
- Subtract the length cut off from the initial length:  
  \[
  14 - 13 = 1
  \]  
- The remaining length of Robin's hair is **1 inch**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the remaining length (1 inch) to the length cut off (13 inches):  
  \[
  1 + 13 = 14
  \]  
- This matches the initial length, confirming the solution is correct and reasonable.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **1 inch**.

Q: The Razorback t-shirt shop makes $ 87 dollars off each t-shirt sold. During the Arkansas game and the Texas tech game they sold a total of 95 t-shirts. If they sold 47 t-shirts during the Arkansas game How much money did they make from selling the t-shirts?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The Razorback t-shirt shop makes $87 per t-shirt sold.
- A total of 95 t-shirts were sold during two games: the Arkansas game and the Texas Tech game.
- During the Arkansas game, 47 t-shirts were sold.
- We assume all t-shirts sold during both games were sold at the same price of $87 each.
- We need to calculate the total revenue from selling all 95 t-shirts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation of total revenue.**
1. Multiply the total number of t-shirts sold (95) by the price per t-shirt ($87).
2. This gives the total revenue from all t-shirts sold.

**Method 2: Break the problem into two parts.**
1. Calculate the revenue from the Arkansas game by multiplying the number of t-shirts sold during that game (47) by the price per t-shirt ($87).
2. Subtract the number of t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game (47) from the total number of t-shirts sold (95) to find the number sold during the Texas Tech game.
3. Calculate the revenue from the Texas Tech game by multiplying the remaining number of t-shirts sold by the price per t-shirt.
4. Add the revenue from both games to find the total revenue.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of total revenue.**
1. Total t-shirts sold = 95.
2. Price per t-shirt = $87.
3. Total revenue = 95 × 87.
   - Break down the multiplication for clarity:
     - \( 95 × 87 = (95 × 80) + (95 × 7) \)
     - \( 95 × 80 = 7600 \)
     - \( 95 × 7 = 665 \)
     - \( 7600 + 665 = 8265 \)
4. Total revenue = $8265.

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into two parts.**
1. Revenue from the Arkansas game:
   - T-shirts sold = 47.
   - Price per t-shirt = $87.
   - Revenue = 47 × 87.
     - Break down the multiplication:
       - \( 47 × 87 = (47 × 80) + (47 × 7) \)
       - \( 47 × 80 = 3760 \)
       - \( 47 × 7 = 329 \)
       - \( 3760 + 329 = 4089 \)
     - Revenue from the Arkansas game = $4089.
2. T-shirts sold during the Texas Tech game:
   - Total t-shirts sold = 95.
   - T-shirts sold during the Arkansas game = 47.
   - T-shirts sold during the Texas Tech game = 95 - 47 = 48.
3. Revenue from the Texas Tech game:
   - T-shirts sold = 48.
   - Price per t-shirt = $87.
   - Revenue = 48 × 87.
     - Break down the multiplication:
       - \( 48 × 87 = (48 × 80) + (48 × 7) \)
       - \( 48 × 80 = 3840 \)
       - \( 48 × 7 = 336 \)
       - \( 3840 + 336 = 4176 \)
     - Revenue from the Texas Tech game = $4176.
4. Total revenue:
   - Revenue from Arkansas game = $4089.
   - Revenue from Texas Tech game = $4176.
   - Total revenue = $4089 + $4176 = $8265.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it calculates the total revenue in one step without breaking it into parts. It is less prone to errors since it involves fewer calculations.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller, logical steps. While it provides a clearer understanding of the revenue from each game, it involves more calculations, which increases the chance of arithmetic errors.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Total t-shirts sold = 95.
2. Price per t-shirt = $87.
3. Total revenue = 95 × 87.
   - \( 95 × 87 = (95 × 80) + (95 × 7) \)
   - \( 95 × 80 = 7600 \)
   - \( 95 × 7 = 665 \)
   - \( 7600 + 665 = 8265 \)
4. Total revenue = $8265.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Using Method 2, we calculated the revenue from each game separately:
  - Arkansas game: $4089.
  - Texas Tech game: $4176.
  - Total revenue = $4089 + $4176 = $8265.
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the accuracy of the solution.

---

The answer is **8265**.

Q: An industrial machine worked for 5 minutes. It can make 4 shirts a minute. How many shirts did machine make?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The machine worked for **5 minutes**.
- The machine produces **4 shirts per minute**.
- We assume the machine worked continuously and at a constant rate of 4 shirts per minute.
- No shirts were lost, damaged, or discarded during production.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct multiplication**  
- Multiply the number of minutes the machine worked by the number of shirts it produces per minute: \( 5 \times 4 \).

**Method 2: Incremental addition**  
- Add the number of shirts produced per minute (4) repeatedly for each of the 5 minutes: \( 4 + 4 + 4 + 4 + 4 \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication**
1. Multiply the number of minutes (5) by the production rate (4 shirts per minute):  
   \[
   5 \times 4 = 20
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 5 \times 4 = (5 \times 2) \times 2 = 10 \times 2 = 20 \).
3. Potential pitfalls:  
   - Misinterpreting the rate (e.g., thinking the machine produces 4 shirts in 5 minutes instead of per minute). However, this is avoided by carefully reading the problem.

#### **Method 2: Incremental addition**
1. Add the production rate (4 shirts) repeatedly for each of the 5 minutes:  
   \[
   4 + 4 + 4 + 4 + 4 = 20
   \]
2. Break it into smaller steps:  
   - First two minutes: \( 4 + 4 = 8 \)  
   - Next two minutes: \( 4 + 4 = 8 \)  
   - Final minute: \( 8 + 4 = 20 \).
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Count the total number of terms (5) and verify each term is 4.  
   - Sum them again: \( 4 + 4 = 8 \), \( 8 + 4 = 12 \), \( 12 + 4 = 16 \), \( 16 + 4 = 20 \).
4. Potential pitfalls:  
   - Losing track of the number of additions or miscounting the terms. This can be avoided by carefully organizing the additions.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct multiplication)** is more efficient and straightforward, especially for larger numbers. It requires fewer steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2 (Incremental addition)** is more intuitive for small numbers but becomes cumbersome and error-prone for larger values.
- For this problem, **Method 1** is clearly better due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Direct multiplication)**:  
\[
5 \times 4 = 20
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the logic: If the machine produces 4 shirts per minute, then in 5 minutes it should produce \( 4 + 4 + 4 + 4 + 4 = 20 \), which matches the result.
- Verify the units: The result (20) is in "shirts," which is consistent with the problem's context.
- The result is reasonable and error-free.

The answer is **20**.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are **12 sets of tables**, and **each set has 14 chairs**.
- There are also **6 extra chairs** in addition to the chairs from the table sets.
- We assume all chairs are functional and available for use.
- The goal is to calculate the **total number of chairs** available for the guests.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct multiplication and addition.**
- Multiply the number of sets of tables (12) by the number of chairs per set (14) to find the total chairs from the sets.
- Add the 6 extra chairs to this total.

**Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps for clarity.**
- First, calculate the total chairs for 10 sets of tables (a simpler number to work with).
- Then calculate the chairs for the remaining 2 sets of tables.
- Add these two results together, then add the 6 extra chairs.

---

1. Multiply the number of sets of tables by the number of chairs per set:
   \[
   12 \times 14 = 168
   \]
   - This gives 168 chairs from the table sets.
2. Add the 6 extra chairs:
   \[
   168 + 6 = 174
   \]
   - Total chairs = 174.

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Multiplication and addition are straightforward here, so there is minimal risk of error if calculations are done carefully.

---

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps**
1. Calculate the chairs for 10 sets of tables:
   \[
   10 \times 14 = 140
   \]
2. Calculate the chairs for the remaining 2 sets of tables:
   \[
   2 \times 14 = 28
   \]
3. Add the results from the two calculations:
   \[
   140 + 28 = 168
   \]
4. Add the 6 extra chairs:
   \[
   168 + 6 = 174
   \]

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Breaking the problem into smaller steps reduces the chance of error, but it introduces more intermediate calculations, which could lead to mistakes if not carefully checked.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves fewer steps and calculations. It is ideal for straightforward problems like this.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and may be helpful for mental math or when working with larger or more complex numbers. However, it is less efficient for this specific problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Multiply the number of sets of tables by the number of chairs per set:
   \[
   12 \times 14 = 168
   \]
2. Add the 6 extra chairs:
   \[
   168 + 6 = 174
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:
  1. Chairs for 10 sets: \(10 \times 14 = 140\).
  2. Chairs for 2 sets: \(2 \times 14 = 28\).
  3. Total from sets: \(140 + 28 = 168\).
  4. Add the 6 extra chairs: \(168 + 6 = 174\).
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Robin has **43 packages of gum**, with **23 pieces in each package**.
- Robin also has an additional **8 extra pieces of gum**.
- We assume all packages are full and contain exactly 23 pieces.
- The total number of gum pieces is the sum of the gum in the packages and the extra pieces.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct multiplication and addition**
- Multiply the number of packages (43) by the number of pieces per package (23) to find the total gum from the packages.
- Add the 8 extra pieces to this total.

**Method 2: Break the problem into smaller, easier calculations**
- Break the multiplication into parts: split 23 into 20 and 3, then multiply each part by 43 separately.
- Add the results of the partial multiplications and then add the 8 extra pieces.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication and addition**
1. Multiply the number of packages by the number of pieces per package:
   \[
   43 \times 23
   \]
   Use long multiplication:
   - \( 43 \times 3 = 129 \)
   - \( 43 \times 20 = 860 \)
   - Add the results: \( 129 + 860 = 989 \)

2. Add the 8 extra pieces:
   \[
   989 + 8 = 997
   \]

3. Double-check the multiplication:
   - Recalculate \( 43 \times 23 \) using distributive property:
     \[
     (43 \times 20) + (43 \times 3) = 860 + 129 = 989
     \]
   - Add the 8 extra pieces again: \( 989 + 8 = 997 \).

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into smaller, easier calculations**
1. Break 23 into 20 and 3, then multiply each part by 43:
   - \( 43 \times 20 = 860 \)
   - \( 43 \times 3 = 129 \)

2. Add the results of the partial multiplications:
   \[
   860 + 129 = 989
   \]

3. Add the 8 extra pieces:
   \[
   989 + 8 = 997
   \]

4. Double-check:
   - Recalculate \( 43 \times 20 = 860 \) and \( 43 \times 3 = 129 \).
   - Add: \( 860 + 129 = 989 \), then \( 989 + 8 = 997 \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves fewer steps and uses standard multiplication and addition.
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math or breaking down complex calculations but introduces more intermediate steps, which could increase the chance of error.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and reliability in this case.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Multiply \( 43 \times 23 \):
   - \( 43 \times 3 = 129 \)
   - \( 43 \times 20 = 860 \)
   - Add: \( 129 + 860 = 989 \)

2. Add the 8 extra pieces:
   \[
   989 + 8 = 997
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate \( 43 \times 23 \) using both long multiplication and the distributive property:
  - \( 43 \times 23 = (43 \times 20) + (43 \times 3) = 860 + 129 = 989 \).
- Add the 8 extra pieces again: \( 989 + 8 = 997 \).
- The result is consistent and reasonable.

---

The answer is **997**.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Marco and his dad picked strawberries together, and the total weight of their strawberries is **20 pounds**.
- Marco's dad's strawberries weighed **17 pounds**.
- We are tasked with finding the weight of Marco's strawberries.
- Assumptions:
  - The total weight includes only Marco's and his dad's strawberries.
  - No strawberries were lost, added, or miscounted.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract the weight of Marco's dad's strawberries from the total weight to find Marco's strawberries:  
  \( \text{Marco's weight} = \text{Total weight} - \text{Dad's weight} \).

**Method 2: Trial-and-Error Method**  
- Assume a weight for Marco's strawberries and check if the sum of Marco's and his dad's strawberries equals the total weight. Adjust the assumption until the total matches.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Method**
1. Start with the total weight of strawberries: **20 pounds**.
2. Subtract the weight of Marco's dad's strawberries:  
   \( 20 - 17 = 3 \).
3. Result: Marco's strawberries weigh **3 pounds**.

**Check:**  
- Add Marco's weight (3 pounds) to his dad's weight (17 pounds):  
  \( 3 + 17 = 20 \), which matches the total weight.  
- This confirms the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Misreading the problem or incorrectly subtracting could lead to an error, but subtraction is straightforward here.

---

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-Error Method**
1. Assume Marco's strawberries weigh **3 pounds**.
2. Add Marco's weight (3 pounds) to his dad's weight (17 pounds):  
   \( 3 + 17 = 20 \).
3. The total matches the given total weight, so the assumption is correct.

**Check:**  
- The assumption of 3 pounds for Marco's strawberries satisfies the condition \( 3 + 17 = 20 \).  
- No further adjustments are needed.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- This method is less efficient because it relies on guessing and checking, which could take longer for more complex problems.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)** is more direct, efficient, and reliable. It involves a single subtraction and is less prone to error.  
- **Method 2 (Trial-and-Error Method)** is less efficient and practical, especially for larger or more complex numbers, as it requires multiple guesses and checks.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better approach for this problem.

1. Total weight of strawberries: **20 pounds**.  
2. Subtract the weight of Marco's dad's strawberries:  
   \( 20 - 17 = 3 \).  
3. Marco's strawberries weigh **3 pounds**.

- Add Marco's weight (3 pounds) to his dad's weight (17 pounds):

  - No apples are added or removed during the comparison.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtraction  
- Subtract the number of apples Adam has from the number Jackie has: \( 10 - 9 \).  
- This directly calculates the difference between their apple counts.

**Method 2:** Counting the difference  
- Start with Adam's count (9) and count up to Jackie's count (10).  
- The number of steps required to reach 10 from 9 is the difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Start with Jackie's total: \( 10 \).
2. Subtract Adam's total: \( 10 - 9 = 1 \).
3. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Reverse the operation: \( 1 + 9 = 10 \), which matches Jackie's total.
4. Potential pitfalls:
   - Subtraction is straightforward, but errors could occur if numbers are misread or miscalculated.

#### **Method 2: Counting the difference**
1. Start at Adam's count: \( 9 \).
2. Count up to Jackie's count: \( 9 \to 10 \).
3. Count the steps: \( 1 \) step.
4. Double-check:
   - Verify that starting at 9 and adding 1 step reaches 10.
5. Potential pitfalls:
   - This method is slower and less efficient for larger numbers, but it works well for small differences.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction):**  
  - Clear, direct, and efficient.  
  - Works well for both small and large numbers.  
  - Minimal risk of error if subtraction is performed carefully.  

- **Method 2 (Counting the difference):**  
  - Intuitive for small numbers but becomes cumbersome for larger differences.  
  - More prone to errors if counting steps is mismanaged.  
  - Less efficient than subtraction.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better because it is faster, more reliable, and scalable to larger numbers.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1 (Subtraction):**
  1. Subtract Adam's total from Jackie's total: \( 10 - 9 = 1 \).
  2. Double-check: \( 1 + 9 = 10 \), confirming the result.

Q: Paul got a box of 601 crayons and 406 erasers for his birthday. At the end of the school year he only had 336 crayons left while not having lost a single eraser. How many more erasers than crayons did he have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paul started with **601 crayons** and **406 erasers**.
- At the end of the school year, Paul had **336 crayons** left.
- Paul did not lose any erasers, so he still has **406 erasers**.
- The goal is to determine how many **more erasers than crayons** Paul has left.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the number of crayons left from the number of erasers left:  
\[ \text{More erasers than crayons} = \text{Erasers left} - \text{Crayons left} \]

**Method 2:**  
Calculate the difference between the initial number of crayons and the crayons left to find how many crayons were lost. Then compare the remaining erasers to the remaining crayons to find the difference:  
\[ \text{Crayons lost} = \text{Initial crayons} - \text{Crayons left} \]  
\[ \text{More erasers than crayons} = \text{Erasers left} - \text{Crayons left} \]

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the number of erasers left: **406**.
2. Subtract the number of crayons left: **336**.  
   \[ 406 - 336 = 70 \]
3. The result is **70 more erasers than crayons**.

**Checks and pitfalls:**  
- This method is straightforward and avoids unnecessary steps.  
- The subtraction is simple, and there is no risk of introducing errors.

#### **Method 2: Calculate crayons lost and compare remaining quantities**
1. Calculate how many crayons were lost:  
   \[ \text{Crayons lost} = \text{Initial crayons} - \text{Crayons left} \]  
   \[ 601 - 336 = 265 \]  
   Paul lost **265 crayons**.
2. Compare the remaining erasers to the remaining crayons:  
   \[ \text{More erasers than crayons} = \text{Erasers left} - \text{Crayons left} \]  
   \[ 406 - 336 = 70 \]
3. The result is **70 more erasers than crayons**.

**Checks and pitfalls:**  
- This method introduces an extra step (calculating crayons lost), which is unnecessary for solving the problem.  
- While the result is correct, the additional calculation could lead to errors if not carefully checked.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it immediately calculates the difference between the number of erasers and crayons left.  
- **Method 2** involves an extra step (calculating crayons lost), which is not required to solve the problem. While it provides additional information, it does not contribute to the final answer and increases the risk of errors.  
- **Method 1** is better for clarity, reliability, and efficiency.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Subtract the number of crayons left from the number of erasers left:  
   \[ 406 - 336 = 70 \]
2. The result is **70 more erasers than crayons**.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify subtraction:  
  \[ 406 - 336 = 70 \]  
  The calculation checks out.
- The result is reasonable because Paul did not lose any erasers, and the difference between the remaining quantities aligns with the given data.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **70**.

Q: Marco and his dad went strawberry picking. Together they collected strawberries that weighed 36 pounds. On the way back Marco ' dad lost 8 pounds of strawberries. Marco's strawberries now weighed 12 pounds. How much did his dad's strawberries weigh now?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Marco and his dad together collected 36 pounds of strawberries.
- On the way back, Marco's dad lost 8 pounds of strawberries.
- Marco's strawberries now weigh 12 pounds.
- We assume no strawberries were gained or lost other than the 8 pounds lost by Marco's dad.
- We need to determine how much Marco's dad's strawberries weigh now.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Subtract Marco's strawberries (12 pounds) from the total collected (36 pounds) to find the original weight of Marco's dad's strawberries.
- Subtract the 8 pounds lost by Marco's dad from his original weight to find his current weight.

**Method 2:**  
- Subtract the 8 pounds lost by Marco's dad directly from the total weight of 36 pounds to find the combined weight of Marco's and his dad's strawberries after the loss.
- Subtract Marco's 12 pounds from this new total to find Marco's dad's current weight.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract Marco's strawberries from the total collected:
   \[
   36 - 12 = 24
   \]
   This means Marco's dad originally had 24 pounds of strawberries.

2. Subtract the 8 pounds lost by Marco's dad:
   \[
   24 - 8 = 16
   \]
   This means Marco's dad now has 16 pounds of strawberries.

3. Double-check:
   - Marco's strawberries weigh 12 pounds.
   - Marco's dad originally had 24 pounds, but he lost 8 pounds, leaving him with 16 pounds.
   - Together, they now have \( 12 + 16 = 28 \) pounds, which is 8 pounds less than the original 36 pounds, confirming the calculation.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Subtract the 8 pounds lost by Marco's dad from the total weight:
   \[
   36 - 8 = 28
   \]
   This is the combined weight of Marco's and his dad's strawberries after the loss.

2. Subtract Marco's strawberries from this new total:
   \[
   28 - 12 = 16
   \]
   This means Marco's dad now has 16 pounds of strawberries.

3. Double-check:
   - After losing 8 pounds, the combined weight is 28 pounds.
   - Marco's strawberries weigh 12 pounds, so Marco's dad's strawberries must weigh \( 28 - 12 = 16 \) pounds.
   - This matches the result from Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it directly calculates Marco's dad's original weight and then accounts for the loss. It is straightforward and easy to follow.
- **Method 2** is slightly less direct but still reliable. It focuses on the combined weight after the loss and then isolates Marco's dad's weight. This method is useful for problems where the combined weight is emphasized.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and more efficient for this problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**:
  1. Marco's dad originally had \( 36 - 12 = 24 \) pounds of strawberries.
  2. After losing 8 pounds, Marco's dad now has \( 24 - 8 = 16 \) pounds of strawberries.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Marco's strawberries weigh 12 pounds.
- Marco's dad originally had 24 pounds but lost 8 pounds, leaving him with 16 pounds.
- Together, they now have \( 12 + 16 = 28 \) pounds, which is 8 pounds less than the original 36 pounds, confirming the solution.

The answer is **16**.

Q: Jessie currently weighs 9 kilograms. After she started to go jogging everyday she lost 62 kilograms in the first week and 140 kilograms in the second week. How much did she weigh before starting to jog?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jessie currently weighs **9 kilograms**.
- She lost **62 kilograms** in the first week and **140 kilograms** in the second week.
- We are tasked with finding how much Jessie weighed **before** she started jogging.
- Assumptions:
  - The weight loss is cumulative (i.e., the total weight lost is the sum of the two weeks).
  - There are no other weight changes (e.g., weight gain) during this period.
  - The problem assumes a hypothetical scenario, as losing this much weight in two weeks is unrealistic for a human.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Add the total weight lost to her current weight.**
- Logic: If Jessie currently weighs 9 kilograms and lost a total of 62 + 140 kilograms, her starting weight can be calculated by adding the total weight lost to her current weight.

**Method 2: Work backward using subtraction.**
- Logic: Start with Jessie's current weight (9 kilograms) and sequentially add back the weight she lost in the second week and then the first week. This method mirrors the process of "undoing" the weight loss.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Add the total weight lost to her current weight.**
1. Calculate the total weight lost:
   \[
   \text{Total weight lost} = 62 + 140 = 202 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]
2. Add the total weight lost to her current weight:
   \[
   \text{Starting weight} = \text{Current weight} + \text{Total weight lost}.
   \]
   Substituting the values:
   \[
   \text{Starting weight} = 9 + 202 = 211 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - If Jessie started at 211 kilograms and lost 202 kilograms, her current weight would indeed be:
     \[
     211 - 202 = 9 \, \text{kilograms}.
     \]
   - This confirms the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Work backward using subtraction.**
1. Start with Jessie's current weight (9 kilograms).
2. Add back the weight lost in the second week:
   \[
   \text{Weight after second week} = 9 + 140 = 149 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]
3. Add back the weight lost in the first week:
   \[
   \text{Starting weight} = 149 + 62 = 211 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]
4. Double-check:
   - If Jessie started at 211 kilograms, lost 62 kilograms in the first week, and then 140 kilograms in the second week, her current weight would be:
     \[
     211 - 62 - 140 = 9 \, \text{kilograms}.
     \]
   - This confirms the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it involves fewer steps and combines the weight lost into a single calculation before adding it to the current weight.
- **Method 2** is more step-by-step and may be easier to follow for someone who prefers to work incrementally, but it involves more intermediate calculations.
- Both methods are equally reliable, but **Method 1** is slightly more efficient.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
1. Calculate the total weight lost:
   \[
   62 + 140 = 202 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]
2. Add the total weight lost to her current weight:
   \[
   9 + 202 = 211 \, \text{kilograms}.
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Jessie started at 211 kilograms and lost 62 kilograms in the first week, her weight would be:
  \[
  211 - 62 = 149 \, \text{kilograms}.
  \]
- After losing 140 kilograms in the second week, her weight would be:
  \[
  149 - 140 = 9 \, \text{kilograms}.
  \]
- This matches the given information, confirming the solution is correct.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **211 kilograms**.

Q: 43 children were riding on the bus. At the bus stop some children got off the bus. Then there were 21 children left on the bus. How many children got off the bus at the bus stop?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Initially, there were 43 children on the bus.  
- At the bus stop, some children got off the bus.  
- After this, 21 children remained on the bus.  
- We assume no children got on the bus at the stop, and no children were lost or unaccounted for.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Subtract the number of children remaining on the bus from the initial number of children: \( 43 - 21 \).  
Method 2: Use trial-and-error reasoning: Find the number of children that, when subtracted from 43, leaves 21.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Start with the total number of children on the bus: 43.  
- Subtract the number of children remaining on the bus: \( 43 - 21 \).  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  \[
  43 - 21 = 22
  \]  
- Double-check the subtraction: Add the result (22) to the remaining children (21) to ensure it equals the original total:  
  \[
  22 + 21 = 43
  \]  
- This confirms the calculation is correct.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the total number of children on the bus: 43.  
- Use trial-and-error reasoning: Find the number \( x \) such that \( 43 - x = 21 \).  
- Rearrange the equation to solve for \( x \):  
  \[
  x = 43 - 21
  \]  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  \[

Q: There are a total of 16 peaches in a basket. If there are 13 red peaches and some more green peaches in the basket. How many green peaches are in the basket?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are a total of 16 peaches in the basket.
- Of these, 13 are red peaches.
- The remaining peaches are green peaches.
- We assume no peaches are missing, damaged, or otherwise unaccounted for.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of red peaches from the total number of peaches to find the number of green peaches: \( \text{Total peaches} - \text{Red peaches} = \text{Green peaches} \).

**Method 2:** Use addition to verify the total number of peaches. Assume the number of green peaches is \( x \), then solve \( \text{Red peaches} + x = \text{Total peaches} \) for \( x \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of peaches: \( 16 \).
2. Subtract the number of red peaches: \( 16 - 13 = 3 \).
3. The result is the number of green peaches: \( 3 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Ensure subtraction is performed correctly.
- Verify that the total number of peaches matches the sum of red and green peaches: \( 13 + 3 = 16 \). This confirms the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Addition**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of green peaches.
2. Write the equation: \( 13 + x = 16 \).
3. Solve for \( x \): \( x = 16 - 13 = 3 \).
4. The result is the number of green peaches: \( 3 \).

**Check for pitfalls:**
- Ensure the equation is set up correctly.
- Verify the solution by substituting \( x = 3 \) back into the equation: \( 13 + 3 = 16 \). This confirms the calculation is correct.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction)** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single operation and is easy to understand.
- **Method 2 (Addition)** is slightly more complex, as it requires setting up an equation and solving for \( x \). However, it is equally reliable and provides a way to verify the solution.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution: \( 16 - 13 = 3 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total number of peaches: \( 13 \text{ red peaches} + 3 \text{ green peaches} = 16 \text{ peaches} \). This confirms the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **3**.
