Q: Stray cats loved eating goldfish in the pond leaving 6 goldfish. Paige had raised 8 goldfish in the pond initially. How many goldfish disappeared?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paige initially raised **8 goldfish** in the pond.  
- Stray cats ate some of the goldfish, leaving **6 goldfish** in the pond.  
- We assume no goldfish were added to the pond, and the only change in the number of goldfish is due to the stray cats eating them.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of goldfish remaining from the initial number: \( 8 - 6 \).  
**Method 2:** Use trial-and-error reasoning: Find the number of goldfish eaten such that the initial number minus this amount equals the remaining number.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.
#### Method 1: Subtraction  
1. Start with the initial number of goldfish: \( 8 \).  
2. Subtract the number of goldfish remaining: \( 8 - 6 \).  
3. Perform the calculation:  
   \[
   8 - 6 = 2
   \]  
4. Verify the result: If 2 goldfish were eaten, then \( 8 - 2 = 6 \), which matches the number of goldfish left in the pond.  

#### Method 2: Trial-and-error reasoning  
1. Start with the initial number of goldfish: \( 8 \).  
2. Assume a number of goldfish eaten, \( x \), and check if \( 8 - x = 6 \).  
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   8 - x = 6 \implies x = 8 - 6 = 2
   \]  
4. Verify the result: If \( x = 2 \), then \( 8 - 2 = 6 \), which matches the number of goldfish left in the pond.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a simple subtraction operation without requiring trial-and-error reasoning.  
- **Method 2** is less efficient because it introduces unnecessary steps and relies on guessing or solving an equation, which is redundant for this straightforward problem.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Solution:  
   \[
   8 - 6 = 2
   \]  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify: If 2 goldfish were eaten, then \( 8 - 2 = 6 \), which matches the number of goldfish left in the pond.  
- The result is consistent with the problem's information and assumptions.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.

- Kelly has 46 Nintendo games and 132 Sony games.
- She wants to give away some Sony games so that she will have **31 Sony games left**.
- We assume no Sony games are lost, broken, or added during this process—only given away.
- The problem is asking how many Sony games Kelly needs to give away.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction Approach**  
- Start with the total number of Sony games Kelly has (132).
- Subtract the number of Sony games she wants to keep (31).
- The result will be the number of Sony games she needs to give away.

**Method 2: Reverse Equation Approach**  
- Represent the number of Sony games Kelly gives away as \( x \).
- Set up an equation: \( 132 - x = 31 \), where \( x \) is the number of Sony games given away.
- Solve for \( x \) by isolating it on one side of the equation.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with the total number of Sony games: 132.
2. Subtract the number of Sony games Kelly wants to keep:  
   \[
   132 - 31 = 101
   \]
3. The result is 101, which represents the number of Sony games Kelly needs to give away.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- Ensure subtraction is performed correctly.  
- Double-check that the result makes sense: If Kelly gives away 101 games, she will have \( 132 - 101 = 31 \) games left, which matches the problem's requirements.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse Equation Approach**
1. Represent the number of Sony games Kelly gives away as \( x \).
2. Set up the equation:  
   \[
   132 - x = 31
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \) by isolating it:  
   Subtract 31 from both sides:  
   \[
   132 - 31 = x
   \]
   Simplify:  
   \[
   x = 101
   \]
4. The result is 101, which represents the number of Sony games Kelly needs to give away.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- Ensure the equation is set up correctly.  
- Verify the solution by substituting \( x = 101 \) back into the equation:  
   \[
   132 - 101 = 31
   \]
   This confirms the solution is correct.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 2 (Reverse Equation Approach)** is slightly more complex because it requires setting up and solving an equation. However, it is useful for verifying the logic of the problem and is a good alternative for those who prefer algebraic reasoning.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and efficiency.

Q: Last week Fred had 49 dollars and Jason had 3 dollars. Fred washed cars over the weekend and now has 112 dollars. Jason delivered newspapers and now has 63 dollars. How much money did Jason earn by delivering newspapers?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Fred started with **49 dollars** and now has **112 dollars**.  
- Jason started with **3 dollars** and now has **63 dollars**.  
- We are tasked with finding how much money Jason **earned** by delivering newspapers.  
- Assumptions:  
  - Jason did not spend or lose any money during this time.  
  - All of Jason's current money (63 dollars) is the sum of his starting amount (3 dollars) and the money he earned.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract Jason's starting amount from his current total to find how much he earned:  
  \( \text{Money earned} = \text{Current amount} - \text{Starting amount} \).  

**Method 2: Reverse Addition Method**  
- Start with Jason's initial amount and add increments until reaching his current total. The sum of the increments will equal the money he earned.  

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Method**
1. Jason's current amount is **63 dollars**.  
2. Jason's starting amount is **3 dollars**.  
3. Subtract the starting amount from the current amount:  
   \[
   63 - 3 = 60
   \]  
4. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Add the result (60) back to the starting amount (3):  
     \[
     60 + 3 = 63
     \]  
   - This confirms the calculation is correct.  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- Subtraction errors could occur if calculations are rushed, but this is unlikely with such simple numbers.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse Addition Method**
1. Start with Jason's initial amount: **3 dollars**.  
2. Add increments to reach his current total of **63 dollars**:  
   - \( 3 + 60 = 63 \).  
3. The total increment added is **60 dollars**, which represents the money Jason earned.  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- This method is less efficient and could lead to errors if the increments are not calculated correctly. However, the simplicity of the numbers minimizes this risk.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)** is more direct, efficient, and less prone to error. It involves a single subtraction operation and a quick verification step.  
- **Method 2 (Reverse Addition Method)** is less efficient because it requires additional steps to "build up" to the total. While it works, it is unnecessarily complex for this problem.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Subtraction Method):**  
1. Subtract Jason's starting amount from his current total:  
   \[
   63 - 3 = 60
   \]  
2. Verify the result:  
   - Add the result (60) back to the starting amount (3):  
     \[
     60 + 3 = 63
     \]  
   - The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Jason started with 3 dollars and now has 63 dollars.  
- The difference between these amounts is 60 dollars, which is reasonable given the context of earning money by delivering newspapers.  
- All calculations have been verified and are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **60**.

Q: Rebecca wants to split a collection of eggs into 3 groups. Rebecca has 4 marbles and 15 eggs. How many eggs will each group have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Rebecca has **15 eggs** and wants to split them into **3 groups**.
- Rebecca also has **4 marbles**, but the marbles are irrelevant to the problem since the question only concerns the eggs.
- We assume the eggs can be evenly divided into 3 groups without breaking or discarding any eggs.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- Divide the total number of eggs (15) by the number of groups (3). This will give the number of eggs per group if the division is exact.

**Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**  
- Start with the total number of eggs (15) and repeatedly subtract the number of eggs in one group (3) until no eggs remain. Count how many groups are formed in the process.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Divide the total number of eggs by the number of groups:  
   \[
   15 \div 3 = 5
   \]
2. Check if the division is exact by multiplying the result (5) by the number of groups (3):  
   \[
   5 \times 3 = 15
   \]
   Since the product equals the original total, the division is exact, and each group will have 5 eggs.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
- If the total number of eggs were not divisible by 3, this method would result in a fraction or remainder, which would require further interpretation. However, in this case, 15 is divisible by 3.

#### **Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with 15 eggs and subtract 3 eggs (one group) repeatedly:  
   - After 1st subtraction: \( 15 - 3 = 12 \)  
   - After 2nd subtraction: \( 12 - 3 = 9 \)  
   - After 3rd subtraction: \( 9 - 3 = 6 \)  
   - After 4th subtraction: \( 6 - 3 = 3 \)  
   - After 5th subtraction: \( 3 - 3 = 0 \)  
2. Count the number of subtractions performed: 5 subtractions were needed to reduce the total to 0. This means there are 5 eggs in each group.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
- This method is more time-consuming and prone to error if the subtraction is not performed carefully. However, it is a valid approach for smaller numbers.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is more efficient and straightforward, especially for larger numbers. It provides a direct answer with minimal steps and is easy to verify.
- **Method 2 (Repeated Subtraction Approach)** is less efficient and more prone to error, as it involves multiple steps and requires careful tracking of the subtractions. However, it can be useful for visualizing the grouping process.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Solution:  
   \[
   15 \div 3 = 5
   \]
   Each group will have 5 eggs.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the number of eggs per group (5) by the number of groups (3):  
   \[
   5 \times 3 = 15
   \]
   The total matches the original number of eggs, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **5**.
Extracted:
5.0
A:
5.0

Q: A waiter had 3 customers. After some more arrived he had 8 customers. How many new customers arrived?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The waiter initially had **3 customers**.
- After some new customers arrived, the waiter had a total of **8 customers**.
- We are tasked with finding how many **new customers arrived**.
- Assumptions:
  - No customers left during this time.
  - The total number of customers is the sum of the initial customers and the new arrivals.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract the initial number of customers from the total number of customers to find the number of new arrivals:  
  \( \text{New Customers} = \text{Total Customers} - \text{Initial Customers} \).

**Method 2: Reverse Addition Method**  
- Start with the initial number of customers and add a variable \( x \) (representing the new customers) until the total equals 8. Solve for \( x \):  
  \( \text{Initial Customers} + x = \text{Total Customers} \).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Method**
1. Write the equation:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = \text{Total Customers} - \text{Initial Customers} \).
2. Substitute the known values:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = 8 - 3 \).
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = 5 \).
4. Double-check: Add the initial customers (3) to the new customers (5) to ensure the total matches:  
   \( 3 + 5 = 8 \). This confirms the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Misreading the problem or incorrectly substituting values could lead to errors, but this method is straightforward and reliable.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse Addition Method**
1. Write the equation:  
   \( \text{Initial Customers} + x = \text{Total Customers} \), where \( x \) is the number of new customers.
2. Substitute the known values:  
   \( 3 + x = 8 \).
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   Subtract 3 from both sides:  
   \( x = 8 - 3 \).  
   \( x = 5 \).
4. Double-check: Add the initial customers (3) to the new customers (5) to ensure the total matches:  
   \( 3 + 5 = 8 \). This confirms the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- This method introduces an extra variable, which might make it slightly more complex for simple problems. However, it is still reliable.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity:** Method 1 (Subtraction) is more direct and intuitive, as it immediately calculates the difference between the total and initial customers. Method 2 (Reverse Addition) introduces a variable, which is unnecessary for this simple problem.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are equally reliable, as they use the same arithmetic operations and yield the same result.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is more efficient because it involves fewer steps and avoids introducing an extra variable.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Chosen Method:** Method 1 (Subtraction Method).  
1. Write the equation:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = \text{Total Customers} - \text{Initial Customers} \).
2. Substitute the known values:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = 8 - 3 \).
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   \( \text{New Customers} = 5 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the initial customers (3) to the new customers (5) to ensure the total matches:  
  \( 3 + 5 = 8 \).  
- The result is consistent with the problem statement, confirming the solution is correct.

Method 1: Add the number of emails received in the afternoon and evening directly: 5 + 8.

  5 + 8 = 13.

  Start with 5, then count up by 8: 5 + 1 = 6, 6 + 1 = 7, ..., 12 + 1 = 13.

- First, calculate the total number of emails received during the day:  
  4 (morning) + 5 (afternoon) + 8 (evening) = 17.  
- Subtract the morning emails (4) from the total:  
  17 - 4 = 13.  
- Double-check the subtraction:  
  Start with 17, then count down by 4: 17 - 1 = 16, 16 - 1 = 15, ..., 14 - 1 = 13.

Q: In Haley's class 13 boys love to play marbles and 50 boys love to play cards. If Haley has 26 marbles How many will each of the boys receive?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Haley has **26 marbles** in total.  
- **13 boys** in her class love to play marbles.  
- The problem asks how many marbles each of the 13 boys will receive if the marbles are distributed equally.  
- Assumptions:  
  - All 26 marbles are distributed equally among the 13 boys.  
  - No marbles are lost, broken, or kept aside.  
  - The division results in a whole number (no fractional marbles).  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct division. Divide the total number of marbles (26) by the number of boys (13) to find how many marbles each boy receives.  

**Method 2:** Use repeated subtraction. Start with the total number of marbles (26) and repeatedly subtract 13 (the number of boys) until the total reaches zero. Count how many times subtraction occurs to determine how many marbles each boy receives.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Division**
1. Divide the total number of marbles (26) by the number of boys (13):  
   \[
   26 \div 13 = 2
   \]  
2. Each boy receives **2 marbles**.  
3. Double-check the calculation: Multiply the result (2 marbles per boy) by the number of boys (13):  
   \[
   2 \times 13 = 26
   \]  
   This matches the total number of marbles, confirming the calculation is correct.  

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Division is straightforward, but errors could occur if the numbers are misread or miscalculated.  

#### **Method 2: Repeated Subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of marbles (26). Subtract 13 (the number of boys) repeatedly:  
   - First subtraction: \( 26 - 13 = 13 \)  
   - Second subtraction: \( 13 - 13 = 0 \)  
2. Count the number of subtractions: There were **2 subtractions**.  
3. Each boy receives **2 marbles**.  
4. Double-check the calculation: Multiply the result (2 marbles per boy) by the number of boys (13):  
   \[
   2 \times 13 = 26
   \]  
   This matches the total number of marbles, confirming the calculation is correct.  

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Repeated subtraction is less efficient for larger numbers and can be prone to counting errors.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct Division)** is more efficient and straightforward, especially for problems involving simple division. It avoids unnecessary steps and is less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2 (Repeated Subtraction)** is less efficient and involves more steps, making it more prone to errors in counting or subtraction. It is better suited for teaching basic concepts but not for solving problems quickly.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.  
- Solution:  
   \[
   26 \div 13 = 2
   \]  
- Each boy receives **2 marbles**.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the result (2 marbles per boy) by the number of boys (13):  
   \[
   2 \times 13 = 26
   \]  
- The total matches the original number of marbles, confirming the solution is correct.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.

Q: A waiter had 14 customers. 5 customers left. How many customers does he still have?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- The waiter initially had 14 customers.  
- 5 customers left.  
- We assume no additional customers arrived during this time.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Subtract the number of customers who left from the initial number of customers: 14 - 5.  
Method 2: Use a counting-down approach: Start at 14 and count down by 1 for each customer who left until 5 customers are subtracted.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Start with the initial number of customers: 14.  
- Subtract the number of customers who left: 14 - 5.  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  - Start with the ones place: 4 - 5. Since 4 is smaller than 5, borrow 1 from the tens place.  
  - Adjust the tens place: 1 becomes 0, and the ones place becomes 14.  
  - Subtract: 14 - 5 = 9.  
- Result: The waiter has 9 customers remaining.  

Potential pitfalls: This method is straightforward, but errors could occur if subtraction is performed incorrectly or borrowing is misunderstood.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start at 14 customers.  
- Count down by 1 for each customer who left:  
  - After 1 customer leaves: 14 - 1 = 13.  
  - After 2 customers leave: 13 - 1 = 12.  
  - After 3 customers leave: 12 - 1 = 11.  
  - After 4 customers leave: 11 - 1 = 10.  
  - After 5 customers leave: 10 - 1 = 9.  
- Result: The waiter has 9 customers remaining.  

Potential pitfalls: This method is more time-consuming and prone to errors if counting is not done carefully.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more efficient and direct, requiring only a single subtraction operation. It is less prone to errors and faster to execute.  
- **Method 2** is slower and involves multiple steps, making it more prone to mistakes during counting. However, it may be helpful for individuals who prefer visualizing the process step by step.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution: Perform the subtraction: 14 - 5 = 9.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recheck the subtraction: 14 - 5 = 9.  
- Verify using Method 2: Counting down from 14 by 5 steps also results in 9.  
- The result is reasonable because the waiter started with 14 customers, and 5 leaving would leave fewer than the initial number.  

The answer is **9**.

- **Assumptions**:  
  - No camper rowed more than once (i.e., no overlap between groups).  
  - All numbers are accurate and represent distinct individuals.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
- **Method 1**: Add the number of campers from each time period directly:  
  \( 36 + 13 + 49 \).  
  This method involves straightforward addition of the three numbers.  

- **Method 2**: Group two numbers first, then add the third:  
  \( (36 + 49) + 13 \).  
  This method simplifies the addition by combining the largest numbers first, reducing the chance of error in mental math.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

   \( 36 + 13 = 49 \).

   \( 49 + 49 = 98 \).  
3. Double-check the addition:  
   - \( 36 + 13 = 49 \) is correct.  
   - \( 49 + 49 = 98 \) is correct.  

#### **Method 2**: Group two numbers first, then add the third.  
1. Combine the largest numbers first:  
   \( 36 + 49 = 85 \).  
2. Add the result to the remaining number:  
   \( 85 + 13 = 98 \).  
3. Double-check the addition:  
   - \( 36 + 49 = 85 \) is correct.  
   - \( 85 + 13 = 98 \) is correct.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1**:  
  - Advantage: Simple and intuitive, especially for smaller numbers.  
  - Disadvantage: Adding three numbers sequentially can increase the chance of error if the numbers are large or if mental math is used.  

- **Method 2**:  
  - Advantage: Grouping the largest numbers first reduces the number of steps and simplifies mental math.  
  - Disadvantage: Requires identifying the largest numbers first, which may not be intuitive for all users.  

**Conclusion**: Both methods are equally reliable for this problem, but **Method 2** is slightly more efficient due to fewer steps and simpler intermediate calculations.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 2**:  
1. Combine the largest numbers first:  
   \( 36 + 49 = 85 \).  
2. Add the result to the remaining number:  
   \( 85 + 13 = 98 \).

  - \( 36 + 49 = 85 \) is correct.  
  - \( 85 + 13 = 98 \) is correct.

- First, calculate the total number of junk mail pieces for one block (38 pieces per block).  
- Then divide this total by the number of houses on the block (19 houses).  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the number of junk mail pieces per house directly by dividing the number of pieces per block (38) by the number of houses on the block (19).  
- This method skips the intermediate step of calculating the total for one block.

   \[
   \text{Junk mail per block} = 38
   \]

   \[
   \text{Junk mail per house} = \frac{38}{19}
   \]
3. Perform the division:  
   \[
   38 \div 19 = 2
   \]
4. Check the result: Multiply the number of houses (19) by the junk mail per house (2) to ensure it equals the total junk mail per block (38):

   \[
   \text{Junk mail per house} = \frac{38}{19}
   \]
2. Perform the division:  
   \[
   38 \div 19 = 2
   \]
3. Check the result: Multiply the number of houses (19) by the junk mail per house (2) to ensure it equals the total junk mail per block (38):

   \]

- **Method 2** skips the intermediate step and directly divides the junk mail per block by the number of houses. This method is more efficient and straightforward for those comfortable with direct division.

Q: Randy has some blocks. He uses 52 blocks to build a tower. If there are 38 blocks left How many blocks did he have at the start?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Randy uses **52 blocks** to build a tower.
- After building the tower, **38 blocks are left**.
- We are tasked with finding how many blocks Randy had at the start.
- Assumptions:
  - No blocks are lost, broken, or added during the process.
  - The total number of blocks Randy started with is the sum of the blocks used and the blocks left.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct Addition**
- The total number of blocks Randy started with is the sum of the blocks used and the blocks left. 
- Use the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Total blocks} = \text{Blocks used} + \text{Blocks left}
  \]

**Method 2: Reverse Subtraction**
- Assume Randy started with an unknown number of blocks, \( x \). Subtract the blocks used from \( x \) to get the blocks left:
  \[
  x - \text{Blocks used} = \text{Blocks left}
  \]
- Rearrange the equation to solve for \( x \):  
  \[
  x = \text{Blocks used} + \text{Blocks left}
  \]

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Addition**
1. Start with the formula:  
   \[
   \text{Total blocks} = \text{Blocks used} + \text{Blocks left}
   \]
2. Substitute the given values:  
   \[
   \text{Total blocks} = 52 + 38
   \]
3. Perform the addition:  
   \[
   52 + 38 = 90
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break the addition into smaller steps: \( 52 + 30 = 82 \), then \( 82 + 8 = 90 \).
   - The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Reverse Subtraction**
1. Start with the equation:  
   \[
   x - \text{Blocks used} = \text{Blocks left}
   \]
2. Rearrange to solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = \text{Blocks used} + \text{Blocks left}
   \]
3. Substitute the given values:  
   \[
   x = 52 + 38
   \]
4. Perform the addition:  
   \[
   x = 90
   \]
5. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break the addition into smaller steps: \( 52 + 30 = 82 \), then \( 82 + 8 = 90 \).
   - The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Clarity**: Both methods are straightforward and rely on the same addition operation. However, Method 1 is more direct because it starts with the formula for total blocks, while Method 2 introduces an unnecessary variable (\( x \)).
- **Reliability**: Both methods are equally reliable, as they involve the same arithmetic operation.
- **Efficiency**: Method 1 is slightly more efficient because it avoids the extra step of rearranging the equation.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and directness.
- Using Method 1:

  \text{Total blocks} = \text{Blocks used} + \text{Blocks left}
  \]
  \[
  \text{Total blocks} = 52 + 38
  \]

  \text{Total blocks} = 90
  \]

- Calculate the total number of items per shelf by adding the action figures and cabinets: \( 8 + 10 \).  
- Multiply the total items per shelf by the number of shelves: \( (8 + 10) \times 4 \).

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total number of action figures across all shelves: \( 8 \times 4 \).  
- Calculate the total number of cabinets across all shelves: \( 10 \times 4 \).  
- Add the total action figures and total cabinets to find the overall total.

   18 \times 4 = 72

   10 \times 4 = 40
   \]

   32 + 40 = 72
   \]
5. No pitfalls observed; the calculation is straightforward.

- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller parts, which can be helpful for mental math or if the numbers are more complex. However, it involves more steps and intermediate calculations, which increases the chance of error.  
- For this problem, **Method 1** is more efficient and clear.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Calculate the total number of items per shelf:  
   \[
   8 + 10 = 18
   \]
2. Multiply the total items per shelf by the number of shelves:  
   \[
   18 \times 4 = 72
   \]

Q: Randy uses 36 blocks to build a tower. If he had 59 blocks How many blocks are left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Randy starts with **59 blocks**.
- He uses **36 blocks** to build a tower.
- We are tasked with finding how many blocks are **left** after building the tower.
- Assumptions:
  - No blocks are lost, broken, or added during the process.
  - The subtraction operation is straightforward, as the problem involves removing blocks from the total.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction**  
- Subtract the number of blocks used (36) from the total number of blocks (59): \( 59 - 36 \).  
- This method is straightforward and efficient.

**Method 2: Complementary addition**  
- Think of the problem as finding the missing number: What number, when added to 36, gives 59?  
- Solve for the missing number using addition: \( 36 + x = 59 \), so \( x = 59 - 36 \).  
- This method reframes subtraction as addition, which can be useful for mental math.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of blocks: \( 59 \).
2. Subtract the number of blocks used:  
   \[
   59 - 36
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction step by step:  
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 9 - 6 = 3 \).  
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 5 - 3 = 2 \).  
   - Combine the results: \( 59 - 36 = 23 \).
4. Double-check the subtraction: Add \( 23 + 36 \) to ensure it equals \( 59 \).  
   - \( 23 + 36 = 59 \), so the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Complementary addition**
1. Reframe the problem: What number, when added to 36, gives 59?  
   - \( 36 + x = 59 \).  
2. Solve for \( x \) by subtracting 36 from 59:  
   \[
   x = 59 - 36
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction step by step (as in Method 1):  
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 9 - 6 = 3 \).  
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 5 - 3 = 2 \).  
   - Combine the results: \( x = 23 \).
4. Verify the solution by adding \( 36 + 23 \):  
   - \( 36 + 23 = 59 \), confirming the result.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is more straightforward and efficient, as it directly solves the problem without reframing it. It is less prone to errors and requires fewer steps.
- **Method 2 (Complementary addition)** is useful for mental math or when subtraction is less intuitive, but it involves an extra step of reframing the problem, which may introduce unnecessary complexity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and directness.
- Solve using Method 1:  
  \[
  59 - 36 = 23
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by adding the remaining blocks to the blocks used:  
  \[
  23 + 36 = 59
  \]
- The calculation checks out, confirming the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **23**.

Q: 21 children were riding on the bus. At the bus stop 10 children got off the bus while some more got on the bus. Then there were 16 children altogether on the bus. How many children got on the bus at the bus stop?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Initially, there were **21 children** on the bus.  
- At the bus stop, **10 children got off** the bus.  
- After some children got on the bus, there were **16 children** on the bus.  
- We assume no children left the bus other than the 10 who got off, and no other events (e.g., children sneaking on or off) occurred.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the number of children who got off the bus from the initial number of children to find how many remained. Then calculate how many children must have gotten on the bus to reach the final total.  

**Method 2:**  
Use an equation to represent the situation:  
Let \( x \) represent the number of children who got on the bus. The equation is:  
\( \text{Initial children} - \text{children who got off} + \text{children who got on} = \text{final total} \). Solve for \( x \).  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Step-by-step solution**  
1. Start with the initial number of children: **21**.  
2. Subtract the number of children who got off the bus:  
   \[
   21 - 10 = 11
   \]  
   This means **11 children remained on the bus** after 10 got off.  
3. Determine how many children must have gotten on the bus to reach the final total of **16**:  
   \[
   16 - 11 = 5
   \]  
   So, **5 children got on the bus**.  

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step solution**  
1. Represent the situation with an equation:  
   \[
   21 - 10 + x = 16
   \]  
   Here, \( x \) is the number of children who got on the bus.  
2. Simplify the equation:  
   \[
   11 + x = 16
   \]  
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 16 - 11 = 5
   \]  
   So, **5 children got on the bus**.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and intuitive, breaking the problem into smaller steps. It is easy to follow and avoids unnecessary complexity.  
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is efficient and generalizable for more complex problems. However, it may be less intuitive for simpler problems like this one.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better suited for this specific problem due to its simplicity and clarity.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Subtract the number of children who got off the bus from the initial number:  
   \[
   21 - 10 = 11
   \]  
   **11 children remained on the bus.**  
2. Calculate how many children got on the bus to reach the final total:  
   \[
   16 - 11 = 5
   \]  
   **5 children got on the bus.**  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the final total:  
   \[
   11 \text{ (remaining children)} + 5 \text{ (children who got on)} = 16
   \]  
   The calculation checks out.  
- The result is reasonable given the problem's context.  

### Final Answer:  
The answer is **5**.

Q: He then went to see the oranges being harvested. He found out that they harvest 74 sacks per day and discard 71 of them. How many sacks of oranges will they have after 51 days of harvest?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The orange harvest produces **74 sacks per day**.
- **71 sacks are discarded** each day.
- This means only **3 sacks are kept per day** (74 - 71 = 3).
- The harvest lasts for **51 days**.
- We assume the rate of production and discarding remains constant throughout the 51 days.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Calculate the net sacks kept per day and multiply by the total number of days.**
- Subtract the discarded sacks (71) from the harvested sacks (74) to find the net sacks kept per day.
- Multiply the net sacks kept per day by the total number of days (51).

**Method 2: Calculate the total sacks harvested over 51 days and the total sacks discarded over 51 days, then subtract the discarded sacks from the harvested sacks.**
- Multiply the daily harvest (74) by the total number of days (51) to find the total sacks harvested.
- Multiply the daily discarded sacks (71) by the total number of days (51) to find the total sacks discarded.
- Subtract the total discarded sacks from the total harvested sacks.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Net sacks per day approach**
1. Calculate the net sacks kept per day:
   \[
   74 - 71 = 3 \, \text{sacks/day}.
   \]
2. Multiply the net sacks kept per day by the total number of days:
   \[
   3 \, \text{sacks/day} \times 51 \, \text{days} = 153 \, \text{sacks}.
   \]
3. Double-check:
   - If 3 sacks are kept each day, then over 51 days, \( 3 \times 51 = 153 \) sacks will be kept.

#### **Method 2: Total harvest and discard approach**
1. Calculate the total sacks harvested over 51 days:
   \[
   74 \, \text{sacks/day} \times 51 \, \text{days} = 3774 \, \text{sacks}.
   \]
2. Calculate the total sacks discarded over 51 days:
   \[
   71 \, \text{sacks/day} \times 51 \, \text{days} = 3621 \, \text{sacks}.
   \]
3. Subtract the total discarded sacks from the total harvested sacks:
   \[
   3774 - 3621 = 153 \, \text{sacks}.
   \]
4. Double-check:
   - Total harvested: \( 74 \times 51 = 3774 \).
   - Total discarded: \( 71 \times 51 = 3621 \).
   - Net sacks: \( 3774 - 3621 = 153 \).

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct because it focuses on the net sacks kept per day and avoids unnecessary intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** involves more steps and larger numbers, increasing the chance of arithmetic errors, but it provides a more detailed breakdown of the total harvest and discard process.
- Both methods are valid and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient and less error-prone.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method.
1. Calculate the net sacks kept per day:
   \[
   74 - 71 = 3 \, \text{sacks/day}.
   \]
2. Multiply the net sacks kept per day by the total number of days:
   \[
   3 \, \text{sacks/day} \times 51 \, \text{days} = 153 \, \text{sacks}.
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If 3 sacks are kept each day, then over 51 days, \( 3 \times 51 = 153 \) sacks will be kept. This matches the result from both methods.
- The result is reasonable because the number of sacks kept per day (3) is small compared to the total harvested (74), and the total number of days (51) is moderate.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **153**.

- Initially, there were **12 roses** and **2 orchids** in the vase.
- Jessica cut more roses and orchids from her garden and added them to the vase.
- After adding the flowers, there are now **10 roses** and **21 orchids** in the vase.
- We are tasked with finding **how many orchids Jessica cut**.
- Assumptions:
  - No flowers were removed from the vase during this process.
  - The flowers Jessica cut were added directly to the vase.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction of initial orchids from the final count.**
- To find how many orchids Jessica cut, subtract the initial number of orchids (2) from the final number of orchids (21).
- This method directly calculates the difference between the initial and final orchid counts.

**Method 2: Use a general equation for the change in flower counts.**
- Let the number of orchids Jessica cut be \( x \).
- The equation for orchids becomes: \( 2 + x = 21 \).
- Solve for \( x \) to determine how many orchids Jessica cut.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

**Method 1: Subtraction of initial orchids from the final count.**
1. Start with the initial number of orchids: \( 2 \).
2. Subtract this from the final number of orchids: \( 21 - 2 \).
3. Perform the subtraction: \( 21 - 2 = 19 \).
4. Double-check: Adding the orchids Jessica cut (19) to the initial count (2) should equal the final count (21). \( 2 + 19 = 21 \), which checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- This method assumes no orchids were removed, which aligns with the problem statement.

---

**Method 2: Use a general equation for the change in flower counts.**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of orchids Jessica cut.
2. Write the equation for orchids: \( 2 + x = 21 \).
3. Solve for \( x \): Subtract \( 2 \) from both sides: \( x = 21 - 2 \).
4. Perform the subtraction: \( x = 19 \).
5. Double-check: Substitute \( x = 19 \) back into the equation: \( 2 + 19 = 21 \), which checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:**
- This method is algebraic and requires setting up the equation correctly, but it is straightforward for this problem.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this specific problem due to its simplicity and efficiency.

1. Subtract the initial number of orchids from the final number: \( 21 - 2 \).
2. Perform the subtraction: \( 21 - 2 = 19 \).
3. Double-check: Adding the orchids Jessica cut (19) to the initial count (2) should equal the final count (21). \( 2 + 19 = 21 \), which checks out.

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Add the number of customers who left (9) to the number of customers remaining (12).  
Method 2: Represent the initial number of customers as \( x \), then set up an equation: \( x - 9 = 12 \). Solve for \( x \).

- Add the number of customers who left (9) to the number of customers remaining (12):

  \]

  \]

- Represent the initial number of customers as \( x \). Set up the equation:  
  \[
  x - 9 = 12
  \]  
- Solve for \( x \) by adding 9 to both sides:  
  \[
  x = 12 + 9
  \]  
- Perform the addition:  
  \[
  x = 21
  \]  
- Double-check the solution by substituting \( x = 21 \) back into the equation:  
  \[
  21 - 9 = 12
  \]  
- The equation holds true, confirming the solution.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is simpler and more intuitive, as it directly adds the two known quantities.  
- **Method 2** introduces an equation, which is useful for more complex problems but unnecessary here.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is more efficient and easier to understand.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

  9 + 12 = 21

- She played tag with **some more kids on Tuesday**.
- The **total number of kids** she played with over both days is **15**.

  - No kids were counted twice (i.e., the kids she played with on Monday are different from those on Tuesday).
  - The total of 15 includes all kids from both days.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract the number of kids Julia played with on Monday from the total number of kids to find how many she played with on Tuesday.  
- Formula: \( \text{Kids on Tuesday} = \text{Total Kids} - \text{Kids on Monday} \).

**Method 2: Trial-and-Error Method**  
- Start with the total number of kids (15) and test different values for the number of kids on Tuesday until the sum of Monday's and Tuesday's kids equals 15.  
- This method is less efficient but can be used to verify the result.

   \[
   \text{Kids on Tuesday} = 15 - 5
   \]
3. Perform the subtraction:  
   \[
   15 - 5 = 10
   \]
4. Result: Julia played with **10 kids on Tuesday**.

- Ensure the subtraction is correct.  
- Verify that the total number of kids (Monday + Tuesday) equals 15:  
  \[
  5 + 10 = 15
  \]
  The calculation checks out.

1. Start with the total number of kids: \( 15 \).
2. Assume a value for the number of kids Julia played with on Tuesday, say \( x \), and check if \( 5 + x = 15 \).
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 15 - 5
   \]
4. Perform the subtraction:  
   \[
   x = 10
   \]
5. Result: Julia played with **10 kids on Tuesday**.

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- Ensure the assumed value satisfies the equation \( 5 + x = 15 \).  
- Verify the total: \( 5 + 10 = 15 \). The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)** is more direct, efficient, and reliable. It involves a single subtraction and is less prone to error.  
- **Method 2 (Trial-and-Error Method)** is less efficient and requires unnecessary guessing, though it can be useful for verification.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better approach due to its simplicity and clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)**:

2. Subtract the number of kids Julia played with on Monday:

Q: At the arcade Dave had won 13 tickets. If he used 8 to buy some toys and 18 more to buy some clothes How many more tickets did Dave use to buy clothes than he did to buy toys?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Dave won 13 tickets at the arcade.
- He used **8 tickets** to buy toys.
- He used **18 tickets** to buy clothes.
- The question asks how many **more tickets** Dave used to buy clothes than toys.
- Assumptions:
  - The total number of tickets Dave used is not relevant to this specific question.
  - We are only comparing the tickets used for toys and clothes.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Direct subtraction: Subtract the number of tickets used for toys (8) from the number of tickets used for clothes (18). This will give the difference in tickets used.

**Method 2:**  
Use a comparison approach: Start with the tickets used for clothes (18) and count backward until you reach the tickets used for toys (8). This method involves step-by-step subtraction to find the difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the number of tickets used for clothes: 18.
2. Subtract the number of tickets used for toys:  
   \( 18 - 8 = 10 \).
3. The result is the difference in tickets used.

**Check:**  
- If Dave used 8 tickets for toys and 10 more tickets for clothes, then the total tickets used for clothes should be \( 8 + 10 = 18 \), which matches the given information.  
- This confirms the calculation is correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Comparison approach**
1. Start with the tickets used for clothes: 18.
2. Count backward to the tickets used for toys (8):  
   - From 18 to 17: 1 ticket.  
   - From 17 to 16: 1 ticket.  
   - Continue until reaching 8.  
   - Total steps: \( 18 - 8 = 10 \).
3. The result is the difference in tickets used.

**Check:**  
- Counting backward step-by-step confirms the difference is 10.  
- This matches the result from Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it involves a single subtraction operation. It is less prone to error and faster to execute.  
- **Method 2** is more time-consuming and involves multiple steps, which increases the chance of making a mistake. However, it can be useful for visualizing the problem or for those who prefer a step-by-step approach.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Subtract the tickets used for toys from the tickets used for clothes:  
   \( 18 - 8 = 10 \).  
2. The difference is 10 tickets.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Dave used 8 tickets for toys and 10 more tickets for clothes, then the total tickets used for clothes should be \( 8 + 10 = 18 \), which matches the given information.  
- Both methods confirm the result is correct.

- This method directly calculates the difference between the two quantities.

**Method 2: Use a comparison framework.**
- Start by listing the two quantities (morning: 5, afternoon: 3).
- Incrementally compare the two values to determine how much larger the morning count is than the afternoon count.
- This method is useful for visualizing the difference step by step.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Write the subtraction equation: \( 5 - 3 \).
2. Perform the calculation: \( 5 - 3 = 2 \).
3. Double-check the calculation:
   - Start with 5 and count backward by 3: \( 5 \to 4 \to 3 \to 2 \).
   - The result is confirmed as 2.
4. Potential pitfalls:
   - Misreading the question and subtracting in the wrong order (e.g., \( 3 - 5 \), which would give an incorrect negative result).

#### **Method 2: Use a comparison framework**
1. Start with the morning count (5) and the afternoon count (3).
2. Compare the two values incrementally:
   - From 3, count up to 5: \( 3 \to 4 \to 5 \).
   - Count the steps: \( 4, 5 \) (2 steps).
3. The difference is 2.
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Reverse the process: Start at 5 and count down to 3: \( 5 \to 4 \to 3 \).
   - Count the steps: \( 4, 3 \) (2 steps).
5. Potential pitfalls:
   - Losing track of the steps during the incremental comparison.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is faster, more straightforward, and less prone to error. It involves a single calculation and is easy to verify.
- **Method 2 (Comparison framework)** is more visual and may be helpful for those who prefer step-by-step reasoning. However, it is less efficient and more prone to counting errors, especially with larger numbers.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

Using **Method 1 (Direct subtraction):**
1. Subtract the afternoon count from the morning count: \( 5 - 3 \).
2. Perform the calculation: \( 5 - 3 = 2 \).

Q: A farmer had 160 tomatoes in his garden. If he picked 56 of them yesterday and 41 today. How many did he have left after yesterday's picking?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The farmer started with **160 tomatoes** in his garden.
- He picked **56 tomatoes yesterday**.
- He picked **41 tomatoes today**.
- The question asks how many tomatoes were left **after yesterday's picking**, so we only consider the tomatoes picked yesterday (56) and ignore today's picking (41) for this calculation.
- We assume no tomatoes were lost, spoiled, or added to the garden during this time.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of tomatoes picked yesterday (56) from the total number of tomatoes (160). This directly gives the number of tomatoes left after yesterday's picking.

**Method 2:** First calculate how many tomatoes were picked in total (yesterday and today), then subtract today's picking (41) from the total picked to isolate yesterday's picking. Finally, subtract yesterday's picking (56) from the total tomatoes (160).

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of tomatoes: **160**.
2. Subtract the number of tomatoes picked yesterday:  
   \( 160 - 56 = 104 \).
3. The result, **104**, represents the number of tomatoes left after yesterday's picking.

**Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
- Subtraction is straightforward, and there are no intermediate steps where errors could easily occur.  
- Double-check: Add the number of tomatoes picked yesterday (56) to the remaining tomatoes (104):  
  \( 104 + 56 = 160 \), confirming the calculation is correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Total picking and reverse calculation**
1. Calculate the total number of tomatoes picked (yesterday and today):  
   \( 56 + 41 = 97 \).  
   This step is unnecessary for the specific question but helps verify the total picked.
2. Subtract today's picking (41) from the total picked to isolate yesterday's picking:  
   \( 97 - 41 = 56 \).  
   This confirms the number picked yesterday is correct.
3. Subtract yesterday's picking (56) from the total tomatoes (160):  
   \( 160 - 56 = 104 \).

**Check for errors or pitfalls:**  
- This method introduces extra steps, which could lead to errors if calculations are not carefully tracked.  
- Double-check: Add the number of tomatoes picked yesterday (56) to the remaining tomatoes (104):  
  \( 104 + 56 = 160 \), confirming the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves only one subtraction step to find the answer. It minimizes the risk of errors and is easier to follow.
- **Method 2** introduces unnecessary intermediate steps (calculating total picking and isolating yesterday's picking), which are not required to answer the specific question. While it still leads to the correct result, it is less efficient and more prone to mistakes.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Using Method 1:  
  \( 160 - 56 = 104 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the number of tomatoes picked yesterday (56) to the remaining tomatoes (104):  
  \( 104 + 56 = 160 \).  
  This confirms the calculation is correct and reasonable.

   \[

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- David did 30 more push-ups than Zachary.
- David did a total of 37 push-ups.
- We are tasked with finding how many push-ups Zachary did.
- Assumptions:
  - The problem is linear, meaning the relationship between David's and Zachary's push-ups is straightforward (addition or subtraction).
  - No other factors (e.g., partial push-ups or shared push-ups) are involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Approach**  
- Since David did 30 more push-ups than Zachary, we can subtract 30 from David's total to find Zachary's total: \( \text{Zachary's push-ups} = \text{David's push-ups} - 30 \).

**Method 2: Equation Approach**  
- Represent Zachary's push-ups as \( x \). Since David did 30 more push-ups than Zachary, we can write the equation:  
  \[
  x + 30 = 37
  \]
  Solve for \( x \) to find Zachary's push-ups.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with David's total push-ups: 37.
2. Subtract the 30 extra push-ups David did compared to Zachary:  
   \[
   37 - 30 = 7
   \]
3. Result: Zachary did 7 push-ups.

**Check:**  
- If Zachary did 7 push-ups, and David did 30 more, then:  
  \[
  7 + 30 = 37
  \]
  This matches David's total, so the calculation is correct.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Misinterpreting the problem and adding 30 instead of subtracting could lead to an incorrect result.

---

#### **Method 2: Equation Approach**
1. Let \( x \) represent Zachary's push-ups.
2. Write the equation based on the problem:  
   \[
   x + 30 = 37
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 37 - 30
   \]
   \[
   x = 7
   \]
4. Result: Zachary did 7 push-ups.

**Check:**  
- Substitute \( x = 7 \) back into the equation:  
  \[
  7 + 30 = 37
  \]
  This matches David's total, so the solution is correct.

**Potential Pitfalls:**  
- Errors in setting up the equation (e.g., reversing the relationship) could lead to incorrect results.

---

  - Direct and simple, requiring only one subtraction step.  
  - Minimal chance of error if the relationship is understood correctly.  
  - Best suited for straightforward problems like this one.

- **Method 2 (Equation Approach):**  
  - More formal and generalizable, especially for more complex problems.  
  - Slightly more effort required to set up and solve the equation.  
  - Useful if the problem involves more variables or relationships.

**Conclusion:**  
For this specific problem, **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.

- Assumptions:
  - The number of kids Julia played with on Tuesday is a positive integer.
  - No other days or external factors are involved.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 2:**

\[ x + 12 = 16 \]

     \[
     4 + 12 = 16
     \]

- The first chapter is **53 pages long**.
- The second chapter is **75 pages long** (irrelevant to this specific question but provided for context).
- The third chapter is **21 pages long**.
- The question asks: **How many more pages does the first chapter have than the third chapter?**
- Assumption: We are comparing only the first and third chapters, and no pages are missing or miscounted.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of pages in the third chapter from the number of pages in the first chapter: \( 53 - 21 \). This directly calculates the difference.

**Method 2:** Use a step-by-step breakdown:
1. Start with the number of pages in the first chapter (53).
2. Subtract the pages in the third chapter (21) incrementally, e.g., subtract 20 first, then subtract 1. This method is useful for mental math.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the number of pages in the first chapter: \( 53 \).
2. Subtract the number of pages in the third chapter: \( 53 - 21 \).
   - Perform the subtraction:
     - Subtract the ones place: \( 3 - 1 = 2 \).
     - Subtract the tens place: \( 5 - 2 = 3 \).
   - Result: \( 53 - 21 = 32 \).
3. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Add \( 32 + 21 \) to confirm it equals \( 53 \):
     - \( 32 + 21 = 53 \), so the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Incremental subtraction**
1. Start with the number of pages in the first chapter: \( 53 \).
2. Subtract \( 20 \) first:
   - \( 53 - 20 = 33 \).
3. Subtract \( 1 \) next:
   - \( 33 - 1 = 32 \).
4. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Add \( 32 + 21 \) to confirm it equals \( 53 \):
     - \( 32 + 21 = 53 \), so the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single subtraction step. It is less prone to error when working with written calculations.
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math or when breaking the problem into smaller, manageable steps. However, it is slightly more time-consuming and introduces more opportunities for error due to multiple steps.
- For this problem, **Method 1** is the better choice because it is faster and more straightforward.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**:
  - \( 53 - 21 = 32 \).
- Double-check:
  - \( 32 + 21 = 53 \), confirming the result.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Both methods confirm the same result.

Q: Debby drinks 6 bottles a day. If she bought a total of 12 water bottles How many days would they last her?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Debby drinks 6 bottles of water per day.  
- She bought a total of 12 water bottles.  
- We assume Debby drinks consistently at the rate of 6 bottles per day.  
- We assume no bottles are wasted, lost, or shared with others.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Divide the total number of bottles by the daily consumption rate: \( \text{Total bottles} \div \text{Bottles per day} = \text{Number of days} \).  
Method 2: Use subtraction iteratively: Start with 12 bottles and subtract 6 bottles each day until the total reaches zero, counting the number of days.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Formula: \( 12 \div 6 = 2 \).  
- Explanation: Debby drinks 6 bottles per day, so dividing the total number of bottles (12) by her daily consumption rate (6) gives the number of days the bottles will last.  
- Calculation: \( 12 \div 6 = 2 \).  
- Check: Multiply the result (2 days) by the daily consumption rate (6 bottles/day): \( 2 \times 6 = 12 \), which matches the total number of bottles.  
- Pitfall: This method assumes Debby drinks exactly 6 bottles per day without variation, which is consistent with the problem's assumptions.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with 12 bottles.  
- Day 1: Subtract 6 bottles. Remaining bottles: \( 12 - 6 = 6 \).  
- Day 2: Subtract another 6 bottles. Remaining bottles: \( 6 - 6 = 0 \).  
- Count the days: Debby finishes the bottles in 2 days.  
- Check: Add up the bottles consumed over the days: \( 6 + 6 = 12 \), which matches the total number of bottles.  
- Pitfall: This method is slower and involves repetitive subtraction, but it is useful for visualizing the process step by step.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Method 1 is more efficient and direct, as it uses division to immediately calculate the number of days. It is reliable and avoids unnecessary steps.  
- Method 2 is slower and involves repetitive subtraction, but it is helpful for visualizing the process in cases where division might be unclear.  
- For this problem, Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Using Method 1: \( 12 \div 6 = 2 \).  
- Debby’s 12 bottles will last her 2 days.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Multiply the result (2 days) by the daily consumption rate (6 bottles/day): \( 2 \times 6 = 12 \), which matches the total number of bottles.  
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable given the problem's assumptions.

Q: Tiffany was collecting cans for recycling. On monday she had some bags of cans. The next day she found 4 more bags worth of cans. If she had a total of 8 bags altogether How many bags did she have on monday?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Tiffany had some bags of cans on Monday.
- On Tuesday, she found 4 more bags of cans.
- Altogether, she had a total of 8 bags of cans after adding the 4 bags she found on Tuesday.
- Assumptions:
  - No bags were lost, removed, or added in any other way.
  - The total number of bags includes the bags she had on Monday and the 4 additional bags found on Tuesday.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Subtraction Approach**  
- Start with the total number of bags (8).
- Subtract the 4 bags found on Tuesday to determine how many bags Tiffany had on Monday.

**Method 2: Equation Approach**  
- Let the number of bags Tiffany had on Monday be \( x \).
- Write an equation to represent the situation: \( x + 4 = 8 \).
- Solve for \( x \) to find the number of bags Tiffany had on Monday.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with the total number of bags: 8.
2. Subtract the 4 bags found on Tuesday:  
   \[
   8 - 4 = 4
   \]
3. This means Tiffany had 4 bags on Monday.

**Check for Reasonableness:**  
- If Tiffany had 4 bags on Monday and found 4 more on Tuesday, the total would be:  
  \[
  4 + 4 = 8
  \]
- This matches the given total, so the solution is reasonable.

#### **Method 2: Equation Approach**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of bags Tiffany had on Monday.
2. Write the equation:  
   \[
   x + 4 = 8
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   Subtract 4 from both sides:  
   \[
   x = 8 - 4
   \]
   \[
   x = 4
   \]
4. This means Tiffany had 4 bags on Monday.

**Check for Reasonableness:**  
- Substitute \( x = 4 \) back into the equation:  
  \[
  4 + 4 = 8
  \]
- The equation holds true, so the solution is reasonable.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Subtraction Approach):** This method is straightforward and involves a single subtraction step. It is efficient and easy to understand.
- **Method 2 (Equation Approach):** This method is slightly more formal and involves setting up and solving an equation. While it is reliable, it is more complex than necessary for this problem.

**Comparison:**  
- Method 1 is simpler and more efficient for this specific problem.

Q: You have 104 dollars. How many packs of dvds can you buy if each pack costs 26 dollars?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- You have $104.  
- Each pack of DVDs costs $26.  
- We assume there are no taxes, discounts, or additional fees.  
- The goal is to determine how many packs of DVDs you can buy with $104.  

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Use division to calculate how many packs can be purchased: Divide the total amount of money ($104) by the cost per pack ($26).  
**Method 2:** Use repeated subtraction: Subtract $26 from $104 repeatedly until the remaining amount is less than $26, counting the number of subtractions.  

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Divide $104 by $26:  
  \[
  104 \div 26 = 4
  \]  
- This means you can buy 4 packs of DVDs.  
- Check the calculation: Multiply the number of packs (4) by the cost per pack ($26):  
  \[
  4 \times 26 = 104
  \]  
- The total matches the original amount, confirming the result.  

**Potential Pitfall:** Ensure the division is performed correctly and that the result is a whole number, as partial packs cannot be purchased.  

---

**Method 2:**  
- Start with $104 and subtract $26 repeatedly:  
  \[
  104 - 26 = 78 \quad \text{(1 pack purchased)}  
  \]  
  \[
  78 - 26 = 52 \quad \text{(2 packs purchased)}  
  \]  
  \[
  52 - 26 = 26 \quad \text{(3 packs purchased)}  
  \]  
  \[
  26 - 26 = 0 \quad \text{(4 packs purchased)}  
  \]  
- Count the number of subtractions: 4 subtractions, meaning 4 packs can be purchased.  

**Potential Pitfall:** Ensure subtraction is performed accurately and stop when the remaining amount is less than $26.  

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward, as division directly provides the answer without requiring multiple steps. It is also less prone to errors compared to repeated subtraction.  
- **Method 2** is more intuitive for those unfamiliar with division but is slower and requires more steps, increasing the chance of calculation errors.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and reliability.

Q: Adam could fit 11 action figures on each shelf in his room. His room has 4 shelves and 40 cabinets. How many total action figures could his shelves hold?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Adam can fit **11 action figures** on each shelf.
- His room has **4 shelves**.
- There are also **40 cabinets**, but the problem specifically asks about the **shelves**, so the cabinets are irrelevant to the calculation.
- We assume all shelves are identical and can hold the same number of action figures.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Multiply the number of action figures per shelf (11) by the number of shelves (4). This is a straightforward multiplication problem.

**Method 2:**  
Break the problem into smaller steps:  
- First, calculate how many action figures fit on 2 shelves (11 × 2).  
- Then double that result to account for all 4 shelves. This method uses smaller, simpler calculations.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication**
1. Multiply the number of action figures per shelf by the number of shelves:  
   \( 11 \times 4 = 44 \).  
2. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 11 \times 4 = (10 \times 4) + (1 \times 4) = 40 + 4 = 44 \).  
3. Verify the result: The calculation is straightforward, and there are no additional steps where errors could occur.  

#### **Method 2: Incremental calculation**
1. Calculate the number of action figures on 2 shelves:  
   \( 11 \times 2 = 22 \).  
2. Double the result to account for all 4 shelves:  
   \( 22 \times 2 = 44 \).  
3. Double-check the calculations:  
   - For \( 11 \times 2 \): \( (10 \times 2) + (1 \times 2) = 20 + 2 = 22 \).  
   - For \( 22 \times 2 \): \( (20 \times 2) + (2 \times 2) = 40 + 4 = 44 \).  
4. Verify the result: The calculations are correct, and the result matches Method 1.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single multiplication step. It is less prone to errors because there are fewer intermediate steps.  
- **Method 2** breaks the problem into smaller, simpler calculations, which may be helpful for mental math or for someone less comfortable with multiplication. However, it introduces more steps, increasing the chance of errors.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution:  
  \( 11 \times 4 = 44 \).  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using Method 2:  
  \( 11 \times 2 = 22 \), then \( 22 \times 2 = 44 \).  
- The result is consistent across both methods.  
- The result is reasonable because 11 action figures per shelf across 4 shelves should yield a total close to \( 10 \times 4 = 40 \), and the extra 1 per shelf adds 4 more, giving 44.

### Final Answer:
The total number of action figures the shelves can hold is **44**.  
The answer is **44**.

**Method 2: Estimation and Refinement**

   \[

     133 \div 19 = 7

Q: An industrial machine can make 6 shirts a minute. It worked for 5 minutes yesterday and for 12 minutes today. How many shirts did machine make today?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The industrial machine makes **6 shirts per minute**.
- It worked for **5 minutes yesterday** and **12 minutes today**.
- The question asks for the number of shirts made **today**, so we only focus on the 12 minutes worked today.
- We assume the machine operates at a constant rate of 6 shirts per minute without interruptions or malfunctions.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct multiplication.**
- Multiply the rate of production (6 shirts per minute) by the time worked today (12 minutes).
- This method directly calculates the total number of shirts made today.

**Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps.**
- First, calculate how many shirts the machine makes in 1 minute (already given as 6).
- Then, calculate how many shirts are made in 10 minutes (6 × 10 = 60).
- Finally, calculate how many shirts are made in the remaining 2 minutes (6 × 2 = 12) and add the results.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct multiplication**
1. Multiply the rate of production by the time worked today:
   \[
   6 \, \text{shirts/minute} \times 12 \, \text{minutes} = 72 \, \text{shirts}.
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break it into smaller steps: \(6 \times 10 = 60\) and \(6 \times 2 = 12\), then add \(60 + 12 = 72\).
3. No pitfalls are present in this method as it is straightforward.

#### **Method 2: Break the problem into smaller steps**
1. Calculate the number of shirts made in 10 minutes:
   \[
   6 \, \text{shirts/minute} \times 10 \, \text{minutes} = 60 \, \text{shirts}.
   \]
2. Calculate the number of shirts made in the remaining 2 minutes:
   \[
   6 \, \text{shirts/minute} \times 2 \, \text{minutes} = 12 \, \text{shirts}.
   \]
3. Add the results:
   \[
   60 \, \text{shirts} + 12 \, \text{shirts} = 72 \, \text{shirts}.
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - The intermediate steps are correct, and the total matches the result from Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single multiplication step. It is less prone to errors because there are fewer intermediate calculations.
- **Method 2** is useful for breaking the problem into smaller, more manageable parts, which can be helpful for mental math or when working without a calculator. However, it introduces more steps, which increases the chance of errors.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
\[
6 \, \text{shirts/minute} \times 12 \, \text{minutes} = 72 \, \text{shirts}.
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Recalculate using Method 2:
  - Shirts in 10 minutes: \(6 \times 10 = 60\).
  - Shirts in 2 minutes: \(6 \times 2 = 12\).
  - Total: \(60 + 12 = 72\).
- The result is consistent across both methods.
