Q: Jessie weighed 114 kilograms. After she started to go jogging everyday she lost 50 kilograms in the first week and 60 kilograms in the second week. How much does she weigh now?
A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Jessie initially weighed 114 kilograms.  
- She lost 50 kilograms in the first week and 60 kilograms in the second week.  
- We assume the weight loss is cumulative (i.e., the total weight lost is the sum of the losses from both weeks).  
- We assume no other weight changes occurred during this period.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Subtract the total weight lost (50 + 60) from Jessie’s initial weight: 114 - (50 + 60).  
Method 2: Subtract the weight lost week by week: First subtract 50 from 114, then subtract 60 from the result.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total weight lost: 50 + 60 = 110.  
- Subtract the total weight lost from Jessie’s initial weight: 114 - 110 = 4.  
- Double-check: Adding the weight lost (110) to the final weight (4) should equal the initial weight (114). 4 + 110 = 114, which checks out.  

**Method 2:**  
- Subtract the first week’s weight loss from Jessie’s initial weight: 114 - 50 = 64.  
- Subtract the second week’s weight loss from the result: 64 - 60 = 4.  
- Double-check: Adding the weight lost in each step (50 + 60 = 110) to the final weight (4) should equal the initial weight (114). 4 + 110 = 114, which checks out.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Both methods yield the same result and are equally reliable.  
- Method 1 is slightly more efficient because it combines the weight losses into a single subtraction step, reducing the number of intermediate calculations.  
- Method 2 is more step-by-step and may be easier for someone who prefers breaking the problem into smaller parts.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is chosen for its efficiency.  
- Solution:  
  - Total weight lost: 50 + 60 = 110.  
  - Final weight: 114 - 110 = 4.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Jessie’s initial weight was 114 kilograms, and she lost a total of 110 kilograms. Her final weight is 4 kilograms.  
- Adding the weight lost (110) to the final weight (4) gives 114, confirming the calculation.

**Method 2:**  
Use a comparison approach: Start with the amount Gwen received from her mom (8 dollars) and count down to the amount she received from her dad (5 dollars) to find the difference.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

1. Start with the amount Gwen received from her mom: **8 dollars**.

   - From 7 to 6: 1 dollar.  
   - From 6 to 5: 1 dollar.  
   - Total difference: **3 dollars**.

- This method is more intuitive for some people but involves multiple steps, which increases the chance of error.  
- It is less efficient than direct subtraction.

---

- **Method 2** is less efficient and more prone to error due to the need for multiple steps (counting down). However, it may be helpful for those who prefer a visual or step-by-step comparison.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Subtract the amount Gwen received from her dad from the amount she received from her mom:  
    **8 - 5 = 3**.  
  - Gwen received **3 dollars more** from her mom than from her dad.

- Start with the total number of marbles Josh originally had: 20.  
- Subtract the number of marbles given to Jack: 20 - 2.  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  - 20 - 2 = 18.  
- Double-check the calculation:  
  - If Josh has 18 marbles now and gave away 2, adding 2 back to 18 should return to the original total: 18 + 2 = 20.

- Start with the number of marbles Josh has now (unknown).  
- Add the number of marbles given to Jack (2) to determine the original total: x + 2 = 20.  
- Solve for x:  
  - Subtract 2 from both sides: x = 20 - 2.  
  - x = 18.  
- Double-check the calculation:  
  - If Josh has 18 marbles now and gave away 2, adding 2 back to 18 should return to the original total: 18 + 2 = 20.  
- This method is slightly more complex but still reliable.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is simpler and less prone to error.

Q: Carol was sending out birthday invitations to her friends. Each package of invitations she bought had 10 invitations in it and she bought 7 packs. If she sends invitations to only 2 friends How many invitations will be left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Carol bought **7 packs** of invitations.
- Each pack contains **10 invitations**.
- She sends invitations to **2 friends**.
- We assume all invitations are intact, none are lost or damaged.
- We are tasked with finding how many invitations Carol has left after sending 2.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total number of invitations by multiplying the number of packs by the number of invitations per pack: \( 7 \times 10 \).  
- Subtract the number of invitations sent to friends: \( \text{Total Invitations} - 2 \).

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the total number of invitations per pack (10) and multiply by the number of packs (7) to get the total.  
- Subtract the number of invitations sent to friends (2) in one step: \( (7 \times 10) - 2 \).  
- This method combines the multiplication and subtraction into a single calculation.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of invitations:  
   \[
   7 \times 10 = 70
   \]  
   Carol has **70 invitations** in total.

2. Subtract the number of invitations sent to friends:  
   \[
   70 - 2 = 68
   \]  
   Carol has **68 invitations left**.

3. Double-check:  
   - Start with 70 invitations.  
   - Sending 2 leaves \( 70 - 2 = 68 \).  
   - The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Combine the multiplication and subtraction into one step:  
   \[
   (7 \times 10) - 2 = 70 - 2 = 68
   \]  
   Carol has **68 invitations left**.

2. Double-check:  
   - First, calculate \( 7 \times 10 = 70 \).  
   - Subtract \( 70 - 2 = 68 \).  
   - The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** separates the problem into two distinct steps (multiplication and subtraction), making it easier to follow and verify each step. This is especially useful for beginners or when explaining the solution to others.
- **Method 2** combines the steps into a single calculation, which is more efficient but may be harder to follow for some.
- Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is clearer and less prone to errors due to its step-by-step nature.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.  
1. Calculate the total number of invitations:  
   \[
   7 \times 10 = 70
   \]  
   Carol has **70 invitations** in total.

2. Subtract the number of invitations sent to friends:  
   \[
   70 - 2 = 68
   \]  
   Carol has **68 invitations left**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate: \( 7 \times 10 = 70 \), then \( 70 - 2 = 68 \).  
- The calculations are consistent and reasonable. Carol started with 70 invitations and sent only 2, so it makes sense that she has 68 left.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **68**.
Extracted:
68.0
A:
68.0

Q: A grocery store had 49 bottles of regular soda, 40 bottles of diet soda and 6 bottles of lite soda. How many bottles of regular soda and diet soda did they have altogether?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The grocery store has:
  - 49 bottles of regular soda.
  - 40 bottles of diet soda.
  - 6 bottles of lite soda.
- The problem asks for the total number of bottles of **regular soda and diet soda** combined.
- We assume no bottles are added or removed during the calculation.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Direct addition of the quantities of regular soda and diet soda: \( 49 + 40 \).  
**Method 2:** Group all soda types first (\( 49 + 40 + 6 \)), then subtract the lite soda (\( 49 + 40 + 6 - 6 \)) to isolate regular and diet soda.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Add the number of regular soda bottles to the number of diet soda bottles:
   \[
   49 + 40 = 89
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break it into smaller parts: \( 49 + 40 = (50 - 1) + 40 = 50 + 40 - 1 = 90 - 1 = 89 \).
   - The result checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method is straightforward and has minimal risk of error, as it directly addresses the problem without unnecessary steps.

---

#### **Method 2: Group all soda types, then subtract lite soda**
1. First, calculate the total number of all soda bottles:
   \[
   49 + 40 + 6 = 95
   \]
2. Subtract the number of lite soda bottles to isolate regular and diet soda:
   \[
   95 - 6 = 89
   \]
3. Double-check the calculation:
   - For \( 49 + 40 + 6 = 95 \), break it into smaller parts: \( 49 + 40 = 89 \), then \( 89 + 6 = 95 \).
   - For \( 95 - 6 = 89 \), break it into smaller parts: \( 95 - 6 = (95 - 5 - 1) = 90 - 1 = 89 \).
   - The result checks out.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method introduces unnecessary steps, increasing the chance of error. It is less efficient than Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is better because it directly addresses the problem without introducing unnecessary steps. It is simpler, faster, and less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is more complex and involves extra calculations that are not required to solve the problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**, add the number of regular soda bottles to the number of diet soda bottles:
  \[
  49 + 40 = 89
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using smaller parts: \( 49 + 40 = (50 - 1) + 40 = 50 + 40 - 1 = 90 - 1 = 89 \).
- The result is reasonable and matches the expected total.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **89**.
Extracted:
89.0
A:
89.0

Q: There are 4 different books and 17 different movies in the ' crazy silly school ' series. If you read 19 of the books and watched 7 of the movies How many more movies do you still have to watch?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There are **4 different books** and **17 different movies** in the "Crazy Silly School" series.  
- You have **read 19 books** and **watched 7 movies**.  
- The goal is to determine how many more movies you still need to watch.  
- Assumptions:  
  - The number of books read (19) exceeds the total number of books available (4), which suggests an error or irrelevant information about books.  
  - We focus only on the movies since the question asks about movies.  
  - No movies are rewatched, and the total number of movies remains fixed at 17.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:** Subtract the number of movies already watched (7) from the total number of movies (17).  
Logic: The difference between the total movies and the movies already watched gives the number of movies left to watch.

**Method 2:** Use a complementary approach: Count how many movies remain unwatched by listing all 17 movies and removing the 7 already watched.  
Logic: This method involves explicitly identifying the unwatched movies, which ensures no errors in subtraction.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of movies: **17**.  
2. Subtract the number of movies already watched: **17 - 7 = 10**.  
3. Double-check the subtraction:  
   - Break it into smaller steps:  
     - Subtract 7 from 10 first: **10 - 7 = 3**.  
     - Add back the remaining 7 to confirm: **3 + 7 = 10**.  
   - The subtraction checks out.  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- This method assumes the total number of movies (17) is correct and that no movies were rewatched or skipped.  

#### **Method 2: Complementary approach**
1. List all 17 movies in the series (hypothetically): Movie 1, Movie 2, ..., Movie 17.  
2. Remove the 7 movies already watched: Movie 1, Movie 2, ..., Movie 7.  
3. Count the remaining movies: Movie 8, Movie 9, ..., Movie 17.  
4. The remaining count is **10 movies**.  

**Potential pitfalls:**  
- This method is more tedious and prone to errors if the list of movies is not carefully tracked.  
- It is unnecessary for simple subtraction problems unless explicitly required.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more efficient and straightforward, as it directly calculates the number of movies left to watch using subtraction.  
- **Method 2** is unnecessarily complex for this problem, as listing and removing movies is redundant when the total and watched numbers are already provided.  
- **Reliability:** Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is less prone to human error.  
- **Clarity:** Method 1 is easier to explain and understand.  

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 1 is better.**  
1. Start with the total number of movies: **17**.  
2. Subtract the number of movies already watched: **17 - 7 = 10**.  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

1. Verify the subtraction:  
   - **17 - 7 = 10**.  
   - Add back the watched movies: **10 + 7 = 17**, confirming the calculation is correct.  
2. Confirm the result is reasonable:  
   - If 7 movies have been watched out of 17, it makes sense that 10 remain unwatched.  

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **10**.
Extracted:
10.0
A:
10.0

Q: Paul got a box of 531 crayons and 38 erasers for his birthday. At the end of the school year he only had 391 left while not having lost a single erasers. How many more crayons than erasers did he have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paul received **531 crayons** and **38 erasers** for his birthday.
- At the end of the school year, Paul had **391 crayons left**.
- Paul did not lose any erasers, so he still has **38 erasers**.
- The goal is to determine how many **more crayons than erasers** Paul had left at the end of the school year.
- Assumptions:
  - No crayons or erasers were added or removed except for the crayons Paul used.
  - The problem is asking for the **difference** between the number of crayons and erasers Paul had left.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct subtraction of erasers from crayons.**
- Subtract the number of erasers Paul has left (38) from the number of crayons he has left (391).
- This will directly give the difference between the two quantities.

**Method 2: Calculate the difference in initial quantities and adjust for crayons used.**
- First, calculate the initial difference between the number of crayons and erasers Paul received: \( 531 - 38 \).
- Then, subtract the number of crayons Paul used: \( 531 - 391 \).
- Finally, adjust the initial difference by the number of crayons used to find the final difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction of erasers from crayons.**
1. Start with the number of crayons Paul has left: \( 391 \).
2. Subtract the number of erasers Paul has left: \( 391 - 38 \).
3. Perform the subtraction:
   \[
   391 - 38 = 353
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 391 - 30 = 361 \), then \( 361 - 8 = 353 \).
5. Result: Paul has **353 more crayons than erasers**.

#### **Method 2: Calculate the difference in initial quantities and adjust for crayons used.**
1. Calculate the initial difference between crayons and erasers:
   \[
   531 - 38 = 493
   \]
2. Determine how many crayons Paul used:
   \[
   531 - 391 = 140
   \]
3. Adjust the initial difference by subtracting the crayons used:
   \[
   493 - 140 = 353
   \]
4. Double-check the calculations:
   - For the initial difference: \( 531 - 38 = 493 \) (confirmed).
   - For crayons used: \( 531 - 391 = 140 \) (confirmed).
   - For the final adjustment: \( 493 - 140 = 353 \) (confirmed).
5. Result: Paul has **353 more crayons than erasers**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it involves a single subtraction: \( 391 - 38 \). It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to error.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and provides additional context (e.g., the initial difference and crayons used), but it requires more calculations and is therefore less efficient for this specific problem.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Subtract the number of erasers from the number of crayons Paul has left:
   \[
   391 - 38 = 353
   \]
2. Double-check the calculation:
   - \( 391 - 30 = 361 \), then \( 361 - 8 = 353 \).

Result: Paul has **353 more crayons than erasers**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify the logic:
  - Paul started with significantly more crayons (531) than erasers (38), so it is reasonable that he still has many more crayons than erasers left.
  - The calculations align with the given information and assumptions.
- Verify the math:
  - Both methods independently confirm the result: \( 391 - 38 = 353 \).

---

The answer is **353**.
Extracted:
353.0

Q: The ring toss game at the carnival made the same amount of money each day. In total in 30 days they earned 420 dollars. Together with game they earned 22 dollars. How much did ring toss game make per day?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The ring toss game made the same amount of money each day for 30 days.
- In total, the ring toss game earned $420 over 30 days.
- Together with another game, they earned $22 in one day.
- We are tasked with finding how much the ring toss game made per day.
- Assumptions:
  - The $420 total is solely from the ring toss game.
  - The $22 daily earnings include both the ring toss game and the other game.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Divide the total earnings of the ring toss game ($420) by the number of days (30) to find the daily earnings of the ring toss game.

**Method 2:**  
Subtract the daily earnings of the other game from the combined daily earnings ($22) to isolate the daily earnings of the ring toss game. Then verify this result by multiplying the daily earnings of the ring toss game by 30 to ensure it matches the total of $420.

---

### Step 3: Work through both methods step by step, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Division**
1. Total earnings of the ring toss game = $420.  
2. Number of days = 30.  
3. Daily earnings of the ring toss game = $420 ÷ 30.  
   - Perform the division:  
     \[
     420 \div 30 = 14
     \]
4. The ring toss game made $14 per day.  
5. Verify: Multiply $14 (daily earnings) by 30 days:  
   \[
   14 \times 30 = 420
   \]
   - The total matches the given $420, confirming the calculation.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method assumes the $420 total is accurate and does not account for the combined daily earnings ($22) unless verified separately.

---

#### **Method 2: Subtraction and Verification**
1. Combined daily earnings of both games = $22.  
2. Total earnings of the ring toss game = $420 over 30 days.  
3. Calculate the daily earnings of the ring toss game:  
   - Divide $420 by 30 days:  
     \[
     420 \div 30 = 14
     \]
   - Daily earnings of the ring toss game = $14.  
4. Subtract the daily earnings of the ring toss game ($14) from the combined daily earnings ($22) to find the daily earnings of the other game:  
   \[
   22 - 14 = 8
   \]
   - Daily earnings of the other game = $8.  
5. Verify: Multiply the daily earnings of the ring toss game ($14) by 30 days:  
   \[
   14 \times 30 = 420
   \]
   - The total matches the given $420, confirming the calculation.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method introduces an extra step (subtracting the other game’s earnings) that is unnecessary for solving the main question.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct, as it focuses solely on the ring toss game’s total earnings and the number of days. It avoids unnecessary steps and calculations.  
- **Method 2** introduces additional complexity by considering the combined daily earnings and subtracting the other game’s earnings, which is not required to solve the main question.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its clarity, efficiency, and focus on the relevant information.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Total earnings of the ring toss game = $420.  
2. Number of days = 30.  
3. Daily earnings of the ring toss game = $420 ÷ 30.  
   - Perform the division:  
     \[
     420 \div 30 = 14
     \]
4. Verify: Multiply $14 (daily earnings) by 30 days:  
   \[
   14 \times 30 = 420
   \]
   - The total matches the given $420, confirming the calculation.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The calculation checks out: $420 ÷ 30 = $14 per day.  
- The result is reasonable, as $14 × 30 days = $420 matches the total earnings of the ring toss game.  

---

The answer is **14**.
Extracted:
14.0
A:
14.0

Q: Matthew had 15 crackers. If Matthew gave equal numbers of crackers to his 5 friends and still had 10 crackers left How many crackers did each friend eat?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Matthew started with **15 crackers**.
- He gave an **equal number of crackers** to his **5 friends**.
- After giving crackers to his friends, Matthew still had **10 crackers left**.
- We assume no crackers were lost, broken, or eaten by Matthew himself during the process.
- The goal is to determine how many crackers each friend received.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction and division approach**  
- Subtract the number of crackers Matthew had left (10) from the total crackers he started with (15) to find how many crackers he gave away in total.  
- Divide the total number of crackers given away by the number of friends (5) to find how many crackers each friend received.

**Method 2: Reverse calculation approach**  
- Start with the number of crackers Matthew had left (10).  
- Add back the crackers given to each friend (x crackers per friend, multiplied by 5 friends) to reach the total crackers Matthew started with (15).  
- Solve for x using algebra.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction and division approach**
1. Subtract the crackers Matthew had left from the total crackers he started with:  
   \[
   15 - 10 = 5
   \]  
   This means Matthew gave away **5 crackers** in total.

2. Divide the total crackers given away by the number of friends:  
   \[
   5 \div 5 = 1
   \]  
   Each friend received **1 cracker**.

3. Double-check: Multiply the number of crackers each friend received by the number of friends and add the crackers Matthew had left:  
   \[
   (1 \times 5) + 10 = 5 + 10 = 15
   \]  
   This matches the total crackers Matthew started with, so the calculation is correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Reverse calculation approach**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of crackers each friend received.  
   The total crackers given to all friends is \( 5x \), and the crackers Matthew had left is 10.  
   The total crackers Matthew started with is the sum of these two quantities:  
   \[
   5x + 10 = 15
   \]

2. Solve for \( x \):  
   Subtract 10 from both sides:  
   \[
   5x = 15 - 10
   \]  
   \[
   5x = 5
   \]  
   Divide both sides by 5:  
   \[
   x = 1
   \]  
   Each friend received **1 cracker**.

3. Double-check: Substitute \( x = 1 \) back into the equation:  
   \[
   5(1) + 10 = 5 + 10 = 15
   \]  
   This matches the total crackers Matthew started with, so the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient because it directly uses subtraction and division, which are simple operations. It avoids introducing variables or equations, making it easier to follow.  
- **Method 2** is more general and flexible, as it uses algebra to solve the problem. However, it is less efficient for this specific problem because it introduces unnecessary complexity.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and clarity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
1. Subtract the crackers Matthew had left from the total crackers he started with:  
   \[
   15 - 10 = 5
   \]  
   Matthew gave away **5 crackers** in total.

2. Divide the total crackers given away by the number of friends:  
   \[
   5 \div 5 = 1
   \]  
   Each friend received **1 cracker**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Multiply the number of crackers each friend received by the number of friends and add the crackers Matthew had left:  
  \[
  (1 \times 5) + 10 = 5 + 10 = 15
  \]  
  This matches the total crackers Matthew started with, confirming the solution is correct.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **1**.
Extracted:
1.0
A:
1.0

Q: The ring toss game at the carnival made 382 dollars in the first 44 days and 374 dollars in the remaining 10 days. How much did they make in all?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The ring toss game made $382 in the first 44 days.
- It made $374 in the remaining 10 days.
- We are tasked with finding the total amount of money made over the entire period.
- Assumptions:
  - The amounts given ($382 and $374) are accurate and represent the total earnings for their respective time periods.
  - There are no additional earnings or deductions not mentioned in the problem.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct addition of the two amounts.**
- Add the earnings from the first 44 days ($382) to the earnings from the remaining 10 days ($374).
- This method is straightforward and involves a single addition operation.

**Method 2: Break the numbers into parts for easier mental math.**
- Decompose $382 and $374 into hundreds, tens, and ones.
- Add the parts separately (hundreds, tens, ones) and then combine the results.
- This method is useful for verifying the calculation or performing mental math.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Write the numbers vertically for clarity:
   ```
     382
   + 374
   -----
   ```
2. Add the ones place: \(2 + 4 = 6\).
3. Add the tens place: \(8 + 7 = 15\). Write down 5 and carry over 1 to the hundreds place.
4. Add the hundreds place: \(3 + 3 = 6\), plus the carried-over 1 gives \(7\).
5. Combine the results: \(382 + 374 = 756\).

**Check for errors:**
- Re-add the numbers to confirm:
  - \(382 + 374 = 756\). The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Break into parts**
1. Decompose the numbers:
   - \(382 = 300 + 80 + 2\)
   - \(374 = 300 + 70 + 4\)
2. Add the hundreds: \(300 + 300 = 600\).
3. Add the tens: \(80 + 70 = 150\).
4. Add the ones: \(2 + 4 = 6\).
5. Combine the results: \(600 + 150 + 6 = 756\).

**Check for errors:**
- Verify each step:
  - Hundreds: \(300 + 300 = 600\) is correct.
  - Tens: \(80 + 70 = 150\) is correct.
  - Ones: \(2 + 4 = 6\) is correct.
  - Total: \(600 + 150 + 6 = 756\) matches the result from Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single addition operation. It is ideal when using a calculator or performing straightforward arithmetic.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller, manageable parts. It is useful for mental math or verifying results but is less efficient overall.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution:
  - \(382 + 374 = 756\).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using both methods:
  - Method 1: \(382 + 374 = 756\).
  - Method 2: \(600 + 150 + 6 = 756\).
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.
- The result is reasonable, as the total earnings ($756) are the sum of two realistic amounts ($382 and $374).

---

The answer is **756**.

756.0

Q: The school is planning a field trip. The school has 24 classrooms. There are 120 students in the school with each classroom having the same number of students. There are 84 seats on each school bus. How many students are in each class?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The school has **24 classrooms**.
- There are **120 students** in total.
- Each classroom has the **same number of students**.
- We are tasked with finding how many students are in each classroom.
- Assumptions:
  - The total number of students is evenly distributed across all classrooms.
  - No students are missing or unaccounted for.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Division Approach**  
Divide the total number of students (120) by the number of classrooms (24). This method directly calculates the number of students per classroom.

**Method 2: Iterative Subtraction Approach**  
Subtract the number of students in one classroom repeatedly from the total number of students until the total reaches zero. Count the number of subtractions performed to determine the number of classrooms.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Start with the total number of students: **120**.
2. Divide this by the number of classrooms: **120 ÷ 24**.
3. Perform the division:  
   - 120 ÷ 24 = 5.
4. Check the result: Multiply the number of students per classroom (5) by the number of classrooms (24) to ensure it equals the total number of students:  
   - 5 × 24 = 120.  
   - The calculation checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
This method assumes the total number of students is evenly divisible by the number of classrooms. If the division results in a fraction, it would indicate an error in the problem setup.

---

#### **Method 2: Iterative Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with the total number of students: **120**.
2. Subtract the number of students in one classroom repeatedly until the total reaches zero. Assume each classroom has the same number of students.
3. Perform the subtraction iteratively:  
   - Subtract 5 students (guessing the number per classroom):  
     120 - 5 = 115.  
     115 - 5 = 110.  
     Continue until the total reaches zero.  
   - After 24 subtractions, the total reaches zero.
4. Count the number of subtractions performed: **24**.
5. Check the result: Multiply the number of students per classroom (5) by the number of classrooms (24) to ensure it equals the total number of students:  
   - 5 × 24 = 120.  
   - The calculation checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
This method is less efficient and prone to errors due to the repetitive nature of subtraction. It is better suited for smaller numbers or when division is not feasible.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is more direct, efficient, and reliable. It involves fewer steps and avoids unnecessary repetition. It is also easier to verify the result by multiplying back.
- **Method 2 (Iterative Subtraction Approach)** is less efficient and more prone to errors due to the repetitive nature of subtraction. While it works, it is not practical for larger numbers.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice for solving this problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1 (Division Approach)**:
1. Divide the total number of students by the number of classrooms:  
   **120 ÷ 24 = 5**.
2. Verify the result: Multiply the number of students per classroom (5) by the number of classrooms (24):  
   **5 × 24 = 120**.  
   The calculation checks out.

- The total number of students (120) is evenly divisible by the number of classrooms (24), resulting in an integer value (5). This confirms the setup is reasonable.
- Verification through multiplication confirms the result: **5 × 24 = 120**.

Q: Frank was reading through his favorite book. He read 8 pages per day. If the book had 576 pages How many days did he take to finish the book?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Frank reads 8 pages per day.
- The book has a total of 576 pages.
- We assume Frank reads consistently at the rate of 8 pages per day without skipping any days or reading extra pages.
- The goal is to determine how many days it took Frank to finish the book.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- Divide the total number of pages (576) by the number of pages Frank reads per day (8). This will give the exact number of days required.

**Method 2: Iterative Subtraction Approach**  
- Start with the total number of pages (576) and repeatedly subtract 8 pages per day until the total reaches zero. Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of days.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Perform the division:  
   \[
   \text{Number of days} = \frac{\text{Total pages}}{\text{Pages per day}} = \frac{576}{8}
   \]
2. Calculate:  
   \[
   576 \div 8 = 72
   \]
3. Double-check the calculation: Multiply the result (72 days) by the number of pages per day (8) to ensure it equals the total number of pages:  
   \[
   72 \times 8 = 576
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:** Ensure the division is performed correctly and that the result is a whole number, as fractional days would not make sense in this context.

---

#### **Method 2: Iterative Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with the total number of pages (576). Subtract 8 pages per day and keep track of the number of subtractions:  
   - Day 1: \( 576 - 8 = 568 \)  
   - Day 2: \( 568 - 8 = 560 \)  
   - Day 3: \( 560 - 8 = 552 \)  
   - Continue this process until the total reaches zero.

2. Count the number of subtractions:  
   - After 72 subtractions, the total reaches zero:  
     \[
     576 - (72 \times 8) = 0
     \]

3. Double-check the result: Multiply the number of days (72) by the pages per day (8) to ensure it equals the total number of pages:  
   \[
   72 \times 8 = 576
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method is time-consuming and prone to errors if the subtraction process is not carefully tracked.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is more efficient and straightforward. It involves a single calculation and is easy to verify.  
- **Method 2 (Iterative Subtraction Approach)** is less practical for large numbers, as it requires repeated calculations and careful tracking, increasing the likelihood of errors.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice due to its simplicity, reliability, and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1 (Division Approach):**  
\[
\text{Number of days} = \frac{\text{Total pages}}{\text{Pages per day}} = \frac{576}{8} = 72
\]

Double-check:  
\[
72 \times 8 = 576
\]
The calculation is correct.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The result (72 days) is reasonable given the total number of pages (576) and the reading rate (8 pages per day).  
- The verification step confirms the calculation is accurate.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **72 days**.

Q: 29 campers went rowing and 66 campers went hiking in the morning. 26 campers went rowing in the afternoon. How many more campers went rowing in the morning than in the afternoon?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- In the morning, **29 campers** went rowing.
- In the afternoon, **26 campers** went rowing.
- We are tasked with finding how many **more campers went rowing in the morning than in the afternoon**.
- Assumptions:
  - The number of campers rowing in the morning and afternoon are distinct and do not overlap.
  - No additional campers joined or left during the day.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Direct subtraction: Subtract the number of campers who went rowing in the afternoon from the number who went rowing in the morning. This gives the difference directly.

**Method 2:**  
Comparison by counting up: Start with the number of campers in the afternoon (26) and count up to the number in the morning (29). The difference is the number of steps required to reach 29.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Subtract the number of campers in the afternoon from the number in the morning:
   \[
   29 - 26 = 3
   \]
2. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Start with 29 and count backward by 26: \( 29 - 26 = 3 \).
3. Potential pitfalls:
   - Misreading the problem and subtracting in the wrong order (e.g., \( 26 - 29 \), which would result in a negative number).

#### **Method 2: Comparison by counting up**
1. Start with the number of campers in the afternoon (26) and count up to the number in the morning (29):
   - From 26 to 27: +1
   - From 27 to 28: +1
   - From 28 to 29: +1
2. Add the steps: \( 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 \).
3. Double-check by recounting:
   - \( 26 \to 27 \to 28 \to 29 \): 3 steps.
4. Potential pitfalls:
   - Losing track of the steps while counting up.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is more efficient and straightforward, as it involves a single calculation. It is less prone to error and faster to execute.
- **Method 2 (Counting up)** is useful for visualizing the difference but is less efficient and more prone to error, especially with larger numbers or when counting steps manually.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Using Method 1:
  \[
  29 - 26 = 3
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify by reversing the subtraction:
  \[
  26 + 3 = 29
  \]
- The result checks out, and the difference is reasonable given the context of the problem.

---

The answer is **3**.

Q: The Razorback shop makes $ 86 dollars off each t-shirt and $ 232 off each jersey. During the Arkansas and Texas tech game they sold 254 t-shirts and 43 jerseys. How much more does a jersey cost than a t-shirt?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The Razorback shop makes $86 off each t-shirt and $232 off each jersey.  
- The question asks how much more a jersey costs than a t-shirt.  
- We assume the cost difference is calculated as the price of a jersey minus the price of a t-shirt.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Direct subtraction of the t-shirt price from the jersey price: \( 232 - 86 \).  
- This method directly calculates the difference between the two prices.  

**Method 2:** Use a ratio-based approach to compare the jersey price to the t-shirt price.  
- Divide the jersey price by the t-shirt price to find how many times more expensive a jersey is, then subtract 1 and multiply by the t-shirt price to find the difference.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Subtract the t-shirt price from the jersey price:  
   \[
   232 - 86 = 146
   \]
2. Double-check the subtraction:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 232 - 80 = 152 \), then \( 152 - 6 = 146 \).  
   - The calculation checks out.  

#### **Method 2: Ratio-based approach**
1. Calculate the ratio of the jersey price to the t-shirt price:  
   \[
   \text{Ratio} = \frac{232}{86} \approx 2.6977
   \]
2. Subtract 1 from the ratio to find how much more expensive a jersey is proportionally:  
   \[
   2.6977 - 1 = 1.6977
   \]
3. Multiply this proportional difference by the t-shirt price to find the cost difference:  
   \[
   1.6977 \times 86 \approx 146
   \]
4. Double-check the multiplication:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 1.6977 \times 80 = 135.816 \), then \( 1.6977 \times 6 = 10.1862 \).  
   - Add the results: \( 135.816 + 10.1862 \approx 146 \).  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct, requiring only subtraction. It avoids unnecessary complexity and is less prone to rounding errors.  
- **Method 2** involves more steps and introduces potential rounding errors during the ratio calculation. While it provides additional insight into proportional differences, it is less efficient for this problem.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.  
- Solution:  
   \[
   232 - 86 = 146
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the subtraction: \( 232 - 86 = 146 \).  
- The result is reasonable because the jersey price is significantly higher than the t-shirt price, and the difference aligns with expectations.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **146**.

Q: Each basket of peaches has 19 red peaches and 4 green peaches. If there are 15 such baskets How many peaches are in the baskets altogether?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Each basket contains **19 red peaches** and **4 green peaches**.
- There are **15 baskets** in total.
- We assume all baskets are identical in terms of the number of peaches.
- The total number of peaches in each basket is the sum of red and green peaches: \( 19 + 4 = 23 \).
- The goal is to find the total number of peaches across all 15 baskets.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate the total number of peaches in one basket (\( 19 + 4 = 23 \)).
- Multiply the total number of peaches in one basket by the number of baskets (\( 23 \times 15 \)).

**Method 2:**  
- Separate the calculation into two parts:  
  1. Calculate the total number of red peaches across all baskets (\( 19 \times 15 \)).  
  2. Calculate the total number of green peaches across all baskets (\( 4 \times 15 \)).  
  3. Add the results of the two calculations to get the total number of peaches.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of peaches in one basket:  
   \[
   19 + 4 = 23
   \]
2. Multiply the total number of peaches in one basket by the number of baskets:  
   \[
   23 \times 15
   \]
   Break this multiplication into smaller steps for clarity:  
   \[
   23 \times 15 = (23 \times 10) + (23 \times 5)
   \]
   \[
   23 \times 10 = 230, \quad 23 \times 5 = 115
   \]
   \[
   230 + 115 = 345
   \]
3. Total peaches = **345**.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the total number of red peaches across all baskets:  
   \[
   19 \times 15
   \]
   Break this multiplication into smaller steps:  
   \[
   19 \times 15 = (19 \times 10) + (19 \times 5)
   \]
   \[
   19 \times 10 = 190, \quad 19 \times 5 = 95
   \]
   \[
   190 + 95 = 285
   \]
   Total red peaches = **285**.

2. Calculate the total number of green peaches across all baskets:  
   \[
   4 \times 15 = 60
   \]
   Total green peaches = **60**.

3. Add the total red and green peaches to find the total number of peaches:  
   \[
   285 + 60 = 345
   \]
4. Total peaches = **345**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it combines the red and green peaches into a single calculation upfront. It requires fewer steps and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller parts, which can be helpful for understanding or verifying the solution. However, it involves more calculations and intermediate steps, which increases the chance of arithmetic errors.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Using Method 1:  
  \[
  23 \times 15 = (23 \times 10) + (23 \times 5)
  \]
  \[
  23 \times 10 = 230, \quad 23 \times 5 = 115
  \]
  \[
  230 + 115 = 345
  \]
- Total peaches = **345**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using Method 2 as a verification:  
  Total red peaches = \( 19 \times 15 = 285 \).  
  Total green peaches = \( 4 \times 15 = 60 \).  
  Total peaches = \( 285 + 60 = 345 \).  
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming the solution is correct.

---

Q: Baker made 81 cakes. Then he made 92 more cakes. He sold 46 of them. How many more cakes did baker make than those he sold?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Baker initially made 81 cakes.
- He then made 92 more cakes.
- He sold 46 cakes.
- We are tasked with finding how many more cakes the baker made than those he sold.
- Assume no cakes were lost, discarded, or otherwise unaccounted for.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of cakes the baker made by adding the initial and additional cakes (81 + 92). Then subtract the number of cakes sold (46) from the total made to find the difference between cakes made and cakes sold.

**Method 2:**  
Directly compare the total number of cakes made (81 + 92) to the number sold (46) by subtracting the number sold from the total made. This avoids intermediate steps and focuses on the difference.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the total number of cakes made:  
   \( 81 + 92 = 173 \).  
   The baker made 173 cakes in total.

2. Subtract the number of cakes sold from the total made:  
   \( 173 - 46 = 127 \).  
   The baker has 127 cakes remaining after selling 46.

3. Compare the total cakes made (173) to the cakes sold (46):  
   \( 173 - 46 = 127 \).  
   The difference between cakes made and cakes sold is 127.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
This method introduces an intermediate step (calculating remaining cakes), which is unnecessary for solving the specific question about the difference between cakes made and sold.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Directly calculate the difference between cakes made and cakes sold:  
   \( (81 + 92) - 46 = 173 - 46 = 127 \).  
   The baker made 127 more cakes than he sold.

**Potential Pitfall:**  
This method skips intermediate steps, but it requires careful attention to ensure the subtraction is performed correctly.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** introduces an intermediate step (calculating remaining cakes), which is unnecessary for solving the specific question. While it is clear and reliable, it is less efficient.
- **Method 2** is more direct and efficient, as it focuses solely on the difference between cakes made and sold. It avoids unnecessary calculations and is easier to follow for this specific problem.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
**Method 2** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
1. Calculate the total number of cakes made:  
   \( 81 + 92 = 173 \).  

2. Subtract the number of cakes sold:  
   \( 173 - 46 = 127 \).  

The baker made 127 more cakes than he sold.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
1. Recalculate the total cakes made:  
   \( 81 + 92 = 173 \).  

2. Verify the subtraction:  
   \( 173 - 46 = 127 \).

Q: The grasshopper and the frog had a jumping contest. The grasshopper jumped 35 inches. The grasshopper jumped 4 inches farther than the frog. How much did they jump altogether?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The grasshopper jumped **35 inches**.
- The grasshopper jumped **4 inches farther than the frog**.
- We are tasked with finding how far **both the grasshopper and the frog jumped altogether**.
- Assumptions:
  - The frog's jump distance can be calculated by subtracting 4 inches from the grasshopper's jump.
  - No other factors (e.g., additional jumps) are involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct calculation using subtraction and addition.**
- Subtract 4 inches from the grasshopper's jump to find the frog's jump distance.
- Add the grasshopper's jump and the frog's jump to find the total distance.

**Method 2: Use algebra to represent the problem.**
- Let the frog's jump distance be \( x \).
- The grasshopper's jump is \( x + 4 \).
- Solve for \( x \) using the given grasshopper jump distance, then calculate the total distance.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. The grasshopper jumped 35 inches.
2. The frog jumped \( 35 - 4 = 31 \) inches.
3. Add the two distances: \( 35 + 31 = 66 \) inches.
4. Double-check:
   - Frog's jump: \( 35 - 4 = 31 \) (correct).
   - Total: \( 35 + 31 = 66 \) (correct).

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Forgetting to subtract 4 inches to find the frog's jump distance.
- Miscalculating the addition of the two distances.

---

#### **Method 2: Algebraic approach**
1. Let the frog's jump distance be \( x \).
2. The grasshopper's jump is \( x + 4 \).
3. From the problem, \( x + 4 = 35 \).
4. Solve for \( x \): \( x = 35 - 4 = 31 \).
5. Add the two distances:
   - Frog's jump: \( x = 31 \).
   - Grasshopper's jump: \( x + 4 = 35 \).
   - Total: \( 31 + 35 = 66 \).
6. Double-check:
   - Substituting \( x = 31 \) into \( x + 4 = 35 \) confirms the grasshopper's jump is correct.
   - Total: \( 31 + 35 = 66 \) (correct).

**Potential pitfalls:**
- Misinterpreting the algebraic equation or solving incorrectly.
- Forgetting to add the two distances after solving for \( x \).

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient because it directly uses subtraction and addition without introducing variables. It is less prone to errors and easier to follow for this specific problem.
- **Method 2** is more general and useful for more complex problems, but it introduces unnecessary complexity for this simple scenario.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Using Method 1:**
1. The frog's jump: \( 35 - 4 = 31 \) inches.
2. Total distance: \( 35 + 31 = 66 \) inches.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Frog's jump: \( 35 - 4 = 31 \) (correct).
- Total distance: \( 35 + 31 = 66 \) (correct).
- The result is reasonable and consistent with the problem's conditions.

Q: The Razorback t-shirt shop makes $ 106 dollars off each t-shirt sold. During the Arkansas game and the Texas tech game they sold a total of 242 t-shirts. If they sold 115 t-shirts during the Arkansas game How many t-shirts did they sell during the Texas tech game?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The Razorback t-shirt shop sold a total of 242 t-shirts during two games: the Arkansas game and the Texas Tech game.
- During the Arkansas game, they sold 115 t-shirts.
- We need to determine how many t-shirts were sold during the Texas Tech game.
- Assumption: No t-shirts were sold outside of these two games, and the total sales figure (242 t-shirts) is accurate.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
- Subtract the number of t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game (115) from the total number of t-shirts sold (242).  
- This method directly calculates the number of t-shirts sold during the Texas Tech game.

**Method 2: Trial-and-Error Method**  
- Start with the total number of t-shirts sold (242) and subtract different values until the remaining number matches the t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game (115).  
- This method is less efficient but can be used to verify the subtraction method.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

**Method 1: Subtraction Method**  
1. Start with the total number of t-shirts sold: 242.  
2. Subtract the number of t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game:  
   \[
   242 - 115 = 127
   \]  
3. Interpret the result: The shop sold 127 t-shirts during the Texas Tech game.  
4. Double-check the calculation: Add the t-shirts sold during both games to ensure the total matches:  
   \[
   115 + 127 = 242
   \]  
   The total checks out, confirming the calculation is correct.

**Method 2: Trial-and-Error Method**  
1. Start with the total number of t-shirts sold: 242.  
2. Subtract different values until the remaining number matches the t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game (115).  
   - Try subtracting 127:  
     \[
     242 - 127 = 115
     \]  
   - The remaining number matches the t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game, confirming that 127 t-shirts were sold during the Texas Tech game.  
3. Double-check the calculation: Add the t-shirts sold during both games to ensure the total matches:  
   \[
   115 + 127 = 242
   \]  
   The total checks out, confirming the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Subtraction Method)** is more direct, efficient, and reliable. It involves fewer steps and avoids unnecessary trial-and-error.  
- **Method 2 (Trial-and-Error Method)** is less efficient and introduces unnecessary complexity. While it can verify the result, it is not practical for solving the problem initially.  

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Chosen Method: Subtraction Method**  
1. Start with the total number of t-shirts sold: 242.  
2. Subtract the number of t-shirts sold during the Arkansas game:  
   \[
   242 - 115 = 127
   \]  
3. Double-check the calculation: Add the t-shirts sold during both games to ensure the total matches:  
   \[
   115 + 127 = 242
   \]  
   The total checks out, confirming the calculation is correct.

Q: Ed had 29 more marbles than Doug. Ed lost 17 of his marbles at the playground. How many more marbles did Ed have than Doug then?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Ed initially had **29 more marbles than Doug**.
- Ed lost **17 marbles** at the playground.
- We are tasked with finding how many more marbles Ed had than Doug after Ed lost 17 marbles.
- Assumptions:
  - Doug did not lose or gain any marbles during this time.
  - The problem does not specify the exact number of marbles Doug had, but we can represent it as a variable.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Use algebraic representation.**
- Let Doug's number of marbles be \( D \).
- Ed initially had \( D + 29 \) marbles.
- After losing 17 marbles, Ed had \( (D + 29) - 17 \).
- The difference between Ed's and Doug's marbles after the loss is:
  \[
  \text{Difference} = [(D + 29) - 17] - D
  \]
- Simplify this expression to find the difference.

**Method 2: Use numerical reasoning with a hypothetical value for Doug's marbles.**
- Assume Doug has \( D \) marbles, and Ed starts with \( D + 29 \).
- Subtract 17 from Ed's total to find his new total.
- Subtract Doug's total from Ed's new total to find the difference.
- This method focuses on step-by-step numerical reasoning without heavy algebra.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Algebraic representation**
1. Start with Ed's initial number of marbles: \( D + 29 \).
2. Subtract the 17 marbles Ed lost: \( (D + 29) - 17 \).
   \[
   (D + 29) - 17 = D + 12
   \]
3. Find the difference between Ed's new total and Doug's total:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = (D + 12) - D
   \]
4. Simplify:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 12
   \]
5. This means Ed had **12 more marbles than Doug** after losing 17 marbles.

#### **Method 2: Numerical reasoning**
1. Assume Doug has \( D \) marbles.
2. Ed initially has \( D + 29 \) marbles.
3. After losing 17 marbles, Ed has:
   \[
   (D + 29) - 17 = D + 12
   \]
4. The difference between Ed's new total and Doug's total is:
   \[
   (D + 12) - D = 12
   \]
5. This confirms that Ed had **12 more marbles than Doug** after losing 17 marbles.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Algebraic representation):**
  - This method is systematic and generalizes the solution for any value of \( D \).
  - It is efficient and avoids unnecessary assumptions or trial-and-error.
- **Method 2 (Numerical reasoning):**
  - This method is intuitive and works well for those who prefer step-by-step numerical reasoning.
  - However, it essentially mirrors the algebraic method and does not offer additional clarity or efficiency.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better because it is more formal, generalizable, and concise.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1**:
1. Ed initially had \( D + 29 \) marbles.
2. After losing 17 marbles, Ed had \( (D + 29) - 17 = D + 12 \).
3. The difference between Ed's new total and Doug's total is:
   \[
   (D + 12) - D = 12
   \]
4. Therefore, Ed had **12 more marbles than Doug** after losing 17 marbles.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Ed starts with 29 more marbles than Doug and loses 17, the difference between their totals should decrease by exactly 17. Initially, the difference was 29, so the new difference is:
  \[
  29 - 17 = 12
  \]
- This confirms the solution is correct.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **12**.

Q: White t - shirts can be purchased in packages. If mom buys 28 packages which contain 56 white t - shirts in total How many white t - shirts does each package have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mom buys **28 packages** of white t-shirts.
- The total number of white t-shirts is **56**.
- We are tasked with finding how many white t-shirts are in **each package**.
- Assumptions:
  - All packages contain the same number of t-shirts.
  - No t-shirts are missing, damaged, or extra.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division**  
- Divide the total number of t-shirts (56) by the number of packages (28) to find the number of t-shirts per package.

**Method 2: Repeated subtraction**  
- Start with the total number of t-shirts (56) and repeatedly subtract the number of packages (28) until the remainder is zero. Count how many t-shirts are subtracted in each step to determine the number per package.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division**
1. Write the division equation:  
   \[
   \text{Number of t-shirts per package} = \frac{\text{Total t-shirts}}{\text{Number of packages}}
   \]
   Substituting the values:  
   \[
   \text{Number of t-shirts per package} = \frac{56}{28}
   \]
2. Perform the division:  
   - 28 goes into 56 exactly 2 times (since \(28 \times 2 = 56\)).
   - Therefore, the result is **2** t-shirts per package.

3. Check the result:  
   Multiply the number of t-shirts per package (2) by the number of packages (28):  
   \[
   2 \times 28 = 56
   \]
   The result matches the total number of t-shirts, so the calculation is correct.

---

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of t-shirts (56). Subtract the number of packages (28) repeatedly until the remainder is zero:
   - First subtraction: \(56 - 28 = 28\)
   - Second subtraction: \(28 - 28 = 0\)

2. Count the number of subtractions performed:  
   - Two subtractions were needed to reduce 56 to 0.

3. Interpret the result:  
   Each package must contain **2** t-shirts, as it took 2 subtractions to account for all 56 t-shirts.

4. Check the result:  
   Multiply the number of t-shirts per package (2) by the number of packages (28):  
   \[
   2 \times 28 = 56
   \]
   The result matches the total number of t-shirts, so the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Division)** is more efficient and straightforward, as it directly calculates the answer in one step. It is less prone to errors and is the standard approach for problems involving equal distribution.
- **Method 2 (Repeated subtraction)** is less efficient and involves more steps. While it works conceptually, it is more time-consuming and less practical for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1 (Division)** is the better method due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Using division:  
  \[
  \text{Number of t-shirts per package} = \frac{56}{28} = 2
  \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the result (2 t-shirts per package) by the number of packages (28):  
  \[
  2 \times 28 = 56
  \]
- The result matches the total number of t-shirts, confirming the solution is correct.

---

The answer is **2**.

Q: Paul got a box of 267 crayons for his birthday. During the school year he lost 231 crayons while he gave away 308 crayons to his friends. How many more crayons did he give to his friends than those he lost?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paul received a box of 267 crayons for his birthday.
- During the school year, he **lost** 231 crayons.
- He **gave away** 308 crayons to his friends.
- The goal is to determine how many **more crayons** Paul gave away than he lost.
- Assumptions:
  - The total number of crayons Paul started with (267) is irrelevant to solving this specific problem.
  - No crayons were recovered or returned.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Direct subtraction of the two quantities.**
- To find how many more crayons Paul gave away than he lost, subtract the number of crayons lost (231) from the number of crayons given away (308): \( 308 - 231 \).

**Method 2: Use a comparison approach.**
- Represent the crayons lost and given away as two separate groups.
- Start with the smaller group (lost crayons) and incrementally add crayons until reaching the larger group (given away crayons). Count the difference.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Write the equation: \( 308 - 231 \).
2. Perform the subtraction step by step:
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 8 - 1 = 7 \).
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 0 - 3 \) (borrow from the hundreds place, making it \( 10 - 3 = 7 \)).
   - Subtract the hundreds place: \( 3 - 2 = 1 \).
3. Combine the results: \( 308 - 231 = 77 \).
4. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Add \( 231 + 77 \) to confirm it equals \( 308 \).

Result: Paul gave away **77 more crayons** than he lost.

---

#### **Method 2: Comparison approach**
1. Start with the smaller group (lost crayons): \( 231 \).
2. Incrementally add crayons until reaching the larger group (given away crayons): \( 308 \).
   - \( 231 + 10 = 241 \).
   - \( 241 + 10 = 251 \).
   - \( 251 + 10 = 261 \).
   - \( 261 + 10 = 271 \).
   - \( 271 + 10 = 281 \).
   - \( 281 + 10 = 291 \).
   - \( 291 + 10 = 301 \).
   - \( 301 + 7 = 308 \).
3. Count the total difference: \( 10 + 10 + 10 + 10 + 10 + 10 + 10 + 7 = 77 \).

Result: Paul gave away **77 more crayons** than he lost.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction):**
  - Clear, efficient, and straightforward.
  - Requires only one calculation and is less prone to error.
  - Ideal for problems involving simple numerical differences.

- **Method 2 (Comparison approach):**
  - Useful for visualizing the difference between two quantities.
  - Involves multiple steps and is more prone to error due to incremental calculations.
  - Less efficient for larger numbers.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better due to its simplicity, reliability, and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Write the equation: \( 308 - 231 \).
2. Perform the subtraction:
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 8 - 1 = 7 \).
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 0 - 3 \) (borrow from the hundreds place, making it \( 10 - 3 = 7 \)).
   - Subtract the hundreds place: \( 3 - 2 = 1 \).
3. Combine the results: \( 308 - 231 = 77 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

1. Verify the subtraction by adding \( 231 + 77 \):
   - \( 231 + 77 = 308 \), confirming the result is correct.
2. The result is reasonable because Paul gave away significantly more crayons than he lost, and the difference aligns with the given numbers.

---

The answer is **77**.

Q: Paco had 40 cookies. He ate 2 of them. Then he bought 37 more cookies How many cookies did Paco have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paco started with **40 cookies**.
- He ate **2 cookies**, reducing the total number of cookies he had.
- After eating, he bought **37 more cookies**, adding to his remaining total.
- We assume no cookies were lost, shared, or otherwise removed except for the 2 he ate.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation.  
- Subtract the 2 cookies Paco ate from the initial 40 cookies: \( 40 - 2 \).  
- Add the 37 cookies he bought to the remaining total: \( (40 - 2) + 37 \).  

**Method 2:** Combine operations into a single equation.  
- Represent the problem as \( 40 - 2 + 37 \), performing all operations in one step.  
- This method simplifies the process by combining subtraction and addition directly.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Start with the initial number of cookies: \( 40 \).  
2. Subtract the 2 cookies Paco ate:  
   \[
   40 - 2 = 38
   \]  
   Now Paco has 38 cookies.  
3. Add the 37 cookies he bought:  
   \[
   38 + 37 = 75
   \]  
   After buying more cookies, Paco has 75 cookies.  

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- This method is straightforward and avoids confusion by breaking the problem into smaller steps.  
- Each step is easy to verify independently.  

#### **Method 2: Combine operations into a single equation**
1. Represent the problem as \( 40 - 2 + 37 \).  
2. Perform the subtraction first (following the order of operations):  
   \[
   40 - 2 = 38
   \]  
3. Add the result to 37:  
   \[
   38 + 37 = 75
   \]  

**Check for pitfalls:**  
- This method is efficient but requires careful attention to the order of operations.  
- Combining steps might lead to errors if calculations are rushed or misinterpreted.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more reliable for most people because it breaks the problem into smaller, manageable steps. Each step can be checked independently, reducing the chance of errors.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines operations into a single equation, but it requires careful attention to the order of operations. It may be less intuitive for some people.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and reliability.  
1. Start with \( 40 \) cookies.  
2. Subtract the 2 cookies Paco ate:  
   \[
   40 - 2 = 38
   \]  
3. Add the 37 cookies he bought:  
   \[
   38 + 37 = 75
   \]  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recalculate using **Method 2**:  
   \[
   40 - 2 + 37 = 38 + 37 = 75
   \]  
- Both methods yield the same result.  
- The result is reasonable: Paco started with 40 cookies, ate a small number (2), and then bought a large number (37), so ending with 75 cookies makes sense.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **75**.

- We assume no other trees were added, removed, or affected during this process.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

- Subtract the number of trees that died from the initial total: \( 9 - 4 \).  
- Add the number of new trees grown after the typhoon: \( (9 - 4) + 5 \).  

**Method 2:** Combine operations into a single equation:  
- Represent the problem as \( 9 - 4 + 5 \), performing subtraction and addition in one step.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Start with the initial number of trees: \( 9 \).  
2. Subtract the number of trees that died:  
   \[
   9 - 4 = 5
   \]  
   Now, there are **5 trees** remaining after the typhoon.  
3. Add the number of new trees Haley grew:  
   \[
   5 + 5 = 10
   \]  
   After growing 5 more trees, Haley now has **10 trees**.  

#### **Method 2: Combine operations into a single equation**
1. Write the equation: \( 9 - 4 + 5 \).  
2. Perform subtraction first (following the order of operations):  
   \[
   9 - 4 = 5
   \]  
3. Add the result to the number of new trees:  
   \[
   5 + 5 = 10
   \]  
   Haley has **10 trees**.

- **Method 2** is more efficient and concise, combining all operations into a single equation. However, it requires careful attention to the order of operations, which could lead to mistakes if not followed correctly.

3. Add \( 5 \) new trees: \( 5 + 5 = 10 \).

   Depth = 15 × Dean = 15 × 17 = 255 feet.  
   (Check: Multiplication is straightforward, and the result seems reasonable given the problem's context.)

1. Express Dean's height directly:  
   Dean = Ron + 4 = 13 + 4 = 17 feet.  
   (Check: This matches the calculation from Method 1.)

2. Substitute Dean's height into the formula for depth:  
   Depth = 15 × (Ron + 4) = 15 × 17 = 255 feet.

- Both methods are reliable and yield the same result, but **Method 1** is easier to follow for most people.

Q: There were 100 dollars in Olivia's wallet. She collected 148 more dollars from an atm. After she visited a supermarket there were 159 dollars left. How much did she spend?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Olivia started with **100 dollars** in her wallet.
- She collected an additional **148 dollars** from an ATM, bringing her total money to 100 + 148 = **248 dollars**.
- After visiting a supermarket, she had **159 dollars** left.
- We are tasked with finding how much money Olivia spent at the supermarket.
- Assumptions:
  - No money was lost, stolen, or used for any other purpose besides spending at the supermarket.
  - All amounts are in dollars, and there are no hidden fees or transactions.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Subtraction of remaining money from the total money before spending.**
- Calculate the total money Olivia had after collecting from the ATM: 100 + 148 = 248.
- Subtract the amount left after shopping (159) from the total money (248): 248 - 159.

**Method 2: Reverse calculation using addition.**
- Start with the amount Olivia has left after shopping (159).
- Add the amount she spent to this to get back to her total money before shopping (248).
- Solve for the amount spent by finding the difference between 248 and 159.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Calculate the total money Olivia had before shopping:
   \[
   100 + 148 = 248
   \]
2. Subtract the amount left after shopping from the total:
   \[
   248 - 159 = 89
   \]
3. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 248 - 159 = (248 - 150) - 9 = 98 - 9 = 89 \).
4. Result: Olivia spent **89 dollars**.

#### **Method 2: Reverse calculation**
1. Start with the amount Olivia has left after shopping:
   \[
   159
   \]
2. Add the amount she spent to this to get back to her total money before shopping:
   \[
   159 + \text{(amount spent)} = 248
   \]
3. Solve for the amount spent:
   \[
   \text{Amount spent} = 248 - 159 = 89
   \]
4. Double-check the subtraction:
   - Break it into smaller steps: \( 248 - 159 = (248 - 150) - 9 = 98 - 9 = 89 \).
5. Result: Olivia spent **89 dollars**.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it involves a straightforward subtraction of the remaining money from the total money before shopping.
- **Method 2** is slightly more roundabout, as it involves setting up an equation and solving for the unknown, which is unnecessary for this problem.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is clearer and less prone to error.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Calculate the total money Olivia had before shopping:
   \[
   100 + 148 = 248
   \]
2. Subtract the amount left after shopping:
   \[
   248 - 159 = 89
   \]
3. Double-check the subtraction:
   - \( 248 - 159 = (248 - 150) - 9 = 98 - 9 = 89 \).

Result: Olivia spent **89 dollars**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Verify the logic: If Olivia started with 248 dollars and has 159 dollars left, the difference (89 dollars) must be the amount she spent.
- Recalculate using both methods: Both yield the same result of 89 dollars.
- The result is reasonable given the context of the problem.
