A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- Edward spent $6 on books and $16 on pens.  
- After spending this money, he has $19 left.  
- We assume Edward did not receive or lose any additional money during this transaction.  
- The goal is to determine how much money Edward had before spending.

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Add the amount Edward spent ($6 + $16) to the amount he has left ($19). This will give the total amount he started with.  
**Method 2:** Represent Edward's initial amount as \( x \). Subtract the money he spent ($6 + $16) from \( x \), and set the result equal to $19. Solve for \( x \).

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total amount Edward spent:  
   \[
   6 + 16 = 22
   \]  
2. Add the amount Edward has left to the total spent:  
   \[
   22 + 19 = 41
   \]  
3. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Edward spent $22 and has $19 left. Adding these together gives $41, which matches the logic of the problem.  

**Potential pitfalls:** This method is straightforward, but errors could arise if the addition is performed incorrectly or if the problem is misinterpreted.

---

**Method 2:**  
1. Represent Edward's initial amount as \( x \). Subtract the money he spent ($6 + $16) from \( x \), and set the result equal to $19:  
   \[
   x - (6 + 16) = 19
   \]  
2. Simplify the equation:  
   \[
   x - 22 = 19
   \]  
3. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 19 + 22 = 41
   \]  
4. Double-check the calculation:  
   - Subtract $22 from $41: \( 41 - 22 = 19 \), which matches the problem statement.  

**Potential pitfalls:** This method requires setting up and solving an equation, which could lead to errors if the equation is miswritten or solved incorrectly.

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive, as it simply involves adding the amounts spent and remaining. It is less prone to errors and requires fewer steps.  
- **Method 2** is slightly more complex, as it involves setting up and solving an equation. While it is mathematically rigorous, it introduces more opportunities for mistakes in the setup or solution process.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution:  
  \[
  6 + 16 = 22
  \]  
  \[
  22 + 19 = 41
  \]  

---

Q: Baker made 13 cakes. He sold 91 of them and bought 154 new cakes. How many more cakes did baker buy than those he sold?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The baker made 13 cakes, but this information is irrelevant to the problem since it does not affect the comparison between cakes sold and cakes bought.
- The baker sold 91 cakes.
- The baker bought 154 cakes.
- The question asks how many more cakes the baker bought than those he sold.
- We assume no cakes were lost, discarded, or otherwise unaccounted for.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of cakes sold from the number of cakes bought: \( 154 - 91 \). This directly calculates the difference between the two quantities.

**Method 2:** Use a step-by-step comparison. Start with the number of cakes sold (91), then incrementally add cakes until reaching the number of cakes bought (154). Count the difference as the number of additional cakes bought.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
1. Start with the number of cakes bought: \( 154 \).
2. Subtract the number of cakes sold: \( 154 - 91 \).
3. Perform the subtraction:
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 4 - 1 = 3 \).
   - Subtract the tens place: \( 15 - 9 = 6 \) (after borrowing from the hundreds place).
4. Result: \( 154 - 91 = 63 \).

**Check:** Add the result (63) to the number of cakes sold (91) to ensure it equals the number of cakes bought (154):
- \( 91 + 63 = 154 \), which is correct.

#### Method 2: Incremental comparison
1. Start with the number of cakes sold: \( 91 \).
2. Incrementally add cakes until reaching the number of cakes bought (154):
   - From 91 to 100: \( 100 - 91 = 9 \).
   - From 100 to 154: \( 154 - 100 = 54 \).
   - Total difference: \( 9 + 54 = 63 \).
3. Result: The baker bought 63 more cakes than he sold.

**Check:** Add the result (63) to the number of cakes sold (91) to ensure it equals the number of cakes bought (154):
- \( 91 + 63 = 154 \), which is correct.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single subtraction operation. It is less prone to error and requires fewer steps.
- **Method 2** is more detailed and may be helpful for those who prefer breaking the problem into smaller parts, but it is less efficient and introduces more opportunities for mistakes in intermediate steps.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and reliability.
- Solution:
  - \( 154 - 91 = 63 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the result (63) to the number of cakes sold (91) to ensure it equals the number of cakes bought (154):
  - \( 91 + 63 = 154 \), which is correct.
- The result is reasonable, as the baker bought significantly more cakes than he sold.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **63**.

- The depth of the water is 16 times Ron's height.
- Dean is 9 feet taller than Ron.
- We are tasked with finding the depth of the water.
- Assumptions:
  - The relationship between the water depth and Ron's height is exact (no rounding or approximations).
  - Dean's height is not directly relevant to the calculation of the water depth but is additional information.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Directly calculate the water depth by multiplying Ron's height by 16. This method uses the given relationship between Ron's height and the water depth.

**Method 2:**  
Break the problem into smaller steps:
1. First, calculate Dean's height (Ron’s height + 9 feet) to confirm it is not relevant to the water depth.

   \[

   - \( 16 \times 10 = 160 \),
   - \( 16 \times 3 = 48 \),

   \[
   \text{Dean's height} = \text{Ron’s height} + 9 = 13 + 9 = 22 \, \text{feet}.
   \]
   - Dean's height is not directly relevant to the water depth, so we proceed to the next step.

   \[

   \]
3. Check: Multiplying 16 by 13:
   - \( 16 \times 10 = 160 \),
   - \( 16 \times 3 = 48 \),
   - \( 160 + 48 = 208 \).

- **Method 2** is more thorough, as it considers all given information, but calculating Dean's height is irrelevant to the problem and adds unnecessary complexity.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

- Using Method 1:
  \[
  \text{Water depth} = 16 \times 13 = 208 \, \text{feet}.
  \]

---

  - \( 16 \times 10 = 160 \),
  - \( 16 \times 3 = 48 \),

Q: There are 87 oranges and 290 bananas in Philip's collection. If the bananas are organized into 2 groups and oranges are organized into 93 groups How big is each group of bananas?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Philip has **87 oranges** and **290 bananas** in his collection.
- The bananas are organized into **2 groups**.
- The oranges are organized into **93 groups** (this information is irrelevant to solving the problem about bananas, but it is noted for completeness).
- We assume the bananas are divided evenly into the 2 groups, meaning each group has the same number of bananas.
- We assume there are no leftover bananas after dividing them into groups.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- To find the size of each group of bananas, divide the total number of bananas (290) by the number of groups (2). This is a straightforward division problem.

**Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**  
- Subtract the number of bananas in one group repeatedly from the total until no bananas remain. Count the number of subtractions to determine the size of each group.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Total bananas = 290.
2. Number of groups = 2.
3. Divide the total bananas by the number of groups:  
   \[
   290 \div 2 = 145
   \]
4. Check the result: Multiply the size of one group by the number of groups to ensure it equals the total:  
   \[
   145 \times 2 = 290
   \]
5. The calculation checks out, so each group contains **145 bananas**.

#### **Method 2: Repeated Subtraction Approach**
1. Start with the total number of bananas: 290.
2. Subtract the size of one group (145 bananas) repeatedly until no bananas remain:  
   - First subtraction: \( 290 - 145 = 145 \)  
   - Second subtraction: \( 145 - 145 = 0 \)
3. Count the number of subtractions: There were 2 subtractions, meaning the bananas were divided into 2 groups of **145 bananas** each.
4. Check the result: Add the sizes of the groups to ensure they equal the total:  
   \[
   145 + 145 = 290
   \]
5. The calculation checks out, so each group contains **145 bananas**.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is more efficient and straightforward, as it involves a single division operation and a quick verification step. It is less prone to errors and is the preferred method for this type of problem.
- **Method 2 (Repeated Subtraction Approach)** is less efficient, as it requires multiple steps and is more time-consuming. However, it can be useful for conceptual understanding or when division is not immediately intuitive.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1 (Division Approach)** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:  
   \[
   290 \div 2 = 145
   \]
- Verification:  
   \[
   145 \times 2 = 290
   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The total number of bananas (290) is evenly divisible by the number of groups (2), so the result of 145 bananas per group is reasonable and correct.
- Verification steps confirm the accuracy of the solution.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **145**.

Q: Mom buys 51 white t - shirts. If white t - shirts can be purchased in packages of 3 How many packages will she have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mom buys **51 white t-shirts**.
- White t-shirts are sold in **packages of 3**.
- We are tasked with determining how many **packages** of 3 t-shirts are needed to account for all 51 t-shirts.
- Assumptions:
  - All t-shirts are packaged in groups of 3.
  - There are no leftover t-shirts outside of the packages.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1: Division with no remainder.**
- Divide the total number of t-shirts (51) by the number of t-shirts per package (3).
- The result of the division will give the number of packages.

**Method 2: Repeated subtraction.**
- Start with the total number of t-shirts (51) and repeatedly subtract 3 (the size of one package) until the total reaches 0.
- Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of packages.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division with no remainder**
1. Divide the total number of t-shirts by the number of t-shirts per package:
   \[
   51 \div 3 = 17
   \]
2. This means there are exactly 17 packages, with no remainder.
3. Check the calculation:
   - Multiply the number of packages (17) by the number of t-shirts per package (3):
     \[
     17 \times 3 = 51
     \]
   - The result matches the original total, confirming the calculation is correct.

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
1. Start with 51 t-shirts and subtract 3 repeatedly:
   - 51 - 3 = 48 (1 package)
   - 48 - 3 = 45 (2 packages)
   - 45 - 3 = 42 (3 packages)
   - 42 - 3 = 39 (4 packages)
   - 39 - 3 = 36 (5 packages)
   - 36 - 3 = 33 (6 packages)
   - 33 - 3 = 30 (7 packages)
   - 30 - 3 = 27 (8 packages)
   - 27 - 3 = 24 (9 packages)
   - 24 - 3 = 21 (10 packages)
   - 21 - 3 = 18 (11 packages)
   - 18 - 3 = 15 (12 packages)
   - 15 - 3 = 12 (13 packages)
   - 12 - 3 = 9 (14 packages)
   - 9 - 3 = 6 (15 packages)
   - 6 - 3 = 3 (16 packages)
   - 3 - 3 = 0 (17 packages)
2. Count the number of subtractions: 17 subtractions, so there are 17 packages.
3. Check the calculation:
   - Add up the total number of t-shirts from 17 packages:
     \[
     17 \times 3 = 51
     \]
   - The result matches the original total, confirming the calculation is correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1 (Division)** is more efficient and straightforward. It involves a single division operation and a quick verification step. This method is less prone to errors and is faster to execute.
- **Method 2 (Repeated subtraction)** is less efficient, as it requires multiple steps and is more time-consuming. While it works, it is not practical for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

- **Method 1 (Division)** is the better method.
- Solve using division:
  \[
  51 \div 3 = 17
  \]
- Verify:
  \[
  17 \times 3 = 51
  \]
- The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- The division result (17) is a whole number, meaning there are no leftover t-shirts.
- The verification step confirms the total number of t-shirts matches the original amount (51).
- The solution is reasonable and error-free.

---

The answer is **17**.

- Assume no cookies were lost, shared, or added during the process.  

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
Method 1: Subtract the number of sweet cookies Paco ate from the total number of sweet cookies he started with: 22 - 15.  
Method 2: Use a step-by-step breakdown: Start with 22 sweet cookies, remove 15 one at a time, and count how many remain.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
- Start with the total number of sweet cookies: 22.  
- Subtract the number of sweet cookies Paco ate: 22 - 15.  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  - Break it down: 22 - 15 = (22 - 10) - 5 = 12 - 5 = 7.  
- Result: Paco has 7 sweet cookies left.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with 22 sweet cookies.  
- Remove 15 cookies one at a time:  
  - After removing 1 cookie: 22 - 1 = 21.  
  - After removing 2 cookies: 21 - 1 = 20.  
  - Continue this process until 15 cookies are removed.  
  - After removing 15 cookies: 22 - 15 = 7.  
- Result: Paco has 7 sweet cookies left.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 2** is reliable but overly tedious, especially for larger numbers, and introduces more opportunities for error due to repetitive calculations.

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Using Method 1:  
  - Start with 22 sweet cookies.  
  - Subtract the 15 sweet cookies Paco ate: 22 - 15 = 7.  
- Solution: Paco has 7 sweet cookies left.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Recalculate: 22 - 15 = 7.

A_model:
Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.  
- The number of ripe orange sacks harvested per day is 5.  
- The number of unripe orange sacks harvested per day is 74.  
- We are tasked with finding how many more sacks of unripe oranges are harvested than ripe oranges.  
- Assume all sacks are counted accurately, and no sacks are lost or miscounted.  

---

Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.  
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of ripe orange sacks from the number of unripe orange sacks: \( 74 - 5 \). This directly calculates the difference.  
**Method 2:** Use a comparison approach: Start with the number of ripe orange sacks (5) and incrementally count up to the number of unripe orange sacks (74), keeping track of the difference.  

---

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.  

**Method 1:**  
1. Write the subtraction equation: \( 74 - 5 \).  
2. Perform the subtraction:  
   - Start with the tens place: \( 7 - 0 = 7 \).  
   - Subtract the ones place: \( 4 - 5 \). Since 4 is smaller than 5, borrow 1 from the tens place:  
     - \( 14 - 5 = 9 \).  
   - Combine results: \( 74 - 5 = 69 \).  
3. Double-check the subtraction: Add the result (69) to the smaller number (5) to confirm it equals the larger number (74):  
   - \( 69 + 5 = 74 \).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Start with the number of ripe orange sacks: \( 5 \).  
2. Incrementally count up to the number of unripe orange sacks (74), keeping track of the difference:  
   - \( 5 + 1 = 6 \), \( 6 + 1 = 7 \), ..., \( 73 + 1 = 74 \).  
   - Count the total increments: \( 74 - 5 = 69 \).  
3. Verify the result by comparing the difference: \( 74 - 5 = 69 \).  

Potential pitfalls:  
- Method 1 is straightforward but requires careful subtraction to avoid errors.  
- Method 2 is tedious and prone to counting mistakes, especially for large differences.  

---

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- **Method 1** is more efficient and reliable because it directly calculates the difference using subtraction. It avoids unnecessary steps and minimizes the risk of errors.  
- **Method 2** is less practical for large differences, as it involves repetitive counting and is time-consuming.  

---

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- **Method 1** is the better choice.  
1. Subtract the number of ripe orange sacks from the number of unripe orange sacks: \( 74 - 5 \).  
2. Perform the subtraction: \( 74 - 5 = 69 \).  
3. Double-check the result: \( 69 + 5 = 74 \).  

---

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- The subtraction was performed correctly, and the result was verified by addition.  
- The result is reasonable because the number of unripe orange sacks (74) is much larger than the number of ripe orange sacks (5).

Q: Nell collects baseball cards. She had 242 cards. She gave 136 of her cards to Jeff. How many cards does Nell have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Nell started with **242 baseball cards**.
- She gave **136 cards** to Jeff.
- We are tasked with finding how many cards Nell has left.
- Assumptions:
  - No cards were lost, damaged, or added during the transaction.
  - The subtraction operation is straightforward, with no hidden complexities.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction**  
- Subtract the number of cards Nell gave to Jeff from the total number of cards she started with: \( 242 - 136 \).  
- This method is straightforward and efficient.

**Method 2: Break the subtraction into parts**  
- Decompose the subtraction into smaller, easier steps:
  1. Subtract 100 from 242 to get a simpler intermediate result.
  2. Subtract the remaining 36 from the intermediate result.
- This method is useful for mental math or when working without a calculator.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Write the subtraction vertically for clarity:
   ```
     242
   - 136
   -----
   ```
2. Start with the ones place:
   - \( 2 - 6 \): Since 2 is smaller than 6, borrow 1 from the tens place. This makes the 2 a 12 and reduces the 4 in the tens place to 3.
   - \( 12 - 6 = 6 \).

3. Move to the tens place:
   - \( 3 - 3 = 0 \).

4. Move to the hundreds place:
   - \( 2 - 1 = 1 \).

5. Combine the results: \( 242 - 136 = 106 \).

**Check for errors:** Add the result (106) to the number of cards given to Jeff (136) to ensure it equals the starting total:
   - \( 106 + 136 = 242 \). The calculation checks out.

---

#### **Method 2: Break the subtraction into parts**
1. Start with the total number of cards: \( 242 \).
2. Subtract 100 first:
   - \( 242 - 100 = 142 \).
3. Subtract the remaining 36:
   - \( 142 - 36 \):
     - Subtract the ones place: \( 2 - 6 \): Borrow 1 from the tens place, making it \( 12 - 6 = 6 \).
     - Subtract the tens place: \( 3 - 3 = 0 \).
     - Subtract the hundreds place: \( 1 - 0 = 1 \).
   - Result: \( 142 - 36 = 106 \).

**Check for errors:** Add the result (106) to the number of cards given to Jeff (136) to ensure it equals the starting total:
   - \( 106 + 136 = 242 \). The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is more efficient and straightforward, especially when working with paper or a calculator. It minimizes intermediate steps and reduces the chance of error.
- **Method 2 (Breaking into parts)** is useful for mental math or when working without tools, as it simplifies the subtraction into smaller, manageable steps. However, it introduces more intermediate steps, which can increase the chance of error.

For this problem, **Method 1** is the better choice due to its simplicity and directness.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
Using **Method 1 (Direct subtraction):**
1. Subtract \( 136 \) from \( 242 \):
   - \( 242 - 136 = 106 \).
2. Double-check the result:
   - \( 106 + 136 = 242 \). The calculation is correct.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- The result \( 106 \) is reasonable because it is less than the starting total (\( 242 \)) and greater than zero, as expected.
- Both methods confirm the same result, and the double-check verifies the accuracy.

---

**The answer is 106.**

Q: Bobby had 19 pieces of candy. He ate 2 pieces of candy. How many pieces of candy does he still have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Bobby started with **19 pieces of candy**.
- Bobby ate **2 pieces of candy**.
- We are tasked with finding how many pieces of candy Bobby has left.
- Assumptions:
  - No additional candy was added or removed during this process.
  - The subtraction operation is straightforward, and no external factors (e.g., sharing candy) are involved.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Direct subtraction.**
- Subtract the number of pieces Bobby ate (2) from the total number of pieces he started with (19): \( 19 - 2 \).
- This method is straightforward and involves a single arithmetic operation.

**Method 2: Counting backward.**
- Start with the total number of pieces (19) and count backward by 2 to determine how many are left.
- This method is useful for visualizing the problem or for situations where subtraction might be less intuitive.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of pieces: \( 19 \).
2. Subtract the number of pieces Bobby ate: \( 19 - 2 \).
   - Perform the subtraction: \( 19 - 2 = 17 \).
3. Double-check the calculation:
   - Add the result (17) to the number of pieces Bobby ate (2): \( 17 + 2 = 19 \), confirming the calculation is correct.
4. Potential pitfalls:
   - Misreading the problem or performing the subtraction incorrectly, though this is unlikely given the simplicity of the numbers.

#### **Method 2: Counting backward**
1. Start with the total number of pieces: \( 19 \).
2. Count backward by 1 step at a time:
   - From 19, subtract 1: \( 19 - 1 = 18 \).
   - From 18, subtract 1: \( 18 - 1 = 17 \).
3. The final number after counting backward by 2 steps is \( 17 \).
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Count forward from the result (17) by 2 steps: \( 17 + 1 = 18 \), \( 18 + 1 = 19 \), confirming the calculation is correct.
5. Potential pitfalls:
   - Losing track of the steps while counting backward, especially with larger numbers.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is more efficient and reliable because it involves a single arithmetic operation. It is less prone to human error and is faster to execute.
- **Method 2 (Counting backward)** is more intuitive for those who prefer a step-by-step approach or for visualizing the problem. However, it is slower and more prone to errors, especially with larger numbers or multiple steps.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1 (Direct subtraction)** is the better method due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solve using Method 1:
  - \( 19 - 2 = 17 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the result (17) to the number of pieces Bobby ate (2): \( 17 + 2 = 19 \), confirming the calculation is correct.
- The result is reasonable because Bobby started with 19 pieces and ate only 2, leaving him with most of his candy.

A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jack received a total of 9 emails during the day.
- He received 7 emails in the morning.
- The number of emails received in the afternoon is unknown.
- Assumption: No emails were deleted, and the total number of emails (9) includes both morning and afternoon emails.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Subtract the number of emails received in the morning from the total number of emails. This will directly give the number of emails received in the afternoon: \( \text{Total emails} - \text{Morning emails} = \text{Afternoon emails} \).

**Method 2:** Use a trial-and-error approach. Start with the total number of emails (9) and subtract different values for the morning emails until the remainder matches the afternoon emails. This method is less efficient but can be used to verify the result.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of emails: 9.
2. Subtract the number of emails received in the morning: \( 9 - 7 = 2 \).
3. The result, 2, represents the number of emails received in the afternoon.

**Check:** Add the morning emails (7) and the afternoon emails (2) to ensure the total matches the given total: \( 7 + 2 = 9 \). The calculation checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method assumes the total number of emails (9) is accurate and includes all emails received during the day.

#### **Method 2: Trial-and-error**
1. Start with the total number of emails: 9.
2. Assume Jack received 7 emails in the morning. Subtract this from the total: \( 9 - 7 = 2 \).
3. Verify the result by adding the morning emails (7) and the afternoon emails (2): \( 7 + 2 = 9 \). The total matches, so the solution is correct.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method is less efficient and involves unnecessary steps, but it can be useful for verifying the result.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct, efficient, and reliable. It involves a single subtraction and a quick verification step.
- **Method 2** is less efficient and unnecessarily repetitive, as it relies on trial-and-error. However, it can be useful for double-checking the result.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and clarity.
- Solution: \( 9 - 7 = 2 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Add the morning emails (7) and the afternoon emails (2): \( 7 + 2 = 9 \). The total matches the given total, confirming the solution.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.

Q: Last week Fred had 114 dollars and Jason had 22 dollars. They washed cars over the weekend and now Fred has 21 dollars and Jason has 78 dollars. How much money did Jason make over the weekend?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Fred started with **114 dollars** and now has **21 dollars**.  
- Jason started with **22 dollars** and now has **78 dollars**.  
- Both Fred and Jason washed cars over the weekend, and we assume no other sources of income or expenses.  
- The goal is to determine how much money Jason **made** over the weekend.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- Calculate how much money Jason has now compared to what he started with.  
- Subtract Jason's starting amount from his current amount: \( \text{Money made} = \text{Current amount} - \text{Starting amount} \).  

**Method 2:**  
- Calculate the total money exchanged between Fred and Jason.  
- Determine how much money Fred lost and assume that this amount was transferred to Jason.  
- Subtract Fred's current amount from his starting amount to find the money he lost, which equals the money Jason gained.  

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation of Jason's earnings**  
1. Jason started with **22 dollars**.  
2. Jason now has **78 dollars**.  
3. Subtract the starting amount from the current amount:  
   \[
   78 - 22 = 56
   \]  
4. Jason made **56 dollars** over the weekend.  

**Check:**  
- Add Jason's starting amount to the money he made:  
  \[
  22 + 56 = 78
  \]  
- This matches Jason's current total, so the calculation is correct.  

#### **Method 2: Calculate money lost by Fred and assume it was gained by Jason**  
1. Fred started with **114 dollars**.  
2. Fred now has **21 dollars**.  
3. Subtract Fred's current amount from his starting amount to find how much he lost:  
   \[
   114 - 21 = 93
   \]  
4. Jason started with **22 dollars** and now has **78 dollars**. Subtract Jason's starting amount from his current amount:  
   \[
   78 - 22 = 56
   \]  
5. Compare Fred's loss (**93 dollars**) to Jason's gain (**56 dollars**).  
   - The numbers do not match, which suggests that not all of Fred's lost money went to Jason.  
   - This method introduces ambiguity because it assumes all money lost by Fred was gained by Jason, which is not explicitly stated in the problem.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more direct and reliable because it focuses solely on Jason's earnings, which is the question being asked.  
- **Method 2** introduces unnecessary complexity by involving Fred's transactions, which are not directly relevant to calculating Jason's earnings. Additionally, it assumes all of Fred's lost money went to Jason, which is not guaranteed.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is the better method due to its simplicity and clarity.  
- Using Method 1:  
  \[
  78 - 22 = 56
  \]  
- Jason made **56 dollars** over the weekend.  

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Jason started with **22 dollars** and made **56 dollars**, so his total is:  
  \[
  22 + 56 = 78
  \]  
- This matches Jason's current total, confirming the calculation is correct.  

### Final Answer:
The answer is **56**.

- She also bought some packs of cake.

Method 2: Use trial-and-error reasoning: Find what number added to 23 results in 27.  

Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

- Start with the total number of grocery packs: 27.  
- Subtract the number of cookie packs: 27 - 23.  
- Perform the subtraction:  
  - Start with the ones place: 7 - 3 = 4.  
  - Move to the tens place: 2 - 2 = 0.  
- Result: 27 - 23 = 4.  
- Double-check: Add the result (4 packs of cake) back to the cookie packs: 23 + 4 = 27. This confirms the calculation is correct.  

**Method 2:**  
- Start with the equation: 23 + x = 27, where x represents the number of cake packs.  
- Solve for x:  
  - Subtract 23 from both sides: x = 27 - 23.  
  - Perform the subtraction:  
    - Start with the ones place: 7 - 3 = 4.  
    - Move to the tens place: 2 - 2 = 0.  
  - Result: x = 4.  
- Double-check: Add the result (4 packs of cake) back to the cookie packs: 23 + 4 = 27. This confirms the calculation is correct.  

Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.  
- Both methods are straightforward and yield the same result.  
- Method 1 is slightly more direct and efficient because it involves a single subtraction operation.  
- Method 2 introduces an equation, which may be helpful for more complex problems but is unnecessary for this simple subtraction scenario.  

Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.  
- Method 1 is better due to its simplicity and efficiency.  
- Solution: 27 - 23 = 4.  

Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.  
- Add the result (4 packs of cake) back to the cookie packs: 23 + 4 = 27. This confirms the calculation is correct.

Q: Jerry had 4 action figures and 22 books on a shelf in his room. Later he added 6 more action figures to the shelf. How many action figures were on his shelf in all?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Jerry initially had **4 action figures** on his shelf.
- He later added **6 more action figures** to the shelf.
- The problem asks for the **total number of action figures** on the shelf after the addition.
- Assumptions:
  - No action figures were removed or lost during this process.
  - The books on the shelf (22) are irrelevant to the calculation.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct addition. Add the initial number of action figures (4) to the number of action figures added later (6): \( 4 + 6 \).

**Method 2:** Break the problem into smaller steps. First, calculate the total by grouping the numbers into parts (e.g., \( 4 + 6 = (4 + 4) + 2 \)) to simplify mental math.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
1. Start with the initial number of action figures: \( 4 \).
2. Add the number of action figures added later: \( 4 + 6 \).
3. Perform the addition: \( 4 + 6 = 10 \).
4. Double-check the calculation:
   - Break it into smaller parts: \( 4 + 6 = (4 + 4) + 2 = 8 + 2 = 10 \).
   - The result is consistent.

**Potential pitfalls:** None, as this is a straightforward addition problem.

---

#### **Method 2: Breaking into smaller steps**
1. Start with the initial number of action figures: \( 4 \).
2. Break the added number (6) into smaller parts for easier mental math: \( 6 = 4 + 2 \).
3. Add the first part: \( 4 + 4 = 8 \).
4. Add the second part: \( 8 + 2 = 10 \).
5. Double-check the calculation:
   - Combine the steps: \( 4 + 6 = 10 \).
   - The result is consistent.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method introduces more steps, which could lead to errors if not carefully tracked.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct, efficient, and less prone to error because it involves a single calculation.
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math or when working with larger numbers, but it introduces unnecessary complexity for this problem.
- **Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better choice for this problem due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

**Method 2:** Calculate the total number of peaches in the basket first (\(6 + 90 + 16\)), then subtract the number of yellow peaches (\(90\)) to isolate the red and green peaches.

   \[
   6 + 90 + 16 = 112
   \]

   \[

   \]

   - Verify the total number of peaches: \(6 + 90 = 96\), then \(96 + 16 = 112\).  
   - Confirm subtraction: \(112 - 90 = 22\).

- **Method 2** involves extra steps (calculating the total and subtracting yellow peaches), which increases complexity and the chance of error.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is simpler and more reliable for this problem.

   \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

  - All roses added to the vase came from the garden.

**Method 2: Reverse Calculation**

- Ensure the subtraction is correct: \( 20 - 7 = 13 \) is accurate.

Q: Carol was sending out birthday invitations to her friends. Each package of invitations she bought had 3 invitations in it and she bought 2 packs. If she wants to invite 9 friends How many extra invitations will she need to buy?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Carol is sending out birthday invitations to 9 friends.  
- Each package of invitations contains 3 invitations.  
- Carol initially bought 2 packs of invitations.  
- We assume all invitations are usable, and no extras are wasted.  
- Carol needs exactly 9 invitations, no more and no less.  

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the total number of invitations Carol currently has by multiplying the number of packs by the number of invitations per pack. Subtract this total from the number of invitations she needs to determine how many more invitations she must buy.

**Method 2:**  
Divide the total number of invitations Carol needs (9) by the number of invitations per pack (3) to determine the total number of packs required. Subtract the number of packs she already bought (2) to find how many additional packs she needs.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction of invitations.**
1. Calculate the total number of invitations Carol currently has:  
   \[
   \text{Current invitations} = \text{Number of packs} \times \text{Invitations per pack} = 2 \times 3 = 6
   \]
2. Subtract the number of invitations Carol currently has from the number she needs:  
   \[
   \text{Extra invitations needed} = \text{Total needed} - \text{Current invitations} = 9 - 6 = 3
   \]
3. Verify the calculation:  
   - Carol has 6 invitations and needs 9. She is short by 3 invitations.  

#### **Method 2: Calculate the total number of packs required.**
1. Divide the total number of invitations Carol needs by the number of invitations per pack to determine the total number of packs required:  
   \[
   \text{Total packs needed} = \frac{\text{Total invitations needed}}{\text{Invitations per pack}} = \frac{9}{3} = 3
   \]
2. Subtract the number of packs Carol already bought to find how many additional packs she needs:  
   \[
   \text{Extra packs needed} = \text{Total packs needed} - \text{Packs already bought} = 3 - 2 = 1
   \]
3. Calculate the number of invitations in the extra pack:  
   \[
   \text{Extra invitations needed} = \text{Extra packs needed} \times \text{Invitations per pack} = 1 \times 3 = 3
   \]
4. Verify the calculation:  
   - Carol needs 3 invitations, which corresponds to 1 additional pack.  

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is more direct and intuitive, as it focuses on the total number of invitations Carol currently has and subtracts from the total needed. It avoids intermediate steps and is less prone to errors.  
- **Method 2** involves more steps, including division and multiplication, which may introduce unnecessary complexity. However, it is useful for understanding the problem in terms of packs rather than individual invitations.  

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is better due to its simplicity and directness.  
- Solution:  
  \[
  \text{Current invitations} = 2 \times 3 = 6  
  \text{Extra invitations needed} = 9 - 6 = 3
  \]

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Carol has 6 invitations and needs 9. Subtracting gives 3 extra invitations needed.  
- If Carol buys 1 additional pack (3 invitations), she will have exactly 9 invitations, which matches the requirement.  

The answer is **3**.

3.0
A:
3.0

Q: The ring toss game at the carnival made 120 dollars in the first 20 days and 66 dollars in the remaining 16 days. How much did they make per day in the first 20 days at the carnival?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- The ring toss game made $120 in the first 20 days.
- We are tasked with finding how much money was made per day during the first 20 days.
- Assume the earnings were evenly distributed across the 20 days (i.e., the same amount was made each day).
- No additional information about fluctuations in daily earnings is provided.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:**  
Divide the total earnings ($120) by the number of days (20). This method uses the formula for average earnings per day:  
\[
\text{Average earnings per day} = \frac{\text{Total earnings}}{\text{Number of days}}
\]

**Method 2:**  
Break the total earnings into smaller chunks (e.g., $100 and $20), calculate the daily earnings for each chunk, and then sum them. This method is useful for mental math or verifying the result.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Division**
1. Use the formula:  
   \[
   \text{Average earnings per day} = \frac{\text{Total earnings}}{\text{Number of days}}
   \]
   Substitute the values:  
   \[
   \text{Average earnings per day} = \frac{120}{20}
   \]
2. Perform the division:  
   \[
   120 \div 20 = 6
   \]
3. Double-check the calculation: Multiply the result (6) by the number of days (20) to ensure it equals the total earnings:  
   \[
   6 \times 20 = 120
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Chunking**
1. Break $120 into smaller chunks: $100 and $20.  
2. Calculate the daily earnings for each chunk:  
   - For $100 over 20 days:  
     \[
     \frac{100}{20} = 5 \, \text{dollars per day}
     \]
   - For $20 over 20 days:  
     \[
     \frac{20}{20} = 1 \, \text{dollar per day}
     \]
3. Add the daily earnings from both chunks:  
   \[
   5 + 1 = 6 \, \text{dollars per day}
   \]
4. Double-check the calculation: Multiply the result (6) by the number of days (20) to ensure it equals the total earnings:  
   \[
   6 \times 20 = 120
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves a single division operation. It is less prone to errors and requires fewer steps.  
- **Method 2** is useful for mental math or breaking down complex numbers, but it introduces more intermediate steps, which can increase the risk of errors.  

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is better for this problem due to its simplicity and reliability.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:  
\[
\text{Average earnings per day} = \frac{120}{20} = 6
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Multiply the result (6) by the number of days (20):  
  \[
  6 \times 20 = 120
  \]
  The calculation checks out.  
- The result is reasonable because $120 spread evenly over 20 days would logically result in $6 per day.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **6 dollars per day**.

### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Ed has 27 marbles.
- Ed has 5 more marbles than Doug initially.
- Doug lost 3 marbles at the playground, but this does not affect the calculation of how many marbles Doug had initially.
- We are tasked with finding how many marbles Doug had initially, before losing any.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use subtraction to find Doug's initial number of marbles. Since Ed has 5 more marbles than Doug, subtract 5 from Ed's total to determine Doug's initial count.  
**Method 2:** Use algebra to represent the relationship between Ed's and Doug's marbles. Let Doug's initial number of marbles be \( x \). Then, Ed's marbles are \( x + 5 \). Solve for \( x \) using Ed's total of 27 marbles.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Subtraction**
1. Ed has 27 marbles, and he has 5 more marbles than Doug.
2. Subtract 5 from Ed's total to find Doug's initial number of marbles:  
   \[
   27 - 5 = 22
   \]
3. Doug initially had 22 marbles.  
4. Check: If Doug had 22 marbles initially, and Ed had 5 more, then Ed would have \( 22 + 5 = 27 \), which matches the given information.

#### **Method 2: Algebra**
1. Let Doug's initial number of marbles be \( x \).  
2. Since Ed has 5 more marbles than Doug, Ed's marbles can be expressed as \( x + 5 \).  
3. Set up the equation based on the given information:  
   \[
   x + 5 = 27
   \]
4. Solve for \( x \):  
   \[
   x = 27 - 5 = 22
   \]
5. Doug initially had 22 marbles.  
6. Check: Substitute \( x = 22 \) back into the equation. If Ed has \( x + 5 = 22 + 5 = 27 \), the equation holds true.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and involves simple subtraction, making it efficient and easy to follow.  
- **Method 2** uses algebra, which is slightly more formal and generalizable but introduces unnecessary complexity for this specific problem.  
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is better for clarity and efficiency in this context.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- Using **Method 1**, subtract 5 from Ed's total:  
  \[
  27 - 5 = 22
  \]
- Doug initially had 22 marbles.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- If Doug initially had 22 marbles and Ed had 5 more, then Ed would have \( 22 + 5 = 27 \), which matches the given information.  
- The solution is consistent and reasonable.

Q: Mom buys 70 white t - shirts in total. If white t - shirts can be purchased in packages and mom buys 14 packages How many white t - shirts does each package have?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Mom buys a total of **70 white t-shirts**.
- The t-shirts are purchased in **14 packages**.
- We are tasked with finding how many t-shirts are in **each package**.
- Assumptions:
  - All packages contain the same number of t-shirts.
  - There are no extra or missing t-shirts.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1: Division Approach**  
- Since the total number of t-shirts is evenly distributed across 14 packages, divide the total number of t-shirts (70) by the number of packages (14). This will give the number of t-shirts per package.

**Method 2: Multiplication and Guess-and-Check**  
- Assume each package contains an unknown number of t-shirts, represented by \( x \). Multiply \( x \) by the number of packages (14) and set it equal to the total number of t-shirts (70). Solve for \( x \) using algebra or trial-and-error.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Division Approach**
1. Start with the total number of t-shirts: 70.
2. Divide this total by the number of packages:  
   \[
   \text{Number of t-shirts per package} = \frac{70}{14}
   \]
3. Perform the division:  
   \[
   \frac{70}{14} = 5
   \]
4. Check the result: Multiply the number of t-shirts per package (5) by the number of packages (14) to ensure it equals the total:  
   \[
   5 \times 14 = 70
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

#### **Method 2: Multiplication and Guess-and-Check**
1. Let \( x \) represent the number of t-shirts in each package.
2. Write the equation:  
   \[
   14 \times x = 70
   \]
3. Solve for \( x \) by dividing both sides of the equation by 14:  
   \[
   x = \frac{70}{14}
   \]
4. Perform the division:  
   \[
   x = 5
   \]
5. Check the result: Substitute \( x = 5 \) back into the equation:  
   \[
   14 \times 5 = 70
   \]
   The calculation checks out.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.

- **Method 2 (Multiplication and Guess-and-Check)** is more flexible and can be useful for more complex problems, but it introduces unnecessary complexity for this specific problem.

   \]

Q: There are 896 skittles in Steven's skittles collection. Steven also has 517 erasers and 90 scales. If the skittles are organized into 8 groups How big is each group?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Steven has 896 skittles in his collection.
- These skittles are to be organized into 8 equal groups.
- We assume all skittles are divisible evenly into the groups (no remainder).
- The erasers (517) and scales (90) are irrelevant to the problem, as they do not affect the division of skittles.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use direct division to calculate the size of each group: \( \text{Total skittles} \div \text{Number of groups} = \text{Size of each group} \).  
**Method 2:** Use repeated subtraction to divide the skittles into groups iteratively, subtracting the size of one group (896 ÷ 8) repeatedly until the total is exhausted.

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct Division**
1. Start with the total number of skittles: 896.
2. Divide by the number of groups: \( 896 \div 8 \).
3. Perform the division step-by-step:
   - 8 goes into 89 (the first two digits of 896) 11 times (since \( 8 \times 11 = 88 \)).
   - Subtract 88 from 89: \( 89 - 88 = 1 \).
   - Bring down the next digit (6), making it 16.
   - 8 goes into 16 exactly 2 times (since \( 8 \times 2 = 16 \)).
   - Subtract 16 from 16: \( 16 - 16 = 0 \).
4. The result is 112, meaning each group contains 112 skittles.

**Potential Pitfalls:** Division is straightforward, but errors can occur if calculations are rushed or misaligned.

#### **Method 2: Repeated Subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of skittles: 896.
2. Determine the size of one group: \( 896 \div 8 = 112 \) (calculated separately).
3. Subtract the size of one group (112) repeatedly until the total reaches zero:
   - \( 896 - 112 = 784 \)
   - \( 784 - 112 = 672 \)
   - \( 672 - 112 = 560 \)
   - \( 560 - 112 = 448 \)
   - \( 448 - 112 = 336 \)
   - \( 336 - 112 = 224 \)
   - \( 224 - 112 = 112 \)
   - \( 112 - 112 = 0 \)
4. Count the number of subtractions: 8 subtractions confirm that the skittles are evenly divided into 8 groups, each containing 112 skittles.

**Potential Pitfalls:** This method is more time-consuming and prone to errors in subtraction if not carefully tracked.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1 (Direct Division):** This method is faster, more efficient, and less prone to errors. It provides the answer directly without requiring iterative steps.
- **Method 2 (Repeated Subtraction):** While it confirms the division process step-by-step, it is slower and introduces more opportunities for calculation errors. It is less practical for larger numbers.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is clearly better due to its simplicity and efficiency.
- Solution:
  \( 896 \div 8 = 112 \)
  Each group contains 112 skittles.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Multiply the size of each group by the number of groups to verify:
  \( 112 \times 8 = 896 \), which matches the original total number of skittles.
- The calculation checks out, and the result is reasonable.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **112**.

Q: Winter is almost here and most animals are migrating to warmer countries. There were 89 bird families living near the mountain. If 60 bird families flew away for winter How many more bird families flew away for the winter than those that stayed behind?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- There were initially **89 bird families** living near the mountain.
- **60 bird families** flew away for the winter.
- The remaining bird families stayed behind.
- We are tasked with finding **how many more bird families flew away than stayed behind**.
- Assumptions:
  - No bird families were added or removed other than those that flew away.
  - The total number of bird families is conserved (89).

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:**  
- First, calculate how many bird families stayed behind by subtracting the number that flew away from the total: \( 89 - 60 \).  
- Then, find the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed behind: \( 60 - \text{(number that stayed behind)} \).

**Method 2:**  
- Directly calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and the number that stayed behind using the formula:  
  \[
  \text{Difference} = \text{(number that flew away)} - \text{(total families - number that flew away)}.
  \]  
- Simplify the formula to avoid intermediate steps.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind:  
   \[
   89 - 60 = 29.
   \]  
   So, 29 bird families stayed behind.

2. Calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed behind:  
   \[
   60 - 29 = 31.
   \]  

3. Double-check the calculations:  
   - Subtracting \( 89 - 60 \) gives \( 29 \), which is correct.  
   - Subtracting \( 60 - 29 \) gives \( 31 \), which is also correct.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Use the formula to directly calculate the difference:  
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 60 - (89 - 60).
   \]  

2. Simplify the expression inside the parentheses:  
   \[
   89 - 60 = 29.
   \]  

3. Substitute back into the formula:  
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 60 - 29 = 31.
   \]  

4. Double-check the calculations:  
   - Subtracting \( 89 - 60 \) gives \( 29 \), which is correct.  
   - Subtracting \( 60 - 29 \) gives \( 31 \), which is also correct.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and straightforward, as it breaks the problem into two clear steps: finding how many stayed behind and then comparing the two groups.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient for mental math, as it combines the steps into a single formula, but it may be less intuitive for some people.  
- Both methods are equally reliable and yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is easier to follow and less prone to errors for most people.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Method 1** is chosen for its clarity and simplicity.  
1. Calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind:  
   \[
   89 - 60 = 29.
   \]  
2. Calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed behind:  
   \[
   60 - 29 = 31.
   \]  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Verify the total: \( 60 + 29 = 89 \), which matches the initial total number of bird families.  
- Verify the difference: \( 60 - 29 = 31 \), which is consistent across both methods.  

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **31**.

Q: Paco had 36 cookies. He gave 14 cookies to his friend and ate 10 cookies. How many cookies did Paco have left?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Paco started with **36 cookies**.
- He gave **14 cookies** to his friend.
- He ate **10 cookies** himself.
- We assume no cookies were lost, dropped, or otherwise unaccounted for.
- The goal is to determine how many cookies Paco has left after these actions.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Sequential subtraction.  
- Subtract the cookies Paco gave to his friend from the total.  
- Then subtract the cookies he ate from the remaining amount.  

**Method 2:** Combine the cookies Paco gave away and ate into a single total, then subtract that total from the starting number.  
- Add the cookies given away and eaten together.  
- Subtract this combined total from the starting number.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Sequential subtraction**
1. Start with the total number of cookies: **36**.
2. Subtract the cookies Paco gave to his friend:  
   \( 36 - 14 = 22 \).  
   Now Paco has **22 cookies** left.
3. Subtract the cookies Paco ate:  
   \( 22 - 10 = 12 \).  
   Now Paco has **12 cookies** left.
4. Double-check:  
   - After giving away 14 cookies, \( 36 - 14 = 22 \).  
   - After eating 10 cookies, \( 22 - 10 = 12 \).  
   - The calculations are consistent.

#### **Method 2: Combine and subtract**
1. Combine the cookies Paco gave away and ate:  
   \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).  
   Paco gave away and ate a total of **24 cookies**.
2. Subtract this total from the starting number:  
   \( 36 - 24 = 12 \).  
   Now Paco has **12 cookies** left.
3. Double-check:  
   - Total cookies given away and eaten: \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).  
   - Subtracting from the starting total: \( 36 - 24 = 12 \).  
   - The calculations are consistent.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Method 1** is straightforward and breaks the problem into smaller, sequential steps. This makes it easier to follow and reduces the chance of error.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the actions into a single subtraction step, but it requires an additional step of summing the cookies given away and eaten. This could introduce errors if the addition is done incorrectly.  
- Both methods are reliable, but **Method 1** is slightly clearer for someone unfamiliar with the problem.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- I will choose **Method 1** because it is clearer and easier to follow step by step.

1. Start with the total number of cookies: **36**.
2. Subtract the cookies Paco gave to his friend:  
   \( 36 - 14 = 22 \).  
   Paco has **22 cookies** left.
3. Subtract the cookies Paco ate:  
   \( 22 - 10 = 12 \).  
   Paco has **12 cookies** left.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Using **Method 2** to verify:  
  - Combine the cookies given away and eaten: \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).  
  - Subtract from the starting total: \( 36 - 24 = 12 \).  
  - The result matches.

- The calculations are consistent, and the result is reasonable given the problem.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **12**.

Q: Bobby had 20 pieces of candy. He ate 34 pieces of candy. Then he ate 18 more. How many pieces of candy did Bobby eat?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Bobby started with 20 pieces of candy.
- He ate 34 pieces of candy, followed by eating 18 more pieces.
- We are tasked with finding the total number of pieces of candy Bobby ate.
- Assumption: The problem does not involve any candy being added to Bobby's initial amount; the focus is solely on the total candy consumed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.

**Method 1:** Add the two quantities of candy Bobby ate directly: 34 + 18.  
This method focuses on summing the two amounts of candy consumed without considering the initial amount, as it is irrelevant to the total eaten.

**Method 2:** Start with Bobby's initial amount (20 pieces), subtract the first amount he ate (34 pieces), and then subtract the second amount he ate (18 pieces).  
This method involves tracking the candy eaten relative to Bobby's starting amount, even though the starting amount is insufficient to cover what he ate.

---

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Direct addition of candy eaten**
1. Bobby ate 34 pieces of candy first.
2. Bobby then ate 18 more pieces of candy.
3. Add the two amounts:  
   \[
   34 + 18 = 52
   \]
4. Double-check the addition:  
   - Break it into smaller steps: \(34 + 10 = 44\), then \(44 + 8 = 52\).  
   - The result checks out.

**Potential Pitfall:** This method assumes the initial amount of candy Bobby had (20 pieces) is irrelevant to the total eaten, which is correct for this problem.

---

#### **Method 2: Subtract from the initial amount**
1. Bobby started with 20 pieces of candy.
2. He ate 34 pieces of candy. Subtract 34 from 20:  
   \[
   20 - 34 = -14
   \]
   This results in a negative number, indicating Bobby ate more candy than he initially had.
3. Bobby then ate 18 more pieces of candy. Subtract 18 from \(-14\):  
   \[
   -14 - 18 = -32
   \]
4. The negative result (\(-32\)) represents the total candy eaten beyond Bobby's initial amount. Since the problem asks for the total candy eaten, we take the absolute value:  
   \[
   \text{Total candy eaten} = 34 + 18 = 52
   \]

**Potential Pitfall:** This method introduces unnecessary complexity by involving negative numbers, which are not needed to solve the problem. It is less efficient than Method 1.

---

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.

- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct, focusing only on the total candy eaten without introducing irrelevant calculations involving Bobby's initial amount. It avoids unnecessary complexity and is less prone to errors.
- **Method 2** is more convoluted, requiring subtraction that leads to negative numbers, which must then be interpreted correctly. While it ultimately arrives at the correct answer, it is less efficient and introduces more room for confusion.

**Conclusion:** Method 1 is the better approach due to its clarity, reliability, and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.

Using **Method 1**:
1. Bobby ate 34 pieces of candy first.
2. Bobby then ate 18 more pieces of candy.
3. Add the two amounts:  
   \[
   34 + 18 = 52
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.

- Re-add the two amounts: \(34 + 10 = 44\), then \(44 + 8 = 52\).  
- The result checks out.
- The total candy eaten (52 pieces) is reasonable given the problem's context.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **52**.

Q: Josh had some marbles in his collection. He lost 21 marbles. If he has 12 marbles now How many marbles did he have in his collection?
A_model:
### Step 1: Clearly state all given information and any assumptions.
- Josh had an unknown number of marbles in his collection initially.
- He lost 21 marbles.
- He now has 12 marbles remaining.
- We assume no marbles were added or removed in any other way except for the 21 marbles he lost.

### Step 2: Propose two different methods to solve the problem, briefly outlining the logic of each.
**Method 1:** Use addition. Since Josh has 12 marbles now and lost 21 marbles, the total number of marbles he originally had is the sum of the marbles he lost and the marbles he currently has: \( \text{Initial marbles} = 12 + 21 \).

**Method 2:** Use subtraction in reverse. Let \( x \) represent the number of marbles Josh originally had. If he lost 21 marbles and now has 12, then \( x - 21 = 12 \). Solve for \( x \) by adding 21 to both sides: \( x = 12 + 21 \).

### Step 3: For each method, work through all intermediate steps in detail, showing calculations, checks, and potential pitfalls.

#### **Method 1: Addition**
1. Start with the marbles Josh currently has: 12.
2. Add the number of marbles he lost: \( 12 + 21 \).
3. Perform the addition: \( 12 + 21 = 33 \).
4. Double-check: If Josh originally had 33 marbles and lost 21, he would have \( 33 - 21 = 12 \), which matches the given information.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method is straightforward, but one must ensure the addition is performed correctly and that the logic of adding the lost marbles to the current count is understood.

#### **Method 2: Subtraction in reverse**
1. Let \( x \) represent the initial number of marbles.
2. Write the equation: \( x - 21 = 12 \).
3. Solve for \( x \) by adding 21 to both sides: \( x = 12 + 21 \).
4. Perform the addition: \( 12 + 21 = 33 \).
5. Double-check: Substitute \( x = 33 \) back into the equation: \( 33 - 21 = 12 \), which matches the given information.

**Potential pitfalls:** This method requires setting up the equation correctly and solving it step by step. Misinterpreting the equation or skipping steps could lead to errors.

### Step 4: Evaluate and compare the two methods—discussing which is better based on clarity, reliability, or efficiency.
- **Clarity:** Both methods are clear, but Method 1 (addition) is more intuitive because it directly adds the marbles lost to the current count.
- **Reliability:** Both methods are reliable, as they involve simple arithmetic and can be double-checked easily.
- **Efficiency:** Method 1 is slightly more efficient because it skips the step of setting up and solving an equation.

### Step 5: Choose the better method and use it to solve the problem, showing all steps.
- **Chosen Method:** Method 1 (addition) is better due to its simplicity and directness.
- **Solution:**
  1. Start with the marbles Josh currently has: 12.
  2. Add the marbles he lost: \( 12 + 21 = 33 \).
  3. Double-check: If Josh originally had 33 marbles and lost 21, he would have \( 33 - 21 = 12 \), which matches the given information.

### Step 6: Double-check the solution for errors or unreasonable results.
- Recheck the addition: \( 12 + 21 = 33 \).
- Verify the logic: If Josh had 33 marbles and lost 21, he would have \( 33 - 21 = 12 \), which is consistent with the problem statement.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **33**.
